♻ ☆ RNAGenScape: Property-Guided, Optimized Generation of mRNA Sequences with Manifold Langevin Dynamics NeurIPS 2026
Danqi Liao, Chen Liu, Xingzhi Sun, Dié Tang, Haochen Wang, Scott Youlten, Srikar Krishna Gopinath, Haejeong Lee, Ethan C. Strayer, Antonio J. Giraldez, Smita Krishnaswamy
Generating property-optimized mRNA sequences is central to applications such as vaccine design and protein replacement therapy, but remains challenging due to limited data, complex sequence-function relationships, and the narrow space of biologically viable sequences. Generative methods that drift away from the data manifold can yield sequences that fail to fold, translate poorly, or are otherwise nonfunctional. We present RNAGenScape, a property-guided manifold Langevin dynamics framework for mRNA sequence generation that operates on a learned manifold of real data. By performing iterative local optimization constrained to this manifold, RNAGenScape preserves favorable computational viability proxies, accesses reliable guidance, and discourages excursions into regions poorly supported by the training data. The framework integrates three components: (1) an autoencoder jointly trained with a property predictor to learn a property-organized latent manifold, (2) a denoising autoencoder that projects updates back onto the manifold, and (3) a property-guided Langevin dynamics procedure that performs optimization along the manifold. Across three real-world mRNA datasets spanning two orders of magnitude in size, RNAGenScape increases median oracle-predicted property gain by up to 267% and success rate by up to 31%, while maintaining good biological viability proxy measures and achieving competitive inference efficiency compared to existing generative approaches.
comment: NeurIPS 2026
♻ ☆ The Montparnasse Algorithm for RNA Design
Tristan Cazenave
Inverse RNA Folding consists of finding a nucleotide sequence that folds into a predefined secondary structure. It is useful for synthetic biology, medicine, and nanotechnology. We propose Montparnasse, a Monte Carlo search framework based on Generalized Nested Rollout Policy Adaptation, augmented with a structural prior derived from the energy model, slow and long adaptation at level 1, a lexicographic multicriteria evaluation, a local repair of the pairs the predicted folding gets wrong, and a fleet of parallel workers coupled through a shared beam whose diversity is maintained on the faults the candidates make rather than on the candidates themselves. On the Eterna100 V1 benchmark, with both solvers folding through the same ViennaRNA 1.8.5 library under the Turner 1999 model, with 100 parallel workers and a budget of 30 minutes per puzzle, Montparnasse solves a median of 96 of the 100 puzzles against a median of 90 for DesiRNA, the previous state of the art, each figure taken over five passes. It is faster on 88 of the 90 puzzles that both solve, with a median solving time of 0.9 s against 5.6 s, and it solves 55 puzzles within the first second. The one puzzle no pass reaches is solved under the same model with a longer budget, so every Eterna100 V1 target is solved by Montparnasse.
QM 7
☆ The long-term divergence exponent and the temporal structure of step-to-step perturbations: a simulation study in two passive walking models
Philippe Terrier
Purpose. Divergence exponents are used to assess the local dynamic stability of gait. Human experiments have related the long-term exponent ($λ_L$) to the complexity of gait fluctuations. Model studies used perturbations without controlled temporal structure. The question was whether $λ_L$ responds to the temporal structure of perturbations in walkers without neuromuscular control. Methods. Two passive walkers, M1 (pilot) and M2 (exploratory), received step-to-step perturbations of five amplitudes ($σ$) and nine temporal structures (ARFIMA parameter $d$), and human stride series of free and metronome walking. Results. At constant amplitude, $λ_L$ increased with $d$ in both models. It was higher with free series than with metronome series or with surrogates. It did not follow $σ$ in M1 and followed $σ$ in M2. Its double dissociation from the short-term exponent was supported in M1 only. In a descriptive analysis, the condition means of $λ_L$ correlated with the scaling exponent of the step durations ($r = 0.980$ in M1 and 0.906 in M2). Conclusion. In two passive walkers with fixed mechanics, $λ_L$ followed the temporal structure of the perturbations; its specificity depended on the model. This response supports the name attractor complexity index (ACI), proposed for $λ_L$ from human experiments.
comment: 49 pages, 6 figures, 2 tables. Includes appendices (2 tables) and supplementary material (1 figure, 8 tables). Code, results and scripts: https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.24365284
☆ When Foundation Models Disagree: Representational Heterogeneity for Calibrated Uncertainty in Whole-Slide Image Classification
Yekai Shen, Yuqian Wang, Zishun Liao, Rongsheng Wang, Wenting Chen, S. Kevin Zhou, Qingsong Yao
Pathology foundation models supply strong representations for whole-slide image classification, yet the confidence attached to their predictions remains hard to trust. Prevailing uncertainty estimators act within a single feature space and thereby overlook the uncertainty that the choice of foundation-model representation itself induces. This work introduces CALIFUSE, a calibration-oriented framework that fuses several pathology foundation models for the specific purpose of uncertainty estimation. Departing from accuracy-driven fusion, CALIFUSE reads representational heterogeneity across encoders as an explicit confidence cue. Slide-level decision consensus is coupled with chunk-level evidence agreement quantified through Jensen-Shannon divergence, so that both prediction stability and fine-grained evidence consistency inform the estimate. Foundation-model encoders stay frozen. A task-specific nnMIL classifier is still trained for each FM; CALIFUSE then estimates confidence at inference without training an extra fusion module. Evaluated on seven WSI classification tasks under several foundation-model combinations, the framework sharpens calibration, misclassification detection, and selective prediction relative to both the strongest single-encoder baseline and naive ensemble averaging, all while preserving competitive balanced accuracy.
comment: accepted by IEEE BIBM 2026
☆ Ab-initio cryo-EM reconstruction from moments of denoised images
Yunpeng Shi, Amit Singer, Yuguan Wang, Andy Zhang
In single-particle cryo-electron microscopy (cryo-EM), high-resolution three-dimensional (3-D) refinement requires a reliable ab-initio model to converge to the correct structure. Both refinement and existing ab-initio methods estimate the orientations of the particle images together with the 3-D structure, which can be slow for large and noisy datasets. We present a method of moments approach that bypasses orientation estimation and recovers a 3-D ab-initio model from the first three moments of denoised images. On experimental datasets, our method produces ab-initio models rapidly and successfully initializes high-resolution refinement. This is the first successful application of method-of-moments-based ab-initio modeling to experimental datasets without assuming a uniform distribution of orientations.
♻ ☆ RNAGenScape: Property-Guided, Optimized Generation of mRNA Sequences with Manifold Langevin Dynamics NeurIPS 2026
Danqi Liao, Chen Liu, Xingzhi Sun, Dié Tang, Haochen Wang, Scott Youlten, Srikar Krishna Gopinath, Haejeong Lee, Ethan C. Strayer, Antonio J. Giraldez, Smita Krishnaswamy
Generating property-optimized mRNA sequences is central to applications such as vaccine design and protein replacement therapy, but remains challenging due to limited data, complex sequence-function relationships, and the narrow space of biologically viable sequences. By performing iterative local optimization constrained to this manifold, RNAGenScape preserves favorable computational viability proxies, accesses reliable guidance, and discourages excursions into regions poorly supported by the training data. ♻ ☆ OpenTME: An Open Dataset of AI-powered H&E Tumor Microenvironment Profiles from TCGA
Maaike Galama, Nina Kozar-Gillan, Christina Embacher, Todd Dembo, Cornelius Böhm, Evelyn Ramberger, Julika Ribbat-Idel, Rosemarie Krupar, Verena Aumiller, Miriam Hägele, Kai Standvoss, Gerrit Erdmann, Blanca Pablos, Ari Angelo, Simon Schallenberg, Andrew Norgan, Viktor Matyas, Klaus-Robert Müller, Maximilian Alber, Lukas Ruff, Frederick Klauschen
The tumor microenvironment (TME) plays a central role in cancer progression, treatment response, and patient outcomes, yet large-scale, consistent, and quantitative TME characterization from routine hematoxylin and eosin (H&E)-stained histopathology remains scarce. We introduce OpenTME, an open-access dataset of pre-computed TME profiles derived from 5,268 H&E-stained whole-slide images across ten cancer types (bladder, breast, colorectal, head and neck, liver, lung, ovarian, pancreatic, prostate, and stomach cancer) from The Cancer Genome Atlas (TCGA). All outputs were generated using HETA (H&E Tissue Analysis), an AI-powered application built on the Atlas family of pathology foundation models, which performs tissue quality control, tissue segmentation, cell detection and classification, tumor zone and tertiary lymphoid structure (TLS) detection, and spatial neighborhood analysis, yielding over 20,000 quantitative readouts per slide at cell-level resolution, including curated features such as tumor-infiltrating lymphocytes, tumor mitotic index, or mature TLS counts. OpenTME is available for non-commercial academic research on Hugging Face. We will continue to expand OpenTME over time and anticipate it will serve as a resource for biomarker discovery, spatial biology research, and the development of computational methods for TME analysis.
♻ ☆ A General Harness for Protein Foundation Model Fitness Prediction
Yang Tan, Qijia Tian, Gangyu Sun, Bozitao Zhong, Mingchen Li, Yuanxi Yu, Nanqing Dong, Liang Hong
Accurate fitness prediction is central to protein engineering and understanding sequence-function relationships. With advances in deep learning, protein foundation models (PFMs) have become widely used for this task. Recent analyses, however, show that these models share preferences reflecting their training corpora, while unreliable inputs can further distort fitness predictions. Family-specific evolutionary evidence and structural context can help address these limitations by providing complementary constraints on model scores, motivating VenusREM-Harness (VRH), a general, model-agnostic, training-free Retrieval-Enhanced Mutation harness. It fuses frozen model scores with multiple sequence alignment (MSA) evidence according to model uncertainty, then applies gated background correction and score shrinkage based on structural confidence and solvent exposure. Across 1,211 assays and 3.1 million measured variants from ProteinGym, VenusMutHub, and the newly curated viral benchmark VenusViroHub, all 71 configurations improve Spearman correlation on all 3 benchmarks by 0.073 on average, with broad gains across 5 metrics. Extended analyses relate retrieval gains to model-MSA preference differences, assess domain-level gains and immune-escape cases, and quantify computational speedups. Built with VRH, VenusREM2 is the first to rank highest in all function, taxon, MSA-depth, and mutation-depth categories, with a ProteinGym Average Spearman of 0.556, 0.038 above the prior best.
♻ ☆ Physiological Trajectories and Observation Patterns in ICU Mortality Prediction: Transportability Under Changing Measurement Conditions
Benjamin T. Pike
Objective: To examine how changes in clinical measurement practices affect the transportability of predictive information from physiological trajectories and observation patterns in longitudinal ICU records. Methods: In-hospital mortality was predicted using measurements from the first 48 hours of ICU admission in MIMIC-III, MIMIC-IV, and eICU. Nested representations captured the latest physiological state (R1), trajectory summaries (R2), and observation timing and frequency (R3). Models trained on 7,762 MetaVision patients were evaluated on 21,682 MIMIC-IV and 43,878 eICU patients from 204 hospitals. Restriction experiments tested measurement timing at matched counts and reduced availability. Results: Physiological trajectories improved prediction beyond latest values across settings, including transfer to eICU (R2-R1 log-loss difference -0.01446, 95% CI -0.01701 to -0.01202). Observation patterns improved prediction following transfer to MIMIC-IV (R3-R2 -0.00564) and in locally developed eICU models (-0.00402, 95% CI -0.00517 to -0.00283), but not clearly following transfer from MetaVision to eICU (+0.00106, 95% CI -0.00087 to +0.00355). At matched observation counts, retaining measurements spread throughout the 48-hour window reduced log loss relative to random retention, more for R3 than R2 (0.04324 versus 0.00721). R3 was also more sensitive to the loss of recent measurements. Retraining under reduced availability restored its advantage over R2. Conclusions: Physiological trajectories transferred consistently, while observation patterns offered less reliable benefits and greater sensitivity to measurement timing and availability. Their transferability may depend on diversity of measurement practices during training. Clinical prediction models should be evaluated across measurement conditions as well as patient populations.

☆ Biases and Limits of AlphaFold Predictions for Non-Globular Proteins
Maria Cristina Aspromonte, Alexander M. Monzon, Miguel A. Andrade-Navarro, Oriol Bárcenas, Viktor Bartosik, Pau Bernadó, Juan Cortés, Zsuzsanna Dosztanyi, Gabor Erdos, Isabella C. Felli, Helena Gradišar, San Hadži, Tamás Hegedűs, Khalil Joron, Pavel Kaderavek, Eitan Lerner, Dirk Linke, Pablo Mier, Ana Pantelić, Roberta Pierattelli, Salvador Ventura, Marija Vidović, Metaxia Vlassi, Lukas Zidek, Silvio C. E. Tosatto
AlphaFold has transformed protein structure prediction, making structural models accessible across the life sciences. We explore experimentally validated cases of non-globular proteins to uncover where predictions diverge from biologically relevant structural states and how molecular context shapes these discrepancies, guiding a more informed interpretation of AlphaFold models.
☆ Micrograph B-factor estimation via Wilson statistics
Ayelet Heimowitz, Amit Singer
The single-particle cryo-electron microscopy imaging process is known to cause exponential decay in the amplitude and power spectra of proteins. This decay is modeled by the B factor and is currently corrected as a post-processing stage of the 3D reconstruction. As a consequence, all experimental images in the dataset are assumed to share the same B factor. In this paper, we suggest a novel approach wherein the B factor is determined and corrected as a preprocessing step to the reconstruction. We show such an approach can improve reconstruction resolution.
☆ ShEPhERD-score: Fast, adaptable three-dimensional alignment of molecular features
Nate Somers, Kento A. Abeywardane, Connor W. Coley, Masha Elkin
Three-dimensional (3D) molecular similarity scoring and alignment are widely employed in ligand-based virtual screening and molecular design, and are increasingly used for training and evaluating machine learning models. Existing open-source methods utilize various molecular representations spanning shape, electrostatics, pharmacophores, and hybrid representations, but their implementations as independent software packages with distinct application programming interfaces (APIs), optimizers, and limited GPU support make them difficult to compare, combine, or extend. Here, we develop ShEPhERD-score into a unified, modular platform for differentiable 3D molecular similarity scoring and alignment by combining diverse molecular representations with high-performance Numba CPU and Triton GPU kernels to support both large-scale library screening and pairwise similarity workflows. In matched comparisons with open-source 3D similarity tools on the DUDE-Z virtual screening benchmark, ShEPhERD-score performed comparably or better in eight of nine cases. Across the scoring modes in ShEPhERD-score, throughput ranges from 17,800 to 1.03 million alignments/second for ten-million-conformer libraries using a single NVIDIA L40S GPU. Moreover, we found that the most effective scoring mode varied by protein target, indicating that target-specific calibration against validation data may be valuable before large-scale screening and underscoring the need for a unified platform that provides diverse molecular representations. Together, these capabilities establish ShEPhERD-score as a single, extensible toolkit for rapid virtual screening and machine learning workflows.
♻ ☆ ProteinGeoBench: Frontier Structural-Biology Agents Are Fluent at Lookup but Lost in Geometry
Matt Turk, Brandon L. Neel, Diana M. Lopez, Zachary D. Smith, Grace N. Johnecheck, James B. Golden
ProteinGeoBench is a set of 131 tasks that give an agent protein structure files and ask it to look up recorded facts, check sequences, calculate geometry from atom coordinates or reason about structures. We ran four frontier models on every task several times (4,249 runs) and 26 models on a subset. A single overall score hides two different skills. Three of the four frontier models are best at looking up facts and worst at calculating from coordinates: Claude Opus 5 scores 0.907 on lookup and 0.716 on calculation. Gemini 3.1 Pro is the exception. Geometry is overrepresented among the hardest tasks. Half of the twelve tasks that all four models fail are calculations, although calculations are a fifth of the suite, and eight of the twelve were solved by none of the 26 models. The three leading models are within 0.031 of one another, too close to order, and with one run per task a different model ranks first in 24% of bootstrap resamples. Reports should therefore give scores by task type and the number of runs per task. They should also state the agent harness, the software that runs the model, and its network access, because both change scores. On a 15-task subset, blocking eight structural databases lowered scores, and removing network access lowered them more. Replacing our harness with each provider's own command-line agent moved scores by up to 0.09 in either direction. Across the 26 models, those that scored higher on the other tasks also scored higher on calculation.

☆ Prognosis Without Prediction: The Intrinsic Geometry of Survival
Atahan Karagoz
Modeling systemic failure in high-dimensional environments is fundamentally limited by supervised algorithms that attempt to predict sparse, noisy or censored endpoints. To break this reliance on explicit outcome regression, we engineered the Self-Organizing Survival Manifolds (SOSM) framework, a deep learning architecture that decouples unsupervised representation learning from survival labels and treats time-to-event progression as an emergent geometric property. The system projects heterogeneous multi-omics arrays into a unified latent space governed by a pseudotime-weighted, curvature-minimizing graph Laplacian, organizing patient trajectories along low-dissipation geodesic flows. To validate the framework, we applied this architecture to a 184-patient clear cell renal cell carcinoma multi-omics cohort. Across a deterministic 5-fold cross-validation pipeline, SOSM achieved a mean Concordance Index of $0.768 \pm 0.068$, while out-of-fold risk stratification yielded significant survival divergence (log-rank $p < 0.001$) accompanied by a 14.7% increase in local manifold dissipation among high-risk patients. Furthermore, interrogating the network's encoder weights confirmed that the model autonomously recovered canonical metabolic and microenvironmental drivers of disease progression without supervised feature selection. By demonstrating that systemic failure can be mapped as a geometric phase transition, SOSM establishes a generalizable computational engine for prognostic modeling across biological, industrial and financial complex systems.
comment: Code available at: https://github.com/Atahanka/SOSM
☆ Horizontal Flow Matching for Single-Cell Spatiotemporal Dynamics
Zihan Wang, Jianzhe Li, Zhenyi Zhang, Peijie Zhou
Learning the spatiotemporal dynamics of cells from spatial transcriptomics requires connecting snapshots of different cells acquired in different coordinate frames. A rotation or translation of a section can appear as cell movement and distort the inferred correspondence. We introduce horizontal flow matching, which learns changes in tissue organization, molecular state, and cell abundance up to shared rigid motion. The dynamics follow a continuity--reaction equation whose action assigns no cost to shared rigid motion, and the resulting horizontal projector removes this component from spatial prediction errors during learning and from predicted velocities during generation. An equivalent endpoint problem jointly estimates correspondence and relative pose in Wasserstein--Fisher--Rao geometry and yields analytic paths whose velocity and growth serve as regression targets. Regressing these targets recovers the horizontal dynamics under ideal population conditions. Our model, HORIZON, uses an equivariant transformer to predict velocity and growth from the current tissue, so that a tissue can be generated continuously from a single initial snapshot. On a simulated tissue observed in perturbed coordinate frames, alignment improves reconstruction and projection reduces rigid drift. On four spatial transcriptomics time courses, HORIZON reconstructs tissue geometry, gene expression, and cell abundance more accurately than existing spatial and unbalanced trajectory methods, and interpolates held-out developmental stages.

☆ Elysium: Simulation-Free Unbalanced Schrödinger Bridges
Zihan Wang, Jianzhe Li, Zhenyi Zhang, Peijie Zhou
Reconstructing cellular dynamics from snapshots is challenging because the destructive nature of single-cell measurements makes tracking the same cells over time impossible. Cellular dynamics are inherently stochastic and involve changes in population size through proliferation and apoptosis. Growth-based methods represent population change as a rate on the density, which does not assign exits and entries to individual cells. Unbalanced Schrödinger bridges model individual events, but neural solvers for killed diffusion bridges simulate trajectories during training. We introduce Elysium, a simulation-free solver that jointly learns state transition and mass change. Using a killed Brownian reference and its time reversal, Elysium constructs direct regression objectives from an optimal endpoint coupling and conditional bridge distributions, without simulating trajectories during training. We show that the minimizers of the expected training objectives recover the optimal unbalanced Schrödinger bridge. Empirically, Elysium reconstructs state transitions, population size, and event distributions on simulated benchmarks with known killed-diffusion ground truth, outperforming existing deterministic and bridge-based solvers. On single-cell time courses, it interpolates cell state distributions at unobserved time points, tracks population expansion and contraction, and captures biphasic drug-response dynamics.

☆ MitoWorld: A Stochastic World Model of the Single-Cell Cycle under Genetic Perturbation
Ting Long, Xiaojing Sun, Cheng Han, Zhicheng Ji, Yingnian Wu, Jiebo Luo, Heng Fan, Dongfang Liu
A cell observed after a gene knockdown has an uncertain future, both in how its appearance will evolve and when it will divide. Existing approaches predict endpoints or population-level changes without jointly modeling individual trajectories and division timing. In this work, we present MitoWorld, a stochastic world model that generates these future appearances as continuous evolution in a Gaussian reference latent, terminated by a division hazard and converted into images by a conditional diffusion renderer. The ordinary transition provably preserves its Gaussian reference, tying its noise to its dynamics; paired observations identify its discrete transition where stationary marginals cannot. The hazard turns division timing into a survival likelihood that can be tested against real cycle lengths. On 325 held-out MitoCheck tracks spanning 17 knockdowns and a control, MitoWorld lowers FID by 34.6\% and the video metric JEDi by 45.6\% relative to the strongest baselines. Its latent-space prediction intervals achieve near-nominal 90\% coverage, are 35\% narrower than those of a model with freely fitted noise. The hazard also yields cell-cycle length distributions with KS distance close to the model-based finite-sample reference. Testable single-cell stochastic dynamics thus offer a generative tool for time-lapse perturbation screens.
☆ Cardiac MRI-informed electrocardiography enables biologically grounded cardiomyopathy detection
Zhengyao Ding, Kewen Wang, Yujian Hu, Ziyu Li, Yiheng Mao, Yue Wang, Haitao Li, Dengnan Zhang, Xuanhong Liu, Yue Wang, Jing Wang, Yue Chen, Longbo Wang, Mengjia Chen, Chengchen Zhao, Xuesen Chu, Yubin Zhang, Mor Peleg, Judith Somekh, Mingxuan Yuan, Hui Yan, Yue Gu, Fan Yi, Zhilong Jia, Zhentao Tang, Xi Hu, Fei Wu, Hongkun Zhang, Ting Chen, Zhengxing Huang
Precise myocardial phenotyping at population scale is limited by the cost and accessibility of cardiac magnetic resonance (CMR), whereas electrocardiography (ECG) is widely available but reflects electrical rather than structural and functional information. Here we develop MyoECG, a cross-modal representation learning framework that distils myocardial structural and functional knowledge from CMR into standard 12-lead ECGs, enabling imaging-informed cardiomyopathy detection and subtype classification from ECGs alone. MyoECG was pretrained on 45,241 paired ECG-CMR examinations from UK Biobank and a tertiary cardiovascular centre, then developed and validated across more than 1.8 million ECGs from seven independent datasets. Across internal and external datasets spanning prevalences of 0.2-54.7%, MyoECG outperformed five pretrained ECG foundation models, with AUROCs of 0.800-0.974 for cardiomyopathy detection. In a multi-reader study of six cardiologists, MyoECG assistance improved mean sensitivity by 0.109 (95% CI, 0.041-0.175; P = 0.009) and specificity by 0.056 (95% CI, 0.004-0.108; P = 0.040). In 41,128 UK Biobank participants, the MyoECG score showed genetic correlations with cardiomyopathy (rg = 0.409) and dilated cardiomyopathy (rg = 0.371), genome-wide associations at four loci, including regions mapped to NFIA and KCNQ1, and associations with 69 circulating proteins related to myocardial stress, cell adhesion, vascular biology and metabolism. The cardiomyopathy correlation attenuated to non-significance after adjustment for 27 measured CMR traits (rg = 0.131; P = 0.143), indicating that the score captures CMR-derived information rather than dataset-specific shortcuts. MyoECG offers a scalable route to CMR-informed cardiomyopathy assessment and a strategy for transferring knowledge from resource-intensive imaging to widely deployable physiological signals.

☆ STORM: A Lightweight Structure-Free Model for Binding Affinity Prediction
Ayush Pandit, Rohan V. Koodli, Wei Deng, Roxana Daneshjou, Ron O. Dror
Accurately predicting a small molecule's binding affinity to a protein, a critical task in drug discovery, remains challenging. Physics-based approaches like free-energy perturbation (FEP) have very high computational costs and require expert setup. Recent machine learning--based cofolding models have reported accuracy approaching that of FEP---and at least one of them, Boltz-2, has already been widely adopted for affinity prediction---but they remain expensive for large computational screens and even more expensive for retraining on newly collected experimental data. To address these challenges, we introduce STORM: Similarity Transfer from Observed Reference Molecules. STORM is a fast, simple machine learning model that ensembles many small model fusions of diverse protein and molecule representations for binding affinity prediction. Trained on the same data, STORM matches or exceeds the accuracy of Boltz-2 on multiple evaluation datasets, while delivering a speed improvement of over five orders of magnitude for both inference and training. Our results also suggest opportunities to substantially improve affinity prediction accuracy by integrating historical experimental affinity data with structural and biophysical information.

♻ ☆ A multiscale discrete-to-continuum framework for structured population models
Eleonora Agostinelli, Keith L. Chambers, Helen M. Byrne, Mohit P. Dalwadi
Mathematical models of biological populations commonly use discrete structure classes to capture trait variation among individuals (e.g. age, size, phenotype, intracellular state). Upscaling these discrete models into continuum descriptions can improve analytical tractability and scalability of numerical solutions. Common upscaling approaches based solely on Taylor expansions may, however, introduce ambiguities in truncation order, uniform validity and boundary conditions. To address this, here we introduce a discrete multiscale framework to systematically derive continuum approximations of structured population models. Using the method of multiple scales and matched asymptotic expansions applied to discrete systems, we identify regions of structure space for which a continuum representation is appropriate and derive the corresponding partial differential equations. The leading-order dynamics are given by an advection equation in the bulk domain and advection-diffusion processes in small inner layers about wavefronts. We further derive discrete boundary layer descriptions for regions where the discrete model cannot be approximated by a continuum description. Finally, we demonstrate the method on a simple lipid-structured model for early atherosclerosis and verify consistency between the discrete and continuum descriptions. The multiscale framework we present can be applied to other heterogeneous systems with discrete structure in order to obtain appropriate upscaled dynamics with asymptotically consistent boundary conditions.
♻ ☆ Deconstructing Intraocular Pressure: A Non-Invasive Multi-Stage Probabilistic Inverse Framework
Md Rezwan Jaher, Abul Mukid Mohammad Mukaddes, A. B. M. Abdul Malek
We propose the mechanistically hierarchical inverse problem (MHIP) paradigm for biological transport systems, where a macroscopic observation emerges from the multiplicative entanglement of intrinsic micro-scale physics and latent macro-scale geometry. Without in vivo ground truth, solving MHIPs from clinical data is severely underdetermined. Standard statistical sampling degenerates along non-identifiable manifolds, while monolithic machine learning baselines collapse to an uninterpretable conditional mean. We propose a two-stage physics-guided decomposition that turns a globally non-identifiable inverse mapping into conditionally identifiable sub-problems. The first stage is a constrained inverse surrogate that isolates the intrinsic parameter under a fixed reference geometry. The second stage performs empirical structural calibration against population-level clinical data. We also introduce Physics-Calibrated Data Scaling (PCDS), a multi-fidelity strategy that builds large training sets from few high-fidelity simulations without patient-specific 3D modeling. In a glaucoma case study, the framework deconstructs a single intraocular pressure (IOP) measurement to estimate trabecular permeability ($\hat{K}_{TM}$) and conventional outflow facility ($\hat{C}_{trab}$). In retrospective validation against published in vivo data, the non-invasive $\hat{C}_{trab}$ estimates showed low bias against measured outflow facility. In an ablation over 27 clinical cohorts, naive monolithic baselines reach 37.0% diagnostic accuracy. Adding the isolated $\hat{K}_{TM}$ estimate raises this to 85.2%. The full two-stage framework reaches 88.9%. The framework also returns a probabilistic profile of the latent hydrodynamic state, offering a general computational blueprint for MHIPs.

☆ Discovery of new antiviral agents through artificial intelligence: in vitro and in vivo results
Roza Izmailyan, Mher Matevosyan, Hamlet Khachatryan, Anastasiya Shavina, Smbat Gevorgyan, Artur Ghazaryan, Irina Tirosyan, Yeva Gabrielyan, Marusya Ayvazyan, Boris Martirosyan, Vardan Harutyunyan, Hovakim Zakaryan
In this research, we employed a deep reinforcement learning (RL)-based molecule design platform to generate a diverse set of compounds targeting the neuraminidase (NA) of influenza A and B viruses. A total of 60291 compounds were generated, of which 86.5% displayed superior physicochemical properties compared to oseltamivir. After narrowing down the selection through computational filters, nine compounds with non-sialic acid-like structures were selected for in vitro experiments. We identified two compounds, DS-22-inf-009 and DS-22-inf-021 that effectively inhibited the NAs of both influenza A and B viruses (IAV and IBV), including H275Y mutant strains at low micromolar concentrations. Molecular dynamics simulations revealed a similar pattern of interaction with amino acid residues as oseltamivir. In cell-based assays, DS-22-inf-009 and DS-22-inf-021 inhibited IAV and IBV in a dose-dependent manner with EC50 values ranging from 0.29 uM to 2.31 uM. Furthermore, animal experiments showed that both DS-22-inf-009 and DS-22-inf-021 exerted antiviral activity in mice, conferring 65% and 85% protection from IAV (H1N1 pdm09), and 65% and 100% protection from IBV (Yamagata lineage), respectively. Thus, these findings demonstrate the potential of RL to generate compounds with promising antiviral properties.

☆ Structured AI ideation yields physical strategies for epithelial barrier repair
Geonho Nah, Johyun Park, Kyung-Ah Cho, Suyoung Hwang, Yoon Hee Lim, Eun-kyung Min, Seunghyun Moon, Donghyun Koh, Seungwon Kim, Jiho Park, Hojin Yoo, Myungsun Nam, Haneul Choi, Kyungsu Kim, In-Sun Hong, YunJae Jung, Minhyeong Lee
AI systems increasingly support literature analysis, candidate evaluation and hypothesis refinement, but enabling researchers to explore research directions beyond established disciplinary approaches remains challenging. We developed Abelio, an AI system designed to generate novel research directions by expanding investigators' avenues of inquiry. Abelio reformulates research objectives, uses these alternative formulations to identify relevant elements outside conventional disciplinary boundaries, and refines the resulting hypotheses. Each stage integrates divergent exploration with convergent selection through a dedicated subsystem. In quantitative evaluations, proposals generated by the Ideation Core (Abelio's hypothesis-generation core) outperformed those of four frontier large language models in scientific soundness and hypothesis diversity, and three of four in distance from existing literature. Subsystem ablation showed that each subsystem contributes to the property it targets, with the contribution of Element Selection measurable before Refinement. To investigate intestinal epithelial barrier restoration, Abelio proposed physical interventions involving membrane-tethered poly(ethylene glycol) (PEG) and cholesterol-anchored complementary DNA. Both proposals were archived before experimentation and subsequently tested independently in damaged Caco-2 monolayers. PEG preserved barrier function in a membrane-surface-dependent manner, whereas complementary DNA enabled sequence-specific restoration that was reversible on demand. These findings demonstrate how structured research ideation can identify experimentally testable approaches beyond established disciplinary conventions.
☆ Fiberpose: state-of-the-art fiber morphometry and instance segmentation with a distance-transform neural network
Viktor A. Balashov, Daniil S. Antonenko
Analysis of fibrous structures in SEM images is a routine task in biomedical engineering and materials science. However, most available methods for fiber quantification still rely on classical image binarization followed by simple post-processing. These approaches often produce unreliable results in images with weak boundaries, heterogeneous contrast, and densely packed fibers. Here, we introduce Fiberpose, a framework that reformulates fiber analysis as direct regression of a smooth distance-transform (DT) field, together with a novel set of geometry-based algorithms specifically adapted to this representation. The predicted field directly encodes fiber geometry, while the accompanying algorithms enable instance reconstruction and measurement of diameter and orientation within a unified workflow. The framework is implemented as a standalone Python desktop application and a web tool https://fiberpose.org. On an independent dataset of fibrous SEM images, Fiberpose achieved state-of-the-art instance-segmentation accuracy in comparison with SAM2 and Cellpose-SAM, and more accurate diameter measurements than DiameterJ, Fibar and GIFT. The method remains effective in dense and weakly contrasted regions and shows qualitative generalization across microscopy modalities not represented in the training data. Fiberpose therefore provides an interpretable geometric representation and an automated pipeline for reproducible quantitative characterization of fibrous materials.
☆ Optimising Recruitment and Sample Allocation in Multi-Site Rare Disease Studies
Farid Zare, Catherine Clancy, Tal Dattner, Martina Messina, Julian Teinert, Jack McGoldrick, Pavel Ješina, Sabine Weller Grønborg, Ute Spiekerkoetter, James J O'Byrne, Trine Tangeraas, Maria Luz Couce, José A. Cocho, Javier de las Heras, Niklas Darin, Dorothea Möslinger, Peter Witters, Ina Knerr, Giancarlo La Marca, Ans T. van der Ploeg, Hidde H. Huidekoper, Terry G. J. Derks, Francjan J. van Spronsen, Sandra D. K Kingma, Anibh M. Das, Carlo Dionisi-Vici, Clara D. van Karnebeek, Julia B. Hennermann, Siobhán O'Sullivan, Sabine A. Fuchs, Luciana Hannibal, M. Rebecca Heiner-Fokkema, Marek J. Noga, Irene M. L. W. Körver-Keularts, Katja Benedikte Prestø Elgstøen, George J. G. Ruijter, J J M Jans, Amy Harms, Thomas Hankemeier, Maurizio Scarpa, Ines Thiele, Shamima Rahman, Stefan Kölker, Ronan M. T. Fleming
Clinical research in rare diseases is challenged by the limited availability and broad geographical distribution of eligible patients. This necessitates multi-centre collaboration and strategic selection of analytical laboratories based on capacity, cost, and capability. We present a mathematical framework based on cardinality optimisation to support key operational decisions in study design, balancing clinical relevance, feasibility, budgetary constraints, and logistical factors. The framework was implemented in the Reconstruction and Computational Modelling for Inherited Metabolic Diseases (Recon4IMD) project, aiming to recruit 1,900 diagnosed patients across 24 healthcare centres and eight metabolomic laboratories. Notably, both the heuristic and greedy allocation baselines became infeasible under progressively tighter laboratory capacity constraints, whereas the optimised model maintained feasibility and cost control. These findings highlight the value of integrated optimisation strategies for improving recruitment, platform selection, and sample allocation in complex, resource-limited research settings.
♻ ☆ Analysis choices can produce apparent rich-club differences: an Alzheimer's disease EEG case study
Kai-Wayne Liu, Ryan Tian En Pang, Pholpat Durongbhan
Background: The weighted rich-club coefficient tests whether a network's most strongly connected nodes are preferentially interconnected. Its interpretation depends on two often-implicit choices: how the randomised comparison networks are constructed, and whether the hubs are re-identified in each comparison network or held fixed at those of the observed network. When hubs are defined by connection strength, these choices change what the coefficient tests. New method: We examined how these choices affect inference using resting-state EEG from 20 people with Alzheimer's disease and 20 controls. Holding the data fixed, we compared four analysis pipelines, crossing two ways of building the comparison networks (global weight reshuffle or strength-conditioned null) with the two hub choices, and ran a calibration study using data constructed to contain no preferential hub coupling beyond nodal strength. Results: An apparent eyes-open group difference in rich-club organisation appeared only under the pipeline combining a global weight reshuffle with re-identified hubs. Under the empirical resampling ensemble, that pipeline reported a group difference in 43% of pseudo-studies, always higher in the Alzheimer's disease group, while the other three stayed near the expected 5%. Comparison with existing methods: Unlike global weight reshuffling, strength-conditioned nulls approximately preserve nodal strengths; fixing observed hubs also retains the identity of the core being tested. Conclusions: The same data can support or dissolve an apparent rich-club difference depending on these choices. No single choice is universally correct, but reporting which were made, and why they fit the question being asked, is essential to interpreting the result.

☆ Calpha: Toward Native Biomolecular Intelligence in a General-Purpose Language Model
Yong Liu, Zhanpeng Shi, Peicong Lin, Zhenyi Zhong, Yizhou Dang, Wendi Wu, Haoling Li, Shuangjia Zheng
Biomolecular foundation models predict the structures of proteins and their complexes accurately, and large language models (LLMs) reason in natural language, but the two remain separate: the abilities of the former are not part of a general-purpose LLM, and the reasoning of the latter does not enter a structure prediction. Calpha aims at one general model that folds proteins and complexes, understands protein sequence and structure, and lets its language reasoning influence the structures it predicts. This report covers the first step, in which an LLM outputs single-chain protein structures. Qwen3.5-9B reads a sequence enriched with ESM2 residue features. It can respond in text or emit a token that invokes a pretrained SimpleFold structure head, which receives information about the protein only through Qwen's residue states. Under a fixed folding request, the head achieves mean TM-scores of 0.808 on the FoldBench-334 development set, 0.635 on CASP16 domains and 0.900 on a CAMEO-style PDB set. We compare these predictions with a baseline in which ESM2-3B encodes the sequence and a fine-tuned SimpleFold-3B head predicts the coordinates. The two systems also differ in trainable weights, training duration and crop length. For chains of 100--299 residues, all three paired confidence intervals include zero. At 300--600 residues, the mean differences are -0.044, -0.160 and -0.025, respectively. In a separate test, four English request types triggered folding on every evaluated example. Protein training reduced accuracy on a 350-item MMLU-Pro subset from 77.4% to 70.3%. An LLM can therefore output single-chain structures from its own states, with lower long-chain scores in the current comparisons and reduced accuracy on the evaluated MMLU-Pro subset. Complex prediction, structure understanding and the effect of language reasoning on the predicted coordinates remain future work.
☆ multiCAsp: Spatially Informed Canonical Association Analysis of Multi-Omic Data
Ziqi Mu, Qiuyi Wu, Zhicheng Ji, Pixu Shi
Simultaneous profiling of multiple molecular layers at spatially resolved locations creates opportunities to identify shared cross-modal biological variation in its tissue context. We present multiCAsp (multi-omic Canonical Association informed by spatial graph), a probabilistic latent-factor model for integrating $M \geq 2$ spatially resolved omic modalities. It incorporates spatial dependence through a graph-Laplacian prior on shared latent factors and uses an expectation-maximization algorithm to estimate factor scores and modality-specific feature loadings. Because the prior is defined on a neighborhood graph rather than on Euclidean distances, it accommodates irregular tissue geometries and extends naturally to multiple tissue sections. Simulations show that multiCAsp consistently achieves higher mean latent subspace and loading recovery than alternative methods across varying sample sizes, feature dimensions, and noise levels, with the largest gains under high noise. Compared with an existing Gaussian process-based spatial factor model, it is also less sensitive to how the spatial coordinates are represented, produces spatially smoother factors, and has lower computational cost. Applied to metabolomic-proteomic data from macaque cortex and gene-protein data from human renal carcinoma, multiCAsp recovers spatially coherent multi-omic factors with interpretable biological signatures. In the macaque data, transferred factors retain broad spatial patterns across animals and support cross-modal prediction of protein abundances from metabolomic measurements.
☆ Weight-Space Programming Changes How an LLM Uses Python for Computational Biology
Makoto Sato
Weight-space programming allows a language model to edit another model's weights without gradient-based training. We used Claude Opus 5.5 to edit Qwen3.5-0.8B and investigated how the edits changed its use of Python on computational biology problems. We compared four initial edited checkpoints with the original model on 50 test problems across three test seeds, yielding 600 paired evaluations. Strictly correct answers increased from 44 to 77, and technically correct answers, which included correct final values rejected for formatting, increased from 64 to 100. Repeated Python calls decreased from 1,834 to 1,044. Among the 64 pairs in which only the edited response was technically correct, the original model had already computed the correct value in 36 but did not submit a technically correct final answer. The edited model corrected the computation in 13 of the 64 pairs. With six additional checkpoints on the same 50 problems, technically correct answers rose from 96 to 154 across 900 pairs. We explored three selected problems with ten test seeds for each of ten checkpoints. On the melting-temperature problem, correct Python outputs decreased from 60 to 22 while technically correct final answers increased from 0 to 18. Weight editing changed both the calculations Qwen ran and whether it submitted a correct answer.

☆ OCCAM: Improved Single-Cell Representation Learning and Perturbation Effect Prediction
Ihab Bendidi, Kian Kenyon-Dean, Alisandra K. Denton, Auguste Genovesio, Emmanuel Noutahi
Predicting the effects of genetic perturbations requires models that generalize beyond observed cell contexts and gene combinations. We introduce Occam, a multilayer perceptron (MLP) framework for representation learning and perturbation-effect prediction. Occam combines cell representations learned through denoising with gene interaction, text, and protein-sequence features. It achieves the highest aggregate representation scores for perturbation information and cell-type preservation with batch integration. In cross-context few-shot prediction, a perturbation and a cell context are each observed during training, but never together. Occam better captures the measured response patterns for these unobserved combinations than four retrained competitors or direct transfer of the same perturbation's measured effects from other training cell lines. Without fine-tuning, Occam best distinguishes perturbations among the evaluated models and simple baselines in cell lines held out from predictor training, across seen and unseen assays. Predicting expression levels accurately in these settings remains challenging. For double perturbations, which target two genes simultaneously, Occam composes learned single-gene responses to better capture the combined response and reduce expression error compared with competing models and summed single-gene effects. These gains extend to both strongly additive and weakly additive responses. Together, these results support simple MLPs for cell representation learning and perturbation-effect prediction across contexts and gene combinations.
♻ ☆ Predator-associated cues promote host riding, and coupling to mobile hosts improves survival in an epizoic limpet
Ryo Nakayama, Katsushi Kagaya, Keita Harada, Tomoyuki Nakano
A host with multiscale movements may be difficult for a small animal to intercept or predict. Boarding turns that moving target into a source of locomotion. The snail Tegula nigerrima previously showed statistical signatures consistent with near-critical locomotor dynamics over limited ranges. We examined how the facultative epizoic limpet Lottia tenuisculpta approaches and boards this host, and whether host mobility improves rider survival. Crab-associated waterborne cues increased riding (19/80 without cues versus 42/76 with cues) and visible host--limpet distance closure. Paired trajectories of eventual riders captured both animals' projected movement toward one another during a nominal ten-minute window ending at the movement-analysis reference frame. Normalised by each animal's shell size, the limpet's projected contribution exceeded the host's in 22/58 pairs. A complementary exploratory assay compared each limpet's cumulative path length on an assigned surface with its no-mucus baseline at a common observation time. Log-transformed ratios varied less among individuals in gastropod-mucus groups than in the no-mucus group. Across the observed frequency range, a matched-trial comparison estimated a 1.21-fold cue-present to cue-absent amplitude ratio (95% recording-date bootstrap interval: 0.97--1.59); the treatment difference in high-frequency spectral slope remained uncertain. Survival was higher on mobile than fixed hosts, with shells occupied in both treatments (posterior median hazard ratio, fixed versus mobile: 2.1). These findings connect the behavioural formation of host--rider coupling to a survival benefit from host mobility, illustrating how one animal gains access to the multiscale locomotion of another.

♻ ☆ What makes vertical federated learning work with thirteen samples? A controlled benchmark on coral thermal-stress multi-omics
Sam Victor
Vertical federated learning (VFL) lets laboratories that hold different omics layers of the same samples train a joint model without sharing raw data, but it is usually developed for datasets with thousands of samples. We benchmarked VFL on a Montipora capitata thermal-stress dataset with 13 samples and 90,579 features across transcriptomics, proteomics, metabolomics and microbiome data. The benchmark crosses two VFL backbones (a split-network model implemented in NVIDIA FLARE, and LASER-VFL) with three per-silo feature-selection strategies (gradient saliency, random, univariate t-test) and two allocations of a 1,300-feature budget, and adds a centralised elastic-net reference. All models were trained for the same number of steps and evaluated by repeated stratified 3-fold and leave-one-out cross-validation, with separate estimates of seed and data-split variability and end-to-end label-permutation tests. Plain VFL on all features classified heat stress well above chance (3-fold AUROC 0.869, 95% CI 0.838-0.895; permutation p = 0.020). Gradient-saliency selection with hand-set omics priors (0.904) could not be distinguished from plain VFL, from equal budgets or from random subsets of the same size (corrected p >= 0.56), and its selected features were no more consistent across seeds than random ones. LASER-VFL failed on all features at a learning rate of 1e-3 but matched the other methods at 1e-4. This version corrects versions 1-3 of this preprint, which reported that standard VFL performed at chance and that domain priors stabilised training; we show that those results arose because every model selected its checkpoint on a single-sample validation set, which evaluated the baseline after one optimiser step. With very few samples, evaluation choices outweighed every method choice we tested, and we give recommendations for evaluating federated models in this regime.

♻ ☆ Probing and steering biology across Boltz-1s trunk-diffusion boundary
Piotr Jedryszek, Tongmeng Xie, Adam Winnifrith, Alexander Hasson, Weronika Ślesak, George Wicks, Toby Winnifrith, Oliver M. Crook
AlphaFold3-class structure predictors pair a representational trunk, which processes sequence and context, with a diffusion module, which generates atomic coordinates. How biological information changes as it crosses this architectural boundary remains poorly understood. We analyze per-residue activations from the Pairformer trunk and diffusion module of Boltz-1 using linear probes, sparse autoencoders (SAEs), and causal interventions. From the trunk, both geometry (secondary structure, disorder) and sequence chemistry (amino-acid identity, signal peptides, disulfide-bond annotations) are linearly decodable. In the diffusion module, the two diverge. Secondary structure remains equally decodable, whereas sequence chemistry becomes much less linearly accessible. We then test whether decodable directions can steer the model by editing the trunk's single representation. Helix and coil directions change predicted structure dose-dependently, but a beta-strand direction that is highly predictive on the same proteins (held-out F1 =0.78) produces no measurable increase in strand content at any of the steered layers. Removing helix or coil directions does not remove that structure. Linear decodability therefore did not imply causal influence at the sites we tested. The same probes also score markedly lower against SwissProt annotations than against DSSP labels, because unannotated residues that the model gets right are charged as false positives; such scores are therefore lower bounds. Finally, supervised probes outscore single SAE features wherever a label already exists.
♻ ☆ Towards Efficient Auto-Interpretability of Boltz-1 Sparse Autoencoders
Piotr Jedryszek, Oliver M. Crook
Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) could turn AI models in Biology and other fields into engines of scientific discovery by explaining the superhuman capabilities of those models. We postulate that a latent is useful if we know three things: whether it is coherent, whether it can be described, and whether that description has predictive power. These questions are routinely conflated. We assemble them into a single pipeline and report some practical innovations. First, cross-seed dictionary stability prioritises which latents are worth spending resources to investigate. Second, an intruder-detection task asks whether a latent's activating examples share a recognizable pattern. Third, a separate pass proposes a candidate biological description which we convert into falsifiable predictions which can be tested in silico. Deployed on the Boltz-1 Pairformer trunk, stability prioritisation finds interpretable latents using about 4.4 times fewer latent evaluations each, and at 5.2 times lower measured LLM cost, while recovering over half of them. However, we notice that in our setup the LLM judge is heavily biased towards consistent residue identity and a simple filter that selects for features with high amino-acid identity precision outperforms the stability thresholding. This simpler method has the added benefit of requiring no extra SAE seeds but it remains uncertain if it reflects a genuinely useful heuristic or an limitation of the LLM judge.
♻ ☆ Quantum Cognition Machine Learning for Forecasting Chromosomal Instability
Giuseppe Di Caro, Vahagn Kirakosyan, Alexander G. Abanov, Jerome R. Busemeyer, Luca Candelori, Nadine Hartmann, Ernest T. Lam, Kharen Musaelian, Ryan Samson, Harold Steinacker, Dario Villani, Martin T. Wells, Richard J. Wenstrup, Mengjia Xu

☆ Reasoning in the loop: antibody lead optimization by probing mechanism and measurement
Samuel Stanton, Aaron Solomon, Emily Delaney, Melanie Montgomery, Crystal Humphries, Timothy Acker, Ryan Henrici, Charbel Eid, Eddie Abrams, Peyton Greenside
Lab-in-the-loop systems, in which a model proposes molecules, automated assays measure their properties and the results retrain the model, are a popular approach to antibody lead optimization. These systems optimize for a specification defined with metrics computed from instrument signals, so their success depends on how well those metrics serve as proxies for the properties a drug candidate needs. When a metric is a poor proxy, neither a better model nor more data will fix it, and identifying the problem usually requires expert review. We report two campaigns run on BigHat Biosciences' Milliner platform, one on an anti-albumin nanobody (VHH) and one on an anti-mesothelin single-chain variable fragment (scFv), in which a research team and a general-purpose AI agent, Claude, brought scientific reasoning into the loop. Over seven rounds and about 1,010 design variants we built libraries from antibody lineage, structure and biophysics, and found from the raw curves that on each scaffold one thermal metric did not measure what its name implied. The libraries were built as controlled comparisons, so the results also show what each mutation contributes to each property. We fit a physical model of the curves, used it to choose metrics suited to each scaffold, and changed the experimental protocol, varying protein concentration and heating rate, to test the model against pre-registered predictions. Both leads improve on their parents in affinity, thermal stability and aggregation while improving or maintaining yield and purity.

☆ Cross-species digital xenocortication model delineates molecular correlates of human brain connectome
Gennadi Glinsky
Cross-species digital xenocortication (CSDX) model based on pairing of a human brain structure with one or more mouse brain structures was constructed and implemented to dissect the molecular correlates of the human brain connectome. Design of the CSDX model is based on the assumption that a human brain region and its named mouse counterpart occupy corresponding positions in an evolutionary conserved mammalian brain connectome plan, so that a projection strength measured between two mouse structures is informative about the relation between the two human structures paired with them. In the CSDX model, gene expression for the node was measured in the human brain, while connection strength between two nodes was measured in the mouse brain. CSDX models of different parcellations were employed to explore the potential regulatory associations of brain connectome patterns and constituents of protein-protein interaction (PPI) cascades of hybrid assembly pathways of endogenous multiprotein complexes. Metrics of continuous engagement of the PPI cascade nucleated protein dimers of CORUM multiprotein complexes have been identified as statistical components of the multivariate transcriptome-connectome associations. It accounts for additional connectome associated variations beyond the five measured axes in the 82 nodes CSDX model. Comparisons of the seven arm models of transcriptome-connectome associations demonstrate that the mouse derived model generally fits the mouse brain connectome better, while the human derived model assigns a significantly larger fraction of its transcriptome-connectome association to unique rather than net shared contributions. This study nominates elite panels of top scoring candidate protein dimers and CORUM multiprotein complexes for follow up mechanistic studies of their potential roles in structural functional features governing mammalian brain connectome.

♻ ☆ Agent-guided epitope excision enables economical de novo binder design
Jun W. Kim, Artiom Myaskovsky, Oscar Donosa, Lawrence Chan, Jameson K. Rogers, Avinatan Hassidim, Yossi Matias, Annalisa Pawlosky
De novo protein design campaigns remain bottlenecked by the inference compute time required to generate and rank candidates, limiting wider functional exploration. ☆ Beyond AUC: Do Mammography Risk Models Predict When Cancer Occurs?
Svetlana Krasnova, Andrey Krylov, Dmitry Sorokin
Longitudinal deep learning models for mammography-based breast cancer risk prediction output either per-year cumulative risks or a discrete distribution over the year of diagnosis, from which cumulative risks F1,...,F5 are derived. These models are evaluated almost exclusively with year-wise AUCs and the concordance index. We show that these metrics are largely insensitive to when cancer occurs: they can be high even for a model that carries no information about the year of diagnosis. We re-evaluate two recent longitudinal models (OA-BreaCR, LMV-Net) on CSAW-CC with a 5-year horizon using timing metrics computed on cancer cases only. Despite C-indices up to 0.75, neither orders cancer cases by time to diagnosis significantly better than chance (c_time 0.51 and 0.55, both 95% CIs covering 0.5). Weighted absolute timing error favours a constant "year 3" prediction over both models, so we use c_time as the primary timing metric. For Mirai's additive-hazard head, also used by LMV-Net, we analyse how per-year increments passed through a ReLU can collapse under severe class imbalance. We also propose a breast-level, two-stage model that separates whether from when. It matches LMV-Net in discrimination (C-index 0.745 vs. 0.746) but orders cancer cases better (c_time 0.58 vs. 0.51, paired 95% CI of the difference 0.01-0.13), on par with Mirai's published weights (0.57).

☆ Scaling large language model-assisted line-of-therapy adjudication with human-in-the-loop evidence-calibrated selection
Olatunji Oni, Sanhita Sengupta, Marc De Benedetti, Nathanial Eddy
Large language models (LLMs) have enabled the ability to scale clinical adjudication tasks traditionally dependent on exhaustive manual review. Line-of-therapy (LoT) adjudication, a major bottleneck in oncology cohort construction, is a prime example. Line boundaries must be inferred from longitudinal treatment histories according to nuanced, study-specific definitions, at every regimen transition for every patient. LLMs can apply this adjudication at scale, but their reliability varies across patients. Treatment histories differ in complexity, and ambiguous or atypical patterns depend on expert clinical judgment. Reliable automation thus requires verified support for model assignments and the ability to identify cases that warrant manual review. Here, we present evidence-calibrated selection (ECS), a human-in-the-loop framework for scaling LLM-assisted LoT adjudication. ECS derives confidence from observed clinician adjudications, prioritizes patients with the least-supported assignments for follow-up review, and returns completed adjudications to the model as precedent. As the evidence base grows, ECS continuously calibrates confidence to estimate residual error, thus enabling more efficient use of clinician time. In simulations on 136 clinician adjudicated patients with multiple myeloma, ECS achieved a residual error of 5% after review of 44.6% of patients, compared to 62.2% when review was directed by LLM self-reported confidence and 81.9% under random sampling. These results support a scalable approach to study-specific LoT adjudication, using clinician evidence to inform automated predictions, minimize error, and reduce review burden.

☆ Automated Left Ventricle Strain Analysis from DENSE MRI
Mohammad Naqizadeh Jahromi, Ariel J. Hannum, Zhan-Qiu Liu, Daniel B. Ennis, Dazhong Wu, Luigi E. Perotti
Background: Quantifying myocardial strains from Displacement ENcoding with Stimulated Echoes (DENSE) MRI provides insight into left ventricular performance, but current data processing pipelines remain limited in automation, robustness, and full left ventricle coverage. Methods: We present a fully automated framework for voxel-wise displacement and strain analysis of the left ventricle from DENSE MRI. Our method integrates a multi-channel nnU-Net model trained on both magnitude and phase images for segmentation, an automated phase unwrapping approach, an adaptation of the regularized spatiotemporal least squares (RSTLS) formulation for displacement computation, and strain calculations based on radial basis function interpolation. The proposed framework enables the estimation of displacements and strains across the left ventricle from a full stack of short-axis images. Results: Validation using a computational phantom shows the framework's overall resilience to data noise. Following computational phantom evaluation, the proposed framework is applied to analyze DENSE MRI short-axis slices acquired in forty-seven (N=47) healthy volunteers and covering the full left ventricle. Circumferential, longitudinal, and radial strains are presented throughout the cardiac cycle and at peak systole, showcasing the performance of the proposed framework. Results show that circumferential and longitudinal strains are computed accurately. However, challenges persist when estimating radial strain, especially in the subendocardial layer. A group-wise strain comparison is also performed based on volunteers' sex and age showing a significant effect of sex on circumferential and longitudinal strains. Conclusions: Overall, the proposed framework provides an automated and modular pipeline to compute displacements and strains from DENSE MR images that can be scaled and generalized toward clinical applications.

☆ From the possible to the optimal: differentiable optimization of gene regulatory networks
Leah Friedman, Gašper Tkačik, Thomas Gregor, Paul François
Understanding the space of ``the actual and the possible'' regulatory architectures capable of realizing biological functions is one of the major challenges in biology. Optimization-based approaches can rationalize regulatory mechanisms from functional objectives and constraints, but their computational cost has limited them to finding individual solutions. Here, we introduce \texttt{jaxgrn}, a framework that combines differentiable programming with stochastic simulations of gene regulatory networks (GRNs) to optimize thousands of networks end-to-end on GPUs. Using morphogen-driven embryonic patterning as a case study, we optimize networks for domain formation and positional information, while varying constraints on input noise, boundary precision and scaling with system size. While recovering known archetypes for scaling, we find that varying the constraints organizes optimal solutions into qualitatively distinct regimes. When optimizing domain formation, we observe phenotypic convergence across a continuum of regulatory architectures, ranging from input-driven to feedback-driven networks. When optimizing positional information, solutions are initially phenotypically diverse despite moderate variation in regulatory architecture; imposing scaling drives both phenotypic and regulatory convergence of the optimized networks onto a few recurring regulatory architectures. Our systematic approach opens the door for variational systems biology in which changing functional constraints reshape the landscape of possible phenotypes and regulatory architectures.
☆ Analysis choices can produce apparent rich-club differences: an Alzheimer's disease EEG case study
Kai-Wayne Liu, Ryan Tian En Pang, Pholpat Durongbhan
The weighted rich-club coefficient tests whether a network's most strongly connected nodes are preferentially interconnected. Its interpretation depends on two often-implicit choices that are easy to overlook: how the randomised comparison networks are constructed, and whether the hubs are re-identified in each comparison network or held fixed at those of the observed network. When hubs are defined by connection strength, these choices change what the coefficient actually tests. We examined how these choices affect inference using resting-state EEG from 20 people with Alzheimer's disease and 20 controls. Holding the data fixed, we compared four analysis pipelines, crossing two ways of building the comparison networks with the two hub choices, and ran a calibration study in which the same pipelines were applied to data constructed to contain no preferential hub coupling. An apparent eyes-open group difference in rich-club organisation appeared under only one of the four pipelines, the one combining a global weight reshuffle with re-identified hubs, and was absent under the other three. More tellingly, that same pipeline reported a group difference in 40% of cases where none was present, almost always higher in the Alzheimer's disease group, while the other three stayed near the expected 5%. This shows that the same data can support or dissolve an apparent rich-club difference depending on choices that are rarely reported. No single choice is universally correct, but reporting which were made, and why they fit the question being asked, is essential to interpreting the result.

♻ ☆ Stochastic partial differential equation model for environmental DNA dynamics in river environments
Hidekazu Yoshioka
Environmental DNA (eDNA) has emerged as a novel tool for quantifying the seasonal abundance of aquatic species in water bodies; however, its mathematical modeling is still at a germinating stage because of its mechanistic uncertainties. We propose a first-step mathematical and computational framework for the eDNA dynamics of migratory fish based on a novel stochastic partial differential equation model with a delayed source input. The model governs spatiotemporal eDNA concentration in rivers where the source comes from a stochastic differential equation for the migration dynamics of the fish. The affine nature of the model facilitates its theoretical analysis, including the guarantee of well-posedness and the closed-form derivation of the Laplace functional despite the proportionality coefficient of the multiplicative noise term being non-Lipschitz. We also propose a discretization scheme for the model that theoretically generates nonnegative numerical solutions. We finally apply the proposed model to eDNA concentration data sampled from midstream reaches of a river system and perform sensitivity analysis.

Biomolecules 2
☆ Generation of protein conformations using a simple inverted transformer
Luigi Leonardo Palese
Molecular dynamics is an extremely powerful approach for studying protein behavior, but it requires enormous computational resources to obtain an adequate sampling of possible conformations. Recently, thanks to successes in predicting three-dimensional protein structures, there has been an explosion of research exploring the opportunities offered by deep learning to address the problem of protein conformational landscape. In this work, an artificial intelligence system based on a variant of the Transformer architecture is introduced and applied to the problem of determining the ensemble of three-dimensional structures that a protein can access and their statistical weight. After being trained on a dataset of conformations of a particular protein obtained using classical all-atom molecular dynamics, this neural network is able to directly generate physically plausible protein conformations similar to those obtained both through molecular dynamics and experimentally. The ordered sequence of conformations obtained as output of the neural network rapidly converges towards the most populated regions of the protein's conformational landscape, suggesting that the system may have learned not only the protein's three-dimensional organization but also the thermodynamic weights of the various feasible conformations. These features, combined with its simplicity and low computational load, make this Transformer variant an ideal candidate for the development of systems capable of performing generative molecular dynamics.
♻ ☆ Spontaneous Mutations from Terahertz Proton Tunneling
Noah Bray-Ali
Protons in the bonds between base pairs of the double helix store the code of life by breaking the dyad symmetry that swaps the sense strand with its complementary partner. When these bonds break during replication and transcription, pairs of protons switch sides, restoring dyad symmetry and destroying genetic information. The observed rate of such spontaneous mutations is obtained in the sudden approximation for bond breaking provided protons in bonds between bases tunnel across the gap with terahertz frequency.

☆ Midsagittal Corpus Callosum Parameterization: A Framework for Spatially Detailed Diffusion MR Imaging Analysis
Caio Santana, Catherine Lebel, Richard Frayne, Letícia Rittner
The corpus callosum (CC) is the largest white matter structure in the human brain and plays a central role in interhemispheric communication. Diffusion MR imaging is widely used to investigate microstructural organization, but commonly used analysis approaches have important limitations. Voxel-based methods rely heavily on accurate inter-subject registration and smoothing choices, tract-based spatial statistics (TBSS) restricts the analysis to a narrow white matter skeleton, and parcellation approaches reduce spatial detail by averaging diffusion measures over relatively large regions. In this work, we introduce a parameterization framework for the analysis of the midsagittal CC that resamples the structure using a standardized grid of points, establishing a geometric correspondence across individuals without explicit inter-subject registration. In a test-retest dataset, the method provided stable measurements while covering most of the midsagittal CC, and sensitivity analyses showed that results in the central CC changed little under perturbations of the input CC mask, whereas peripheral regions were more sensitive. The framework was further evaluated in group analyses of aging and Alzheimer's disease in datasets that differ in acquisition protocol and spatial resolution. Effect-size maps and quantitative comparisons showed that its results were largely consistent with TBSS and parcellation-based analyses in the overall spatial pattern of effects, while also revealing additional spatially localized differences. These findings suggest that the parameterization provides a simple, computationally efficient, and spatially detailed approach for investigating midsagittal CC microstructure, offering a structure-specific complement to whole-brain and region-based methods.
