☆ CARVE: A glycan-specific framework for assessing protein-glycan binding-pose quality
Yifei Xiao, Huifeng Zhao, Silong Zhai, Zichang Jin, Tingjun Hou, Yu Kang
Protein-glycan interactions depend on the precise three-dimensional arrangement of carbohydrates at protein-binding interfaces, yet reliable assessment of predicted complex structures remains challenging. Existing evaluations commonly rely on fixed root-mean-square deviation (RMSD) thresholds, which do not fully account for the structural diversity and conformational flexibility of glycans. Here we developed CARVE, a glycan-specific framework for evaluating protein-glycan structure predictions. Through topology-aware glycan parsing, CARVE integrates orientation RMSD (oRMSD), interface-ring RMSD (irRMSD) and the fraction of native contacts (Fnat) to assess glycan scaffold geometry, interface-ring placement and native-contact recovery. These descriptors define four quality categories (CARVE-Level) with geometric thresholds that scale with the degree of polymerization (DP), and a continuous CARVE-Score, which reproduced those categories on 25,068 independently generated poses without recalibration (macro-F1 = 0.892). Pairing these measures with glycan-specific topological, chemical and geometric validity checks, we benchmarked nine prediction methods on CARVE-Bench, comprising 1,974 experimentally determined protein-glycan complexes. Among the top 25 predictions per target, HADDOCK3 achieved the highest Acceptable-level success rate (47.0%), whereas no method produced a high-quality, structurally valid pose for more than one in five. Collectively, CARVE provides a standardized basis for assessing protein-glycan structure prediction methods and guiding the development of more accurate docking and co-folding approaches.
☆ Deciphering multiple binding modes in large protein complexes via Enhanced Sampling and beta-VAE: the CYFIP1-eIF4E case
Francesco Pignotti, Emanuele Telari, Edoardo Milanetti, Anna La Teana, Jesmina Rexha, Alice Romagnoli, Luca Maragliano, Giovanni Di Muccio
Eukaryotic initiation factor 4E (eIF4E) drives cap-dependent translation, regulated by 4E-binding proteins (4E-BPs) competing for its dorsal and lateral surfaces. CYFIP1, a scaffold protein, is both a WAVE Regulatory Complex component and a non-canonical 4E-BP repressing translation via a short helical motif, linked to autism spectrum disorders and cancer. Prior work modeled only this isolated motif as a peptide, leaving the full interface uncharacterized. Starting from this preliminary evidence, we modeled the full-length proteins as flexible partners: all-atom simulations fed a data-driven docking search (HADDOCK3), which guided funnel-restrained Temperature-Accelerated Molecular Dynamics of a coarse-grained Martini 3 representation. Ten replicas accumulated 100 $μ$s of enhanced sampling. Funnel-restraint variables efficiently sample binding and unbinding but are structurally degenerate: direct clustering of the raw trajectory collapses these poses into poorly resolved groups. We instead reweighted the biased trajectories onto a contact-based latent space built by benchmarking PCA against a beta-Variational Autoencoder, then clustered minima of the equilibrium free-energy surface: only intermediate regularization resolved distinct, homogeneous modes. Three such modes recur, engaging eIF4E's canonical dorsal site, its non-canonical lateral site, and both. We find that CYFIP1 also engages eIF4E through a second surface, the same that mediates its WAVE-complex dimerization, and a basic patch. Both plausibly explain residual binding previously reported after motif mutation. This suggests its translational and cytoskeletal roles compete for the same surface. Most contacts on eIF4E fall outside this dorsal/lateral interface. This heterogeneous, plastic ensemble offers a structural basis for mutagenesis and therapeutic peptide design.
☆ Multi-Assay Antibody Developability Prediction with Shared Structure-Language Representations
Daria B. Kokh
Early assessment of antibody developability is critical in drug discovery, but computational approaches remain limited by the scarcity of experimental data. We explore two strategies for working within that limit. First, we designed per-residue physicochemical descriptors encoding the local structural environment, including new terms such as directional charge asymmetry and screened electrostatic potential. These feed a convolutional network, combined with protein language model (PLM) embeddings in a two-stream architecture: the two are encoded and predicted independently and merged only at the prediction stage. Second, a shared trunk with per-assay heads passes information between related assays. Both are implemented in the Multi-Assay Developability Model (MADM) and evaluated on eight assays: HIC, heparin affinity (HAC), AC-SINS, and polyspecificity ELISA against BVP, dsDNA, insulin, ovalbumin and soluble membrane proteins (SMP), with labels from 29 open sources. Structural descriptors match or exceed PLM embeddings (AbLang2, ESM2) on several assays; combining the streams gives a small but consistent gain in accuracy and reduces seed-to-seed variance. Multi-assay training raises Spearman rho for ovalbumin and SMP by about 0.1, where assays correlate and share sequence space. Under cluster-split cross-validation the strongest results are for HAC, ovalbumin, insulin, SMP and HIC (rho = 0.70-0.82); AC-SINS, BVP and dsDNA are weaker (0.50-0.55). In these three, sequence clusters largely coincide with data source, so a held-out fold measures transfer to differently calibrated measurements as well as to new sequences. On the 80-antibody GDPa3 hold-out, performance falls by rho around 0.1 for all but SMP (0.22). Transfer to the VHH panel of Ginkgo GDPa5 gives comparable results, and scFv validation against binary SMP labels yields F1 = 0.89, AUC = 0.95.

♻ ☆ Learning Task-Specific Antibody Representations via Function-Aware Masking
Ayan Goel, Thomas A. Walton, Amirali Aghazadeh
Antibody-specific language models pretrained via masked language modeling (MLM) learn representations that are critical for downstream sequence design and property prediction tasks. Yet, the corruption process itself is rarely leveraged as a source of inductive bias during pretraining. While preferentially masking complementarity-determining regions (CDRs) improves binding-related predictions, antibodies possess diverse biological priors over a variety of functions. Herein, we introduce function-aware masking, a family of pretraining algorithms that align mask placement with specific functional priors (e.g., from IMGT annotations or structure predictions) to shape the learned representation space. We show that these specialist masking strategies significantly improve performance on their respective objectives, yielding up to a 14% gain on structure-related tasks and up to a 5.9x improvement on CDR-related tasks. To further improve performance across multiple functional axes, we develop hybrid masking strategies that integrate multiple priors, balancing reconstruction over binding, structural, and biophysical objectives. Our results demonstrate that informed mask placement provides a parameter-free mechanism for imposing functional inductive biases in antibody language model training.

♻ ☆ Monroe: A Molecular Foundation Model for In-Context Probabilistic Inference NeurIPS 2026
Blazej Banaszewski, Andrew W. Fitzgibbon
Bioassay activity prediction is often data-limited because drug-discovery datasets rely on time-consuming and expensive wet-lab experiments for data generation and evaluation. This challenge has inspired recent research into molecular foundation models (MFMs), which aim to encode general-purpose chemical knowledge into molecular representations that generalize well in data-constrained scenarios. This paper presents Monroe, a new MFM with several innovations over the existing state of the art: increased scale allowing pre-training on over 81 million molecules from the PM6 quantum chemistry dataset; improved graph representation of stereochemistry; improved training losses including conformer denoising and embedding decorrelation; improved multi-task learning; and the use of a prior-data-fitted model (TabPFNv3) for downstream in-context prediction. Our evaluations use a principled pairwise comparison framework that measures statistically significant performance differences. Across established Polaris benchmarks, Monroe matches or exceeds existing MFMs, while on activity cliff benchmarks, designed to assess utility for molecular discovery, it achieves significant improvements over prior methods. Finally, ablation and transfer experiments show that PFN-based downstream predictors also substantially improve two leading existing models, MiniMol and CheMeleon, yielding new state-of-the-art variants we call MiniMol_PFN and CheMeleon_PFN, suggesting that our downstream adaptation strategy generalizes beyond Monroe. Source code is at github.com/blazejba/Monroe

☆ LaMe: a Joint Latent Mediation Framework for High-Dimensional Systems
Gaël Poux-Médard, Kaniav Kamary, Yohann De Castro, Vivian Viallon
Mediation analysis seeks to understand how an exposure influences an outcome through intermediate variables. However, as increasingly rich datasets become available, hundreds to thousands of potential mediators make these systems difficult to model and hard to interpret. Latent-variable approaches offer a natural way to accommodate these high-dimensional settings. However, existing latent mediation methods face limitations, including overparameterization, biased inference, or limited scalability. We develop LaMe, a joint linear-Gaussian framework that summarizes a high-dimensional exposure-mediator-outcome system through a small number of latent variables. We identify natural direct and indirect effects under an explicit assumption that the manifest measurements fully capture the latent mediators, and characterize conditions under which the latent representation effectively restricts the observed cross-covariance structure. Simulations assess recovery of observed association parameters, natural effects, and manifest features associated with latent mediation pathways across a range of challenging settings. Four real-world applications demonstrate competitive predictive performance with compact latent representations, including single-dimensional representations of biomarker and proteomic measurements. Together, these results establish LaMe as a practical framework for estimating and summarizing multivariate mediation systems under explicit structural and measurement assumptions.
☆ COPASI Skills: Agent Skills for COPASI analyses through Basico
Frank T. Bergmann, Sven Sahle
Biochemical models represent biological systems as networks of reactions that describe the production, consumption, and transformation of molecular species over time. These models provide a quantitative framework for analyzing system dynamics and enable computational experiments that complement traditional wet-lab approaches, helping researchers gain mechanistic insight and make predictive assessments of biological behavior. COPASI is a widely used program for simulating and analyzing those models. These tasks are made availabe to Python through Basico. COPASI Skills is a set of instruction files that tell an assistant how to drive Basico for one analysis at a time. Each skill is a directory containing a `SKILL.md` file. A short description at the top tells the assistant when the skill applies, and the rest of the file states which calls to make, which files to write, and which potential neighboring skill to choose additionally. A researcher can ask for example, in ordinary language, for a time course, a fit, or a published model. The assistant follows the matching skill and leaves tables or a report next to the model. The collection is released under a two-clause BSD license from [https://github.com/fbergmann/copasi-skills](https://github.com/fbergmann/copasi-skills).

☆ More Genes, Faster Inference: Efficient Large-Panel Spatial Transcriptomics Prediction from Histology
Songming Zhang, Yuxiao Luo, Shifu Luo, Siran Chen, Shuquan Su, Jinyan Li
Spatial transcriptomics (ST) prediction from histology aims to infer spatially resolved gene expression profiles directly from tissue images. This is a challenging problem that is currently often addressed with complicated model architectures, additional inputs, and complicated training procedures, while remaining limited to fixed, small gene panels. This raises a natural question: Is there an efficient method for large-panel ST prediction? Here, we present GeneFlow, a simpler yet effective framework for large-scale spatial gene expression prediction that unifies flow-matching generative modeling with structured multimodal fusion. Specifically, GeneFlow first encodes both histological morphology and cross-gene interactions in spatial spots through histology and gene tokenizers. The model directly predicts spatial gene expression, rather than an intermediate velocity field, while being trained with a velocity-based flow-matching objective. We show that, even without relying on heavy generative pipelines, GeneFlow achieves state-of-the-art performance across multiple ST benchmarks, with up to 56.8% gains in 1,000-gene prediction. Extensive experiments further show that GeneFlow produces biologically meaningful spot profiles while reducing computational cost by orders of magnitude, thereby improving efficiency and scalability, particularly in clinical settings. We believe GeneFlow provides an opportunity for leveraging widely available histology images for downstream biological discovery. Code is available at: https://github.com/ZZhangsm/GeneFlow

☆ Match and Correct: A Repeated Computational Motif in Evoformer Layers NeurIPS 2026
Ishan Khire, Naren Manikandan, Tyler Hayes, Giri Krishnan
Triangle attention and triangle multiplicative updates form key components of the Evoformer, and were introduced specifically to encourage consistency with the triangle inequality. Yet whether representational changes over layers reduce triangle inequality violations has not been tested. We probe the pair representation at each of OpenFold's 48 Evoformer layers, decoding final C$_α$-C$_α$ distances with four probe families (a shared linear probe, per-protein linear probes, a sequence-separation-gated linear probe, and XGBoost) and reconstructing intermediate 3D structures from the decoded distances via multidimensional scaling. Across a cohort of 552 CAMEO and ECOD proteins, distance decodability ($R^2$) and reconstruction quality (TM-score) rise in early layers, plateau in the middle of the network, and rise again in late layers. Directly measuring triangle-inequality violations in the decoded distances reveals a similar, more exaggerated pattern: violations rise to a peak, fall to a trough, rise to a second peak, and only then decline, revealing two distinct episodes of decreasing normalized triangle violations rather than one continuous improvement. Cumulative forward and reverse layer-wise ablations of the Pair Transition, MSA Transition, and triangle mechanisms localize the regions in which specific Evoformer components become essential. The two stages of geometric refinement coincide with the use of the Pair Transition MLP and the triangle mechanisms. These results characterize how distance information and decoded geometric consistency evolve across layers, and motivate hypotheses about the computations behind their trajectories.

☆ Functional nestedness of soil nematode communities across Europe: integrating morphological counts and eDNA reads
Giuseppe Nicolosi, Michael Bonkowski, Carlo Febbi, Christian Mulder
1. Soil nematodes occupy multiple trophic positions and contribute to key processes in soil food webs. Their communities vary across land uses and environmental conditions, yet it remains unclear whether these differences mainly reflect taxon replacement or nested patterns of taxon occurrence. 2. Here, we analysed two independent European datasets of soil nematode communities, SOB4ES, based on eDNA metabarcoding, and NemEu, based on morphological identification, to examine how genus composition varies across land uses. We partitioned community dissimilarity into turnover and nestedness-resultant components and compared sites under contrasting land uses with those sharing the same land use, while accounting for geographical and soil-related variation. We also assessed matrix-level nestedness, differences among trophic groups and associations between genus composition and environmental gradients. 3. In both datasets, genus turnover was consistently greater between contrasting land uses than within the same land use. Although the differences were modest, they remained significant after accounting for geographical distance and genus richness, whereas nestedness-resultant dissimilarity contributed little to land-use differentiation. These findings suggest that land-use-associated differences in European nematode communities are expressed primarily through changes in genus identity rather than nested patterns of taxon occurrence. 4. The consistency of this result across independently assembled datasets supports the broader relevance of taxonomic turnover for understanding soil biodiversity patterns across European land uses.

♻ ☆ TractSpLearn: Specialized Shared-Manifold Learning for Individualized Detection of Subtle White Matter Abnormalities in Mild Traumatic Brain Injury
Jiqing Huang, Ali Al-Husseini, Yi Chen, Anna Gard, Laurent Lamalle, Mohamed Ali Bahri, Markus Nilsson, Niklas Marklund, Christophe Phillips, Evgenios N. Kornaropoulos
Traumatic brain injury (TBI) often leads to subtle white matter damage that remains undetected on conventional MRI. Diffusion kurtosis imaging (DKI), an extension of diffusion tensor imaging (DTI), provides complementary information on non-Gaussian water diffusion and is sensitive to complex white-matter microstructure. With the advent of ultra-high-field MRI, the spatial resolution and signal-to-noise ratios (SNR) have been significantly enhanced, enabling more precise visualization of subtle abnormalities. Building on these advances, we developed TractSpLearn, an individualized tract-based learning framework that jointly considers within-group variability and between-group differences. Unlike the original TractLearn framework, which learns a normative manifold exclusively from healthy controls, TractSpLearn incorporates both healthy controls and patients to learn a shared manifold with a healthy-anchored representation and an additional patient-related component. To assess the performance of the proposed method, we compared TractSpLearn with the original TractLearn in three cohorts: (i) healthy controls (HC), (ii) athletes with persistent post-concussive syndromes (PPCS), and (iii) athletes with repeated head injuries (RHI), with abnormalities particularly evident in axial kurtosis (AK) and mean diffusivity (MD). In RHI, TractSpLearn highlighted recurrent abnormalities across patients. In the PPCS cohort, the overall group-level differences were more modest, potentially reflecting both limited statistical power due to the small sample size and partial normalization of white-matter alterations during recovery. Still TractSpLearn identified abnormality evidence in more patients and across more affected tracts than TractLearn.

♻ ☆ ViroGym: Realistic Large-Scale Benchmarks for Evaluating Viral Proteins
Yichen Zhou, Jonathan Golob, Amir Mohammad Karimi Mamaghan, Stefan Bauer, Patrick Schwab
Protein language models (pLMs) have shown strong potential for zero-shot prediction of missense variant effects, yet systematic benchmarking on viral proteins remains limited, a critical gap given the need for proactive tools that can anticipate emerging mutations ahead of experimental validation. Biomolecules 6
☆ Using an energy pixels approach to estimate the size a 2nd Generation DEL must be to train a high-resolution binding model
Boris P. Belotserkovskii, R. Edward Watts
DNA-encoded libraries (DELs) are commonly used to find small-molecule binders to a target protein. But the hard part is no longer finding a hit. It is building a machine-learning model good enough to guide the medicinal chemistry that follows, one that can resolve the shift in affinity when a chemist changes a single atom. Such a model needs two things from its training data: dense sampling of the small patch of chemical space around the hit, and an adequate number of genuinely tight binders within it. A diverse naive DEL gives neither. It samples an enormous space, so any one neighborhood is covered far too thinly, and the tightest binders are rare. A model trained on that naive DEL data must extrapolate to binders tighter than any it has seen, and that is where models can fail. One way to get training data with both properties is a second-generation DEL built around the hit. We ask how complex that library must be. We treat binding as a sum of small, independent interactions, called pixels on the target and antipixels on the ligand. From the spread of affinities measured in the naive library, with no structural information and nothing yet synthesized, we estimate the complexity such a library must have to supply that dense sampling and those tight binders. The method does two useful things. It turns the number of tight binders a model needs into the number of compounds a library must contain. And it estimates the expected yield of a randomly designed second-generation DEL, so the gain from designing the library with machine learning or active learning can be measured against a baseline, without the expense of a control library as large as the experiment itself.

☆ AI for Biologists: The Knowledge Boundaries of Foundation Models in the Age of Agentic Systems
Tian Cai, Andrew Kleist, Jaimin Patel, Duccio Malinverni, Alexander K. Taylor, Malvika Sharan, Renato Umeton, M. Madan Babu
Artificial intelligence (AI) is changing how biological research is done. Biological foundation models trained on millions of nucleotides, protein, and single-cell measurements predict protein structures, mutation effects, and cellular responses to genetic perturbations. Agentic systems, AI assistants built on large language models, can run multistep analyses from plain-language requests and increasingly call foundation models as tools. Yet it is often unclear when these models' outputs can be trusted. We frame this as a model's knowledge boundary: the range of inputs, tasks, and conditions over which its outputs are reliable. This review explains how foundation models are built and proposes that five design elements (data, architecture, training scheme, evaluation scheme, and post-analysis) define this boundary. We examine evidence on where current models succeed or fail and present standardized descriptions of model limits so that researchers, and the agents acting for them, can use these tools reliably.

☆ Mechanism-Level Chemical Reaction Simulation with Electron Bookkeeping Transformer
Sarah Cao, Shitong Luo, Connor W. Coley
Modeling chemical reaction mechanisms is central to understanding and predicting chemical reactivity. We present Electron Bookkeeping Transformer (Bookkeeper), a data-driven model that simulates chemical reactions at mechanistic resolution by generating electron movement trajectories. Following the symbolic arrow-pushing formalism in chemistry, Bookkeeper explicitly models each electron as a token and predicts its movement sequentially, ensuring mass and charge conservation during both elementary step and reaction pathway predictions by construction. On reaction-size-balanced test sets, Bookkeeper improves top-1 elementary-step accuracy, top-1 pathway rank accuracy, and top-1 to top-5 product prediction accuracy over FlowER. On the entire test set, it achieves higher elementary-step top-1 accuracy but lower pathway accuracy than FlowER. Bookkeeper also samples elementary steps substantially faster, adapts to several unseen reaction types with minimal fine-tuning, and generates candidate chemical transformations for further screening. These results suggest that explicit electron bookkeeping is a useful framework for virtually screening chemical reactions across diverse reagents, validating computer-assisted synthesis planning predictions, and providing mechanistic interpretations of reactivity.
☆ Confident and Wrong: Structure Confidence Is a Poor Selection Filter for Protein Function
Eric LoPrete, Pavle Jeremić
Structure-guided protein design can generate plausible candidates, but structural confidence does not predict function. We trained a function-guided model on measured metal-binding affinity with protein sequence, ligand identity, and assay conditions, and used it to design rare earth element (REE) binding variants of lanmodulin, a lanthanide-binding protein with four EF-hand domains. We screened 297 designs from three strategies against terbium and dysprosium, adjacent lanthanides among the hardest REE pairs to separate. Selection on structural confidence inverted the outcome it was meant to improve. The Structure-Confidence-Selected panel carried the highest pTM, complex ipLDDT, and composite confidence scores, and expressed at 97%, yet only 16.7% of its designs bound terbium (95% CI 10.4 to 25.7), against 83.3% (74.6 to 89.5) for the function-guided panel, whose confidence scores were the lowest. The odds of binding differed 25-fold between the two panels (Fisher exact $p = 1 \times 10^{-20}$). No structural-confidence metric separated binders from non-binders consistently across panels, whereas the function-trained model's predicted pKd gave the highest ROC AUC in every panel, 0.79 to 0.92, and ranked measured affinity among function-guided binders at Spearman $ρ= 0.98$. On the design objective, Tb/Dy selectivity, the function-guided designs also exceeded the natural lanmodulin family, outranking a randomly drawn ortholog 84% of the time across 149 orthologs. Structural confidence is a plausibility signal, not a selection criterion for function; direct supervision on measured function is what ranks designs.

♻ ☆ Multiphysics Simulation Framework for Continuous Ion Transport and Energetics in Atmospheric Pressure Interfaces
Yihui Yan, Jozef Lengyel, Frederic Rosu, Valerie Gabelica
Optimizing atmospheric pressure interfaces requires balancing efficient ion transport with unwanted collision activation. The complex environment at the inlet region, including rapid pressure drops, RF/DC electric fields, ion-gas collisions, and space charge effects, makes simulation of this system challenging. In this work, we introduce MuCITE (Multiphysics simulation for Continuous Ion Transport and Energetics), which combines continuous ion injection, precomputed electric and gas flow fields, and particle-in-cell space charge updates. Furthermore, by employing improved impulsive collision theory, it can not only predict transport but also analyze the loss position of ions and the evolution of their kinetic energy, internal energy, and dissociation probability during transport. Transport predictions with ion currents and activation trends of in-source fragmentation were compared with experimental measurements using a custom-built source and a Q-Exactive HF source. The results show that the simulation predictions agree well with the experimental results and can serve as a reference before experiments. MuCITE allows users to derive ion transport, output beam characteristics, and ion energy through the configurable interface geometry and operating conditions. It supports simulation-guided design and tunable parameters to achieve predicted efficient ion transport or in source activation.

♻ ☆ Symmetry-Induced Conservation Laws, Three-Channel Attribution, and Solid-Phase Recycling in Prebiotic Enantiomeric Excess Amplification
Zeyu Jia, Anlin Gong, Bingmei Wu
Enantiomeric excess is the primary observable used to quantify chiral amplification across prebiotic chemistry, asymmetric autocatalysis, and multiphase crystallization. Because enantiomeric excess divides net chiral excess by total chiral mass, identical increases can arise either from parity-breaking synthesis of chiral centers or from symmetric phase and oligomer partitioning that depletes soluble mass. We establish a pathwise conservation law, a model-free three-channel attribution identity, and a continuous path-covariant Aumann--Shapley attribution for mirror-symmetric reaction networks. In any closed network lacking autocatalytic feedback or epimerization, net chiral excess remains invariant along every stochastic trajectory, locking liquid-phase amplification inversely to surviving chiral mass while Shear--Kramers--Lorentz relative-entropy balance bounds dissipation. We re-analyze seven experimental benchmark systems and show that standalone phase removal loses $91.7\%$--$98.9\%$ of chiral solute into racemic sediment in closed batches and washes out under open racemic inflow. We show that coupling eutectic partitioning with racemization recycling achieves $96.5\%$ homochiral crystal recovery, whereas evaporative autocatalysis crosses the open-flow bifurcation threshold with $81.9\%$ homochiral yield. Finally, we extend our non-crossing parity invariant across a fifty-nine-point multicomponent peptide ligation and seeded co-precipitation benchmark, evaluate a one-hundred-twelve-row calcite adsorption dataset, and resolve mineral-occlusion non-identifiability using three-block antipodal crystal pairs.

☆ Protein Language Models Fail To Capture Contact Predictions Under Invariant Relabeling NeurIPS 2026
Paarth Batra, Aryan Keluskar
Protein language models (pLMs) produce highly accurate contact maps from single-sequence inputs. People often take this accuracy as proof that these models implicitly learn invariant coevolutionary statistics, extracting the same signals as classical alignment-based methods like Potts. In this work, we introduce a diagnostic framework around a global bijective relabeling of the amino-acid alphabet. This transformation permutes residue letters while leaving all alignment-level information, column entropies, and pairwise edit distances unchanged. Pseudolikelihood Potts models and inverse-covariance estimators remain invariant under this transformation, but pLMs fail the test. Across multiple architectures (ESM-2, MSA Transformer, AMPLIFY) and model scales (8M to 3B parameters), pLM contact maps collapse under arbitrary relabeling. Using Hamming distance controls and biochemical grouping interventions, we attempt to isolate what likely causes this degradation and find that these models rely on explicit, canonical residue identities (``alphabet anchoring'') rather than pure coevolutionary statistics. Increasing scale makes models more responsive to biochemical classes, but their performance still degrades under relabeling. Our findings show a gap between how pLMs and classical coevolutionary models compute contacts. More broadly, our work shows how diagnostic perturbations that leave classical models provably unchanged can expose hidden representation biases and prevent over-reliance on neural black boxes in biological modeling.

☆ A risk-aware individualized blood pressure target to avoid hypotension in surgery
Simon Bur{é}, Jona Joachim, Florent Di Meglio
We propose an online method for choosing both a blood pressure target and a norepinephrine dose for an individual surgical patient. From a short record of mean arterial pressure (MAP) and norepinephrine delivery, the method fits a reduced pharmacokinetic/pharmacodynamic model and uses a local Gaussian approximation to propagate uncertainty to future MAP readings. It searches for the lowest target for which a bolus and constant infusion keep the predicted probability of at least one MAP reading below 65 mmHg after the rise phase within a prescribed limit. If no such target is found, the decision returns to the clinician. We assess calibration of the predicted minimum MAP on retrospective surgical records under the doses actually administered. In a cohort of 4,096 virtual patients, the method recommended a dose for 74 % of patients at a nominal risk of 5 %. Among the patients receiving a recommendation, 6.9 % had a reading below 65 mmHg. A fixed target of 70 mmHg yielded a rate of 25 % when applied to the full virtual cohort.
♻ ☆ Calibeating Prediction-Powered Inference NeurIPS 2026
Lars van der Laan, Mark Van Der Laan
We study semisupervised mean estimation with a small labeled sample, a large unlabeled sample, and a black-box prediction model whose output may be miscalibrated. A standard approach in this setting is augmented inverse-probability weighting (AIPW) [Robins et al., 1994], which protects against prediction-model misspecification but may be inefficient when the prediction score is poorly aligned with the outcome scale. We introduce Calibrated Prediction-Powered Inference, which post-hoc calibrates the prediction score on the labeled sample before using it for semisupervised estimation. This simple step requires no retraining and can calibeat the original score, improving it both as a predictor of the outcome and as a regression adjustment for semisupervised inference. We study both linear and isotonic calibration. For isotonic calibration, we establish first-order optimality guarantees: isotonic post-processing can improve predictive accuracy and estimator efficiency relative to the original score and simpler post-processing rules, while replacing the fitted isotonic score by its conditional-mean oracle yields no additional first-order variance reduction. For linear calibration, we show first-order equivalence to PPI++. We also clarify the relationship between existing estimators, showing that the original PPI estimator is a special case of AIPW and can be inefficient when the prediction model is accurate, while PPI++ is AIPW with empirical efficiency maximization [Rubin and van der Laan, 2008]. In simulations and real-data experiments, our calibrated estimators often outperform PPI and are competitive with, or outperform, AIPW and PPI++. We provide an accompanying Python package, ppi_aipw, at https://larsvanderlaan.github.io/ppi-aipw/.

♻ ☆ Attributing Cohen's d: Training Data Attribution for Disease-Related Effects in Normative Age Biomarkers
Jakob Snel, Marc-Andre Schulz
Normative age models are trained to predict chronological age in a nominally healthy cohort. In patients, the gap between predicted and chronological age is read as disease risk. We attribute the disease-related effect size of the age gap directly to individual training samples, rather than using a prediction-level loss as the attribution target. For the Cohen's d effect size, the resulting closed-form influence functional, validated against leave-one-out retraining, ranks training samples by their effect on held-out case-control separation. Across four diseases and two biomarker modalities in UK Biobank, removing the 10% most influential training samples raises held-out disease-related effect size in every seed. It more than doubles the metabolomic-age effect for type 2 diabetes and raises the brain-age effect for multiple sclerosis by roughly a third. Random removal leaves effect size flat even at 50% removal, confirming the gain comes from which samples are removed, not how many. Flagged subjects, missed by diagnosis-based exclusion, show elevated cardiometabolic markers the model never sees, consistent with subclinical contamination of the reference cohort. For type 2 diabetes, where the method gains most, the marker recovered is HbA1c, the standard measure of blood sugar control. We release our influence-function package pyinfluence.
♻ ☆ On the Promises and Limits of Multi-omics Integration for Deconvolution: The HADACA3 Benchmark
Hugo Barbot, Elise Amblard, Nicolas Homberg, Lucie Lamothe, Morgane Térézol, Hadaca Consortium, Mira Ayadi, Aurélia Baurès, Lucile Armenoult, Yasmina Kermezli, Audrey Cassard, Jérome Cros, Carl Herrmann, Sebastien Dejean, Lionel Spinelli, David Causeur, Florent Chuffart, Anaïs Baudot, Yuna Blum, Magali Richard
Understanding the cellular composition of complex tissues, such as tumors, is a key challenge in biology and medicine. A common approach, known as deconvolution, aims to estimate Understanding the cellular composition of complex tissues, such as tumours, is a key challenge in biology and medicine. Deconvolution methods estimate cell-type proportions from bulk molecular measurements, and the increasing availability of multiple molecular modalities has raised the expectation that their integration should improve estimation accuracy. However, whether and under which conditions multi-omics integration actually improves deconvolution remains unclear. Here we present HADACA3, a community-driven competition followed by a modular benchmark of more than 18,000 pipelines combining pre-processing, feature selection, deconvolution and integration of RNA and DNA methylation (DNAm), across nine pancreatic cancer datasets. With the available references, DNAm alone achieves the highest median performance, outperforming RNA alone and most integration strategies. Dataset-specific optimisation of generic integration pipelines rarely improves upon DNAm alone: the best configurations mostly reduce the weight of RNA, and their gains do not generalise across datasets. The only consistent improvement, confirmed on unseen data, comes from an integration rule that estimates cancer cell types from DNAm alone and averages both modalities for non-cancer cell types. These findings challenge the assumption that integrating more molecular data necessarily improves deconvolution. Instead, effective multi-omics integration relies on prior knowledge of the cell-type-specific strengths of each modality, rather than on generic statistical integration strategies.

☆ CupOFMoCA: Coupled Objective-Guided Discrete Flows for Molecular Conjugate Assembly NeurIPS 2026
Ruoxi Rosie Zhang, Ziang Li, Jiatao Gu, Pranam Chatterjee
Molecular conjugates, including PROTACs and peptide-drug conjugates (PDCs), derive their function from the joint behavior of multiple coupled components, yet most generative approaches design these components independently and combine them only after generation. Such staged pipelines ignore cross-component dependencies and often produce conjugates that are chemically invalid, fall outside empirical conjugate distributions, or lose function upon assembly. In this work, we introduce Coupled Objective-Guided Discrete Flows for Molecular Conjugate Assembly (CupOFMoCA), a discrete generative method that enforces cross-component chemical constraints during conjugate generation. CupOFMoCA restricts guided updates to chemically feasible conjugate states and reweights local transitions using molecular design objectives. Across three PDC systems, coupled generation achieved 100% conjugation validity among valid peptides and increased valid yield over matched post-hoc filtering, with comparable predicted binding scores across the full valid sets. In three-seed evaluations of three PROTAC systems, CupOFMoCA achieved the highest assembly validity on two systems and generated more assembly-valid molecules per second than the evaluated baselines. We further evaluated the generated PROTACs with post-hoc DegradeMaster scoring and explicit-solvent molecular dynamics, supporting their predicted activity and conformational properties. Together, these results establish CupOFMoCA as an efficient generator of molecular conjugates, supporting therapeutic development via coordinated control of assembly and molecular properties.

☆ Silscore: modelling siRNA potency with positional thermodynamics and data-selected nucleotide-identity terms
Giuseppe Sacco, Isabella Baldini, Riccardo Paone, Mattia Capulli, Guido Sanguinetti, Giovanni Bussi
Small interfering RNAs (siRNAs) enable programmable gene silencing, but candidates targeting the same transcript can differ markedly in potency. Existing predictors range from interpretable empirical rules with limited accuracy to high-capacity models that can lose performance when datasets or targets change. We present Silscore, a guide-only model that combines a quadratic preference for overall duplex stability, a position-resolved profile of nearest-neighbour stacking energies and sparse nucleotide-identity terms selected from residuals left unexplained by thermodynamics. Its main fit uses 26 parameters, fewer than half as many as any trainable comparator evaluated here. We evaluate Silscore on the canonical split of the Huesken dataset, two external datasets (Mixset and Katoh-Suzuki), and an evaluation in which the same guides are tested after training on dissimilar targets. Silscore attains the highest observed Pearson correlation in all three transfer evaluations, and the data support an advantage over every applicable comparator on Katoh-Suzuki. For a fixed evaluation set, we compare two equally sized training sets with higher or lower sequence similarity to the evaluation sequences. After training on the less similar sequences, Silscore has the highest observed correlation, and the data do not establish a change in its performance. The fitted parameters can also be interpreted directly: identity terms locate sequence effects that base-pairing energies cannot express, global stability has a data-estimated intermediate optimum, and local energy profiles recover terminal asymmetry and a preference for weaker seed and stronger central pairing. Silscore thus provides a compact, data-driven model that combines strong predictive performance with robustness when tested in new settings and expresses previously reported empirical preferences as quantities estimated from data.

☆ Expanding the Decision Space of Pretrained Models through Structured Re-Decoding
Tinglan Wang, Qun Su, Shihang Wang, Chi Wang, Lin Wang, Shaobo Chen, Haixu Liu, Hai Wei, Yin Ma, Yu Kang, Qi Gao, Xiaojun Yao, Tingjun Hou, Kai Wen
Native decoders limit how pretrained models combine local predictions under new structural constraints. We introduce Structured Re-Decoding (SRD), a training-free framework that constructs local candidates from model scores, samples, or external specifications, jointly optimizes their selection, and verifies reconstructed outputs. We show that candidate recombination expands feasible support and can improve attainable reward under a fixed KL divergence budget. Across molecular generation, protein design, RNA structure prediction, and antibody modeling, SRD enables improved structured inference, including verified molecules absent from the original sample pool. External chemical templates further extend SRD to noncanonical amino acids, peptide cyclization, and grammar-guided molecular construction. Preliminary experiments on a physical coherent Ising machine demonstrate the feasibility of hardware-based optimization, suggesting broader opportunities for quantum and quantum-inspired approaches to structured inference and combinatorial optimization across scientific domains.

♻ ☆ Automatic Generation of Novel Highly-Functional Molecular Sequences
Vincent R. Ragusa, Christoph Adami
While multiple methods now exist to generate functional sequence variants from existing annotated examples, it is unclear how well such methods can be used to generate sequences that exceed the functionality of those contained in the example set. Using two complete fitness landscapes, we quantify how effectively generative tools can search sequence space and find rare, novel, highly functional sequences, focusing on variational auto-encoders and information-theoretic methods. ☆ Subject-Held-Out Evaluation of Deep Ground Reaction Force Estimators Across Two Laboratories: Leakage and Cross-Laboratory Transfer
Hernan David Santos, Yinong Chen, Yu Ding, Xudong Zhang, Jian Tao
Deep networks that estimate ground reaction forces and centers of pressure from motion-capture markers are validated within one laboratory, in a seldom-stated coordinate frame, and sometimes with trial-level splits that put the same person in the training and the test set. We measured what each practice costs, and whether such models transfer between laboratories. Three sequence models (convolutional, convolutional-recurrent, and convolutional-transformer) were evaluated on two independent datasets, locomotion (6 subjects) and occupational patient handling (10 subjects), under one pipeline, one tuning procedure, three seeds, and four protocols: trial-level split, leave-one-subject-out, pooled two-laboratory training with matched held-out subjects, and cross-laboratory transfer. At a fixed configuration, trial-level splits overstated the subject-held-out coefficient of determination by +0.040 to +0.194; on occupational data the overstatement exceeded every difference between architectures. With subjects held out, the three models reached values between 0.583 and 0.804; the leader differed between datasets, and gaps lay within subject-to-subject variability. Pooling the laboratories changed neither dataset on matched subjects. Transfer between laboratories was below chance in both directions, including the vertical force, which no in-plane rotation can affect. Leaving force targets in the plate frame while inputs were heading-aligned cost 0.57 of locomotion anteroposterior-force explained variance, enough to make that force look hardest; a Newton's-law correlation detected the mismatch before training. Split protocol and target frame changed reported accuracy more than architecture did. Learned force estimators should be validated with subjects held out, checked for frame consistency before training, and tested across laboratories before deployment claims are made.

☆ Robot-Induced Bias in Movement Smoothness Metrics for Rehabilitation: Quantification and Correction in Simulation
David Regev, Alex Scherbakov, Eran Beeri Bamani
Robot mechanics can bias kinematic measures of recovery and limit comparisons across rehabilitation devices. We developed a closed-loop human-robot simulation calibrated to published healthy and stroke group kinematics. Paired runs held motor-control parameters and movement plans fixed to quantify bias in seven metrics relative to a low-impedance reference, normalized using external repeatability benchmarks. Within a subset informed by published device specifications, median log dimensionless jerk (LDLJ) bias was 0.93 and 1.79 benchmark units for stroke and healthy profiles, respectively. Sobol analysis identified Coulomb friction and apparent mass as leading contributors; LDLJ bias persisted across 11 robustness variants. LDLJ bias increased as simulated movements became less fragmented. If recovery similarly reduces fragmentation, this effect could partly mask improvement in measured smoothness. Correction combining identified robot dynamics and observed metrics with gradient-boosted trees reduced median normalized absolute error from 0.77 to 0.10 for LDLJ and from 0.24 to 0.08 for spectral arc length (SPARC) in the device-informed subset of a random 20% hold-out. Median smoothness residuals remained at or below 0.15 benchmark units across four separately held-out parameter regions. Under an alternative controller, LDLJ error decreased from 1.60 to 0.17 without retraining, although transfer varied across metrics. These simulation results support mechanical characterization and movement-dependent correction toward more comparable robotic assessments, motivating validation in patients.

♻ ☆ Navigating Selection Bias in Genome-wide Association Studies (GWAS)
Ciarrah-Jane S Barry, Tahereh Gholipourshahraki, Zoltán Kutalik, Bjarni J Vilhjálmsson, Esben Agerbo
Selection bias arises when inclusion in a genetic study depends on traits, or causes of traits, that are associated with genotype. It is present in volunteer-based biobanks, case-control designs and studies of disease progression, and genome-wide association study (GWAS) summary statistics from affected samples can propagate this bias into post-GWAS analyses, such as polygenic scores (PGS), Mendelian randomization (MR) and genetic correlation analyses. Despite broad potential impact of selection bias it is sometimes ignored in genetic studies. Many methods exist to detect and correct selection bias, but they rely on different assumptions and require specific data. We organise ten methods applicable to GWAS by the decisions an analyst faces: whether to diagnose or correct, which data are available (individual-level or summary-level), and which form of selection is operating (non-representative participation, case-control ascertainment, index events, sex-differential participation or survival to recruitment). For each method, we describe its principle, applicability, limitations and data requirements. A companion GitHub repository implements nine methods in R and prepares input for the external SPREG software. It provides worked examples using simulated data with known true effects for each form of selection. An interactive tool supports method selection. We conclude with open methodological challenges.

♻ ☆ Flexible Flows for Biological Sequence Design NeurIPS 2026
Yogesh Verma, Dani Korpela, Harri Lähdesmäki, Vikas Garg
Designing functional biological sequences requires navigating vast discrete spaces under strict evolutionary and biophysical constraints. Discrete Flow Matching (DFM) offers a generative framework over such spaces, but existing approaches rely on biologically uninformative couplings and offer limited flexibility for variable-length sequence generation and fine-grained control. We propose a structured coupling that encodes domain-specific preferences among sequence elements, biasing the source distribution toward plausible regions without modifying the flow objective or training procedure. Building on this, we introduce a latent edit-based rate parameterization that models variable-length generation via edit operations conditioned on a shared global latent, akin to a latent variable model, while remaining tractable. We further introduce a latent classifier-free guidance mechanism that steers generation coherently in continuous latent space, along with Dirichlet-prior temperature scaling for test-time control over edit operations. ♻ ☆ Trajectory Inference of Human Aging from Cross-Sectional DNA Methylation Data
Chandan Gupta, Syed Haider, Pietro Liò
DNA methylation (DNAm) serves as one of the most robust molecular biomarkers of biological aging. While conventional epigenetic clocks accurately predict chronological age from high-dimensional CpG profiles, they treat aging as a static regression task, meaning they can only output a single score rather than simulating how an entire profile continuously changes over time. To reconstruct these continuous dynamics, we frame lifelong human epigenetic aging as a trajectory inference problem across discrete age snapshots derived from widely available cross-sectional data. We introduce a two-stage computational pipeline: first, an age-regularized Variational Autoencoder (VAE) maps high-dimensional CpG profiles onto a chronologically ordered latent manifold while preserving a generative decoder bridge back to the original methylation space. Second, we model the continuous movement across this latent space via Regularized Unbalanced Optimal Transport (RUOT) that unifies deterministic drift, random diffusion, and non-conservative mass changes. By resolving this RUOT formulation using the DeepRUOT framework, our model fluidly accommodates population-level density shifts like survivorship bias and cellular attrition without requiring rigid biological priors. Evaluated on a large-scale, 80-year pan-tissue dataset, our model demonstrates robust distribution interpolation and uncovers a prominent late-life surge in the learned growth field that mathematically captures the variance expansion driven by stochastic epigenetic drift. Finally, by decoding continuous latent paths back to individual CpG sites, we reconstruct and empirically verify distinct biological aging archetypes, offering a rigorous, generative paradigm for simulating human molecular aging.

♻ ☆ CHRep: Cross-modal Histology Representation and Post-hoc Calibration for Spatial Gene Expression Prediction
Changfan Wang, Xinran Wang, Donghai Liu, Fei Su, Lulu Sun, Zhicheng Zhao, Zhu Meng
Spatial transcriptomics (ST) enables spatially resolved gene profiling but remains expensive and low-throughput, limiting large-cohort studies and routine clinical use. Predicting spatial gene expression from routine hematoxylin and eosin (H&E) slides is a promising alternative, yet under realistic leave-one-slide-out evaluation, existing models often suffer from slide-level appearance shifts and regression-driven over-smoothing that suppress biologically meaningful variation. CHRep is a two-phase framework for robust histology-to-expression prediction. In the training phase, CHRep learns a structure-aware representation by jointly optimizing correlation-aware regression, symmetric image-expression alignment, and coordinate-induced spatial topology regularization. In the inference phase, cross-slide robustness is improved without backbone fine-tuning through a lightweight calibration module trained on the training slides, which combines a non-parametric estimate from a training gallery with a magnitude-regularized correction module. Unlike prior embedding-alignment or retrieval-based transfer methods that rely on a single prediction route, CHRep couples topology-preserving representation learning with post-hoc calibration, enabling stable neighborhood retrieval and controlled bias correction under slide-level shifts. Across the three cohorts, CHRep consistently improves gene-wise correlation under leave-one-slide-out evaluation, with the largest gains observed on Alex+10x. Relative to HAGE, the Pearson correlation coefficient on all considered genes [PCC(ACG)] increases by 4.0% on cSCC and 9.8% on HER2+. Relative to mclSTExp, PCC(ACG) further improves by 39.5% on Alex+10x, together with 9.7% and 9.0% reductions in mean squared error (MSE) and mean absolute error (MAE), respectively.
♻ ☆ Stand-In Effects and Pooling Amplification: Lessons from Biological World Models for Testing Action Effects
Bowen Xu, Boyu Chen, Jiaying Li
Whether an action-conditioned world model responds to actions is usually checked by replacing or removing the actions and measuring the loss in accuracy. Taking biological experiments as the guiding case, we show that such diagnostics report action effects where there are none: under autocorrelated designs, future actions stand in for history the model does not see. When the design law is known, a conditional randomization test written as an e-value is valid in finite samples whatever the model. A second failure arises whenever many sites share one action sequence, as in world models: the pooled score turns small model errors along the action contrast into an e-value near zero, so a valid test loses its power although the effect is real. Tempering the score changes the bet, not the validity. The log e-value is concave in the tempering factor and zero at zero, so too small a factor costs little and too large a one can cost everything; every single factor we tried, estimated or tuned, failed in some setting, and mixtures over a fixed grid never did. Our own preregistered analysis of optogenetic ERK experiments failed by pooling; a tempered statistic, registered before the data were obtained, then rejected the null on a never-opened field (E = 1.1e10). In three registered tests on models trained by others, the pooled test rejected in 0 of 96 units of a diffusion model, and reached the power bar in 10 of 26 Atari games under IRIS's token likelihood and 1 of 6 control tasks under TD-MPC2; mixtures fixed in advance reached it in 96 units, 24 games and 6 tasks. On recorded robot video with iVideoGPT the mixture rejected in 155 of 159 trajectories; the pooled test did not collapse there (143). Under exact nulls the statistics made 14 rejections in 26,664 tests.

☆ Emergent Biological Capabilities in a Foundation Model for Molecular Interactions NeurIPS 2026
Adi Mashiach
Foundation models trained on large unlabeled corpora develop emergent capabilities, tasks that go beyond the training objective. We extend this paradigm to multimodal biology with Bonbon, a foundation model for molecular interactions trained on approximately 3.2 trillion tokens of paired protein-ligand sequences. From a single representation space, Bonbon exhibits three zero-shot emergent capabilities at hierarchical levels of resolution: functional (mechanism of action), residue (binding site localization, including orthosteric/allosteric discrimination), and bond (covalent versus non-covalent engagement). All three capabilities generalize to out-of-distribution complexes. Bonbon was trained on protein-ligand sequences alone, without any task-specific supervision and without structural coordinates. We conducted controlled scaling experiments across six model scales from 42M to 1.6B parameters; every capability evaluated shows a sharp jump at the largest scale on continuous metrics, even as pretraining loss decreases smoothly. This demonstrates that loss-based scaling laws alone are insufficient to predict when zero-shot emergent capabilities become available. The capabilities reported here emerge after training at a 1:2,000 parameter-to-token ratio to accommodate the sparsity of paired biological interaction data. The same frozen representations achieve state-of-the-art binding and affinity prediction at five orders of magnitude greater speed than co-folding methods. In prospective wet-lab validation across nine disease targets spanning seven protein families, Bonbon achieves a 61.5% dose-response hit rate on de novo compounds, yielding structurally novel molecules not previously reported to inhibit these proteins, including for out-of-distribution targets.
comment: Accepted at NeurIPS 2026 (Main Track, Oral)
☆ ANTIOCH-VS: From Sequence to Hits with Modular, Open-Source Ensemble Virtual Screening
Hale S. Hasdemir, Venkata Sai Sreyas Adury, Pratyush Tiwary
Structure-based virtual screening needs a receptor conformation that fits the ligand class, but for most targets no ligand-bound crystal is available -- and a single predicted model or an apo structure often misses the binding-competent state. We present ANTIOCH-VS, an open-source framework that runs an entire in silico screening campaign, from a protein sequence to a ranked shortlist of candidate binders, as one plug-and-play workflow. It first builds a conformational ensemble by reduced-MSA subsampling with ColabFold, then uses AlphaFold2-RAVE (AF2RAVE) -- short molecular-dynamics simulations with State Predictive Information Bottleneck analysis -- to pick out docking-ready, Boltzmann-weighted metastable states. It then detects the binding pocket, prepares receptors and ligands, docks the library across the ensemble with DOCK6 or AutoDock Vina, builds a consensus ranking, and triages the top hits by physics-based metadynamics (CTMD). We benchmark it on two very different targets, JAK2 kinase and ADRB2 GPCR, using their DUD-E benchmark sets of known binders and property-matched decoys. Starting from sequence alone -- no ligand-bound crystal, no manual intervention -- the AF2RAVE ensembles gave top-20 shortlists strongly enriched for true binders: a 20% hit rate for JAK2 and 60% for ADRB2 (random ~1-2%), on par with ligand-bound crystals and, for the kinase, above single AF2 models and an apo crystal. The full pipeline runs end to end on a workstation or HPC from a single command, or each module can be run on its own with user-supplied inputs. ANTIOCH-VS is freely available at https://github.com/tiwarylab/antioch-vs
comment: 34 pages, 19 figures (6 main text, 13 supplementary); code available at https://github.com/tiwarylab/antioch-vs
♻ ☆ Fundamental Relation between Conductance of Biomolecules and the Fukui Function
Gabor Vattay
We derive the finite-temperature conductance of a molecule coupled to metallic leads using open-system time-dependent density functional theory (TDDFT). Tracing out the phonon bath via Wick's theorem yields an exchange-symmetric collision integral that preserves Pauli exclusion. When internal thermalization is faster than lead escape, as holds in proteins with multifractal orbitals at the Anderson transition, the exchange-correlation kernel projects out of the slow Liouvillian mode, making conductance directly proportional to the finite-temperature Fukui function of conceptual DFT.

♻ ☆ Platelet plug microstructure and flow modulate fibrin gelation dynamics: Insights from computational simulations
Janneke M. H. Cruts, Frank J. H. Gijsen, Aaron L. Fogelson, Anna C. Nelson
During the formation of a thrombus, the architecture of the growing platelet aggregate is heterogeneous, with areas of dense and loosely packed platelets. The surface of activated platelets facilitate biochemical coagulation reactions that ultimately result in the formation of a fibrin network which stabilizes the thrombus. How platelet-plug microstructure and flow jointly govern the onset and development of fibrin is incompletely understood. We developed a novel 2D computational framework that integrates (1) a pre-adhered, discrete platelet aggregate, (2) a reduced coagulation model that generates thrombin, and (3) a fibrin polymerization model. Three platelet-plug configurations were constructed with prescribed interplatelet gaps and simulations were performed for each with various wall shear rates. We quantified spatiotemporal clotting metrics, including coagulation factor concentrations, fibrin evolution, and gelation onset. Across geometries, gelation initiation accelerated with increasing plug density. For more dense geometries, gelation emerged first near the plug periphery. As the platelet density increased, intraplug transport was increasingly restricted and the thrombin concentrations in the spaces between platelets increased. In contrast, the loose plug supported fibrinogen replenishment deeper into the plug core. Thus, despite slower coagulation initiation due to reduced platelet surface area, monomer generation persisted further into the interior, causing gelation to begin at the vessel wall instead of the periphery. While the proposed model is a simplified representation of blood clot formation under flow, this framework provides a basis for systematic studies of platelet-coagulation interactions under flow, including extensions toward therapeutic developments relevant to prevention of cardiovascular disease.

☆ Pyrite: Building and Benchmarking Protein-Ligand Pose Ranking Agents NeurIPS 2026
Megha Srivastava, Michael H. Lim, Allison M. Keys, Pranav Murugan, Nate Gruver
Predicting how a ligand binds to its target is central to small-molecule drug discovery. Modern cofolding models can generate diverse candidate poses, but reliably ranking these candidates remains challenging. We introduce Pyrite, a pairwise pose-ranking benchmark comprising 1,500 pose pairs across diverse protein-ligand complexes. Expert chemists equipped with 3D visualization tools and physics-based scores perform near chance, highlighting the difficulty of interpreting available chemical signals. To investigate automated ranking, we introduce PyriteRep, a structured representation of molecular geometry, protein-ligand interactions, and physicochemical scores, and develop two LLM-based agents: PyriteReasoner, which directly compares poses through text-based reasoning, and PyriteFeaturizer, which generates executable code to construct features for logistic regression, optionally incorporating features derived from LLM subcalls. These agents achieve up to 67% and 74% accuracy, respectively, exceeding observed expert performance. LLM-guided feature discovery generally outperforms direct reasoning, while Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) improves open-weight models, enabling them to match or surpass closed-source frontier models in our evaluations. These results establish Pyrite as a challenging test of pose-ranking ability and highlight LLM-guided feature discovery as a promising approach to interpret and combine the outputs of computational chemistry tools.

☆ Benchmarking large language models for parameter-level structural identifiability classification in epidemic ODE models
Geunsoo Jang, K. Selçuk Candan, Gerardo Chowell
Structural identifiability determines whether unknown parameters can be uniquely recovered from ideal model outputs, making it a prerequisite for interpreting parameter estimates. We evaluated whether large language models (LLMs) can reliably provide preliminary structural identifiability classifications for epidemic models. We constructed a retrospective benchmark of 88 scenarios from 14 of the 27 studies considered, encompassing 696 parameter-level formal reference classifications from published results or Julia-based symbolic analysis. Scenarios were evaluated using Domain-Aware and canonically relabeled ("Domain-Anonymized") prompts, with ten runs per configuration and prompt condition. Six configurations completed all scheduled responses, yielding 83,520 parameter-level decisions for the primary comparison. GPT-5.5 Instant and GPT-5.5 Thinking Medium had the highest accuracies among complete configurations (88.71% and 88.07%). Canonical relabeling improved both configurations and attenuated their performance decline in scenarios with many unknown parameters or state variables, although effects differed across model families. Locally identifiable parameters were the most difficult class (highest strict recall among complete configurations: 42.00%), although the local reference set was small. In multivariable analysis, each additional unknown parameter reduced the odds of correct classification (OR 0.881; 95% CI 0.852-0.910), whereas additional observed outputs increased them (OR 1.666; 95% CI 1.503-1.846). Exploratory evaluations indicated strong performance for GPT-5.5 Thinking High and Claude Opus 5, despite incomplete coverage. LLMs may generate preliminary hypotheses or flag cases for formal review, provided outputs are checked against published results or formal symbolic methods. They should not be used to certify the structural identifiability of a complete model.

☆ Influence of the branching geometry on SPION propagation: coupling of experiment and simulation
Daniel Fleischhauer, Luiz C. P. Wille, Stefan Lyer, Jens Kirchner, Dietmar Drummer
Magnetic Drug Targeting (MDT) is an emerging therapeutic concept in cancer medicine, where superparamagnetic iron oxide nanoparticles (SPIONs) are used as carriers for medication. Their magnetic properties allow for the manipulation of particle transport within the vascular structure and thus targeted delivery to the tumor site. To develop effective concepts for particle steering and accumulation, a detailed understanding of the particle--flow interaction, especially at asymmetric vascular bifurcations, is needed. Based on hierarchical scaling laws, generalized bifurcation geometries with varying degrees of asymmetry were derived and additively manufactured. The particle distribution was investigated by in situ optical measurements under different flow conditions and magnetic field configurations and compared with Lagrangian particle and Volume of Fluid Simulations (VoFS). While the particle mass partition followed the hydraulic flow split in asymmetric bifurcations, density-driven stratification dominated in the symmetric bifurcation, where one daughter branch received only a minor fraction of the particle mass at low flow rates despite carrying half of the flow. The Lagrangian model reproduced the predominantly advective propagation, whereas only the VoFS approach captured the stratification-induced asymmetry. Magnetic actuation counteracted the stratification, reversed the passive branch preference and increased particle retention. From a molecular communication perspective, bifurcation geometry, collective volume effects and magnetic actuation thus jointly determine the received particle signal at vascular bifurcations.

☆ Similar visual tuning conceals different recovery dynamics in a model of macaque V1
Xialei Huang, Qingyu Yan, Zheng Bian, Zhuo-Cheng Xiao
Biologically detailed neuronal networks can model how brain circuits perform observed functions, but their high computational cost limits the comparisons needed to test biological hypotheses. To address this limitation, we extend a multiscale approach in which responses of small populations of spiking neurons are computed in advance and reused across a larger cortical circuit. In particular, we approximate these local responses by deep neural networks and achieve an estimated acceleration of the order $10^5$ for steady-state evaluation relative to spiking network simulation. Beyond computational acceleration, the smooth deep surrogate of local responses provides derivatives, allowing us to analyze linear stability and to trace how a perturbation in brain activity is transmitted through different pathways. We demonstrate the approach in a model of an input layer of macaque primary visual cortex (V1), testing circuits under visual stimulation and controlled perturbations. We resolve pathway contributions to stability, strong recurrent responses, and slow recovery. Yet different combinations of pathway strengths can support similar responses to visual stimuli while producing different recovery dynamics. Therefore, Steady responses and perturbation recovery place complementary constraints on candidate circuits. Overall, our approach offers efficient evaluation with an interpretable circuit structure, making systematic comparisons across stimuli and biological hypotheses practical.
☆ The Dose Makes the Poison: Exposure-Aware Evaluation of Generative Drug Design NeurIPS 2026
Maria Franz, N. Benjamin Erichson
Generative models are increasingly used to propose molecules for drug discovery. They can produce far more candidates than can be experimentally tested, making computational selection essential. Most selection methods rely on predicted molecular properties, such as potency and toxicity, but overlook how these properties translate into drug exposure at a given dose. As a result, molecules that appear promising on paper may require impractical doses or raise safety concerns. We investigate this gap by using physiologically based pharmacokinetic (PBPK) simulations to re-evaluate molecules selected from three PARP1-targeted generative pipelines. Of 60 shortlisted molecules, seven fail to achieve sufficient target exposure, while 28% of the remaining candidates show predicted safety risks at their required dose. Moreover, 85% of candidates with an achievable dose are outperformed by molecules overlooked during static selection. These findings suggest that accounting for dose and exposure can substantially change which generated molecules are considered promising for drug development.

♻ ☆ Learning Deep Phylogeny Through Epistasis-Aware Geometry
Jason Lamanna, Erfan Mowlaei, Xinghua Shi, Sudhir Kumar, Vincenzo Carnevale
Epistasis shapes protein evolution because the effect of a substitution depends on its sequence background. Site-independent substitution models can recover phylogeny accurately, but their resolution declines as substitutions accumulate and reshape mutational constraints. Potts models describe family-specific epistatic landscapes but leave the histories traversed through them unresolved. We introduce a generative framework that learns family-specific evolutionary structure directly from sequences evolving on such landscapes. Our variational autoencoder uses a covariance-matching loss to target epistatic dependencies and a correlation loss to align latent distance with sequence divergence before p-distance saturates. We compare two implementations that differ only in latent geometry. EucEvoGen uses Euclidean space, and HyperEvoGen uses a Poincaré ball. Trained on natural protein alignments, both reproduce family-level sequence diversity and recover higher-order sequence statistics beyond pairwise marginals more accurately than a Potts-model generator. In simulations of sequence evolution along Yule trees under a Potts Hamiltonian, HyperEvoGen recovers the known topologies at least as accurately as maximum-likelihood inference, improving on it for four of five tree-distance metrics. Its ancestral sequence reconstructions trail maximum likelihood for closely related sequences, overtake it beyond roughly 0.3 p-distance, and retain that advantage as divergence increases. EucEvoGen matches HyperEvoGen in sequence generation but yields less accurate phylogenetic inference. This comparison links the accuracy of deep phylogenetic inference to the global organization of the latent space. These results suggest that family-specific representations of epistatically constrained sequence space can extend phylogenetic inference into the regime where stationary site-independent models lose resolution.

♻ ☆ A Bayesian approach to model uncertainty in single-cell genomic data
Shanshan Ren, Thomas E. Bartlett, Lina Gerontogianni, Swati Chandna
Network models provide a powerful framework for analysing single-cell count data, facilitating the characterisation of cellular identities, disease mechanisms, and developmental trajectories. However, uncertainty modeling in unsupervised learning with genomic data remains insufficiently explored. Conventional clustering methods assign a singular identity to each cell, potentially obscuring transitional states during differentiation or mutation. This study introduces a variational Bayesian framework for clustering and analysing single-cell genomic data, employing a Bayesian Gaussian mixture model with Dirichlet priors to estimate the probabilistic association of cells with distinct clusters. This approach captures cellular transitions, yielding biologically coherent insights into neurogenesis and breast cancer progression. The inferred clustering probabilities enable further analyses, including Differential Expression Analysis and pseudotime analysis. We propose the Area Under the Curve as a novel way of quantifying clustering performance in relation to cell-type specific marker genes, as it better captures the clustering characteristics of interest and aligns closely with visual assessments in the scRNA-seq data. To summarise, this methodological advancement enhances the resolution of single-cell data analysis, enabling a more nuanced characterisation of dynamic cellular identities in development and disease.
♻ ☆ Reconstruction quality and restart repeatability are insufficient indicators of muscle synergy recovery by nonnegative matrix factorisation
Ye Ma, Dongwei Liu, Meijin Hou, Chenyi Guo
Muscle-synergy studies use factorisation methods such as nonnegative matrix factorisation (NMF) to extract muscle synergies from surface electromyography (sEMG). Reconstruction quality and restart repeatability are commonly used to evaluate these estimates, but whether they indicate recovery of underlying synergies remains unclear. Here, we defined synergy recovery as selection of the correct ground-truth rank and a minimum matched spatial cosine similarity of at least 0.80. We generated 4320 independent 16-muscle sEMG sequences across five simulation factors: ground-truth rank, signal-to-noise ratio, sequence length, spatial mixing and activation mixing. Rank selection used a variance accounted for (VAF) threshold of 0.90. At the selected ranks, NMF achieved VAF of at least 0.90 for every sequence, with median restart repeatability of 0.985. However, synergy recovery was achieved in only 7.92% of sequences. Supplying the ground-truth rank increased recovery to 60.79%. Reconstruction quality and restart repeatability had modest associations with spatial cosine similarity (Spearman's rho = 0.253 and 0.281, respectively). Recovery varied with all five factors, with nine significant two-factor interactions in exploratory analyses. In a supporting analysis, we assessed recovery using reference signals constructed from spatial weights and activation coefficients estimated from measured sEMG during five movement conditions. Selected ranks were lower than reference ranks in all five conditions despite high reconstruction quality. Accurate reconstruction and repeatable estimates therefore did not establish recovery of the underlying synergy structure. Physiological interpretations require additional evidence of functional relevance and assessment of sensitivity to the selected rank.

♻ ☆ Stand-In Effects and Pooling Amplification: Lessons from Biological World Models for Testing Action Effects
Bowen Xu, Boyu Chen, Jiaying Li
Action-conditioned world models are increasingly trained on data from randomized experiments, and whether their predictions respond to actions is usually checked by replacing or removing the actions and measuring the loss in accuracy. Taking biological experiments as the guiding case, we show that such diagnostics report action effects where there are none: under autocorrelated designs, future actions stand in for history the model does not see. When the design law is known, a conditional randomization test written as an e-value is valid in finite samples whatever the model, provided the resampler conditions on the full state the design used. A second failure arises whenever many sites share one action sequence, as in world models: the pooled score turns small model errors along the action contrast into overwhelming evidence against a true effect. In a pixel world, its median log e-value fell from -17.3 to -6832 as frames grew from 64 to 4096 pixels, while the oracle's rose. Tempering the score changes the bet, not the validity. Our own preregistered analysis of optogenetic ERK experiments failed in exactly this way; a tempered statistic, registered before the data were obtained, then rejected the null on a never-opened field (E = 1.1e10). Every single tempering factor we tried, estimated from residuals or tuned on validation data, collapsed in some setting; mixtures over a fixed grid of factors never did. In registered tests on three world-model families trained by others, the pooled test rejected in 0 of 96 units of a diffusion model in which actions mattered, and reached the registered power bar in 10 of 26 Atari games under IRIS's own token likelihood and 1 of 6 control tasks under TD-MPC2; mixtures fixed in advance reached it in all 96 units, 24 of 26 games and 6 of 6 tasks. Under exact nulls the statistics made 4 rejections in 22,848 tests.
