♻ ☆ DiffeoMorph: Learning Distributed 3D Morphogenesis with Rotation-Invariant Spectral Shape Matching
Seong Ho Pahng, Guoye Guan, Benjamin Fefferman, Sahand Hormoz
Biological systems can form complex three-dimensional structures through the collective behavior of agents that share a common update rule and operate without central control. How such distributed control gives rise to precise global patterns remains a central question not only in developmental biology but also in distributed robotics, programmable matter, and multi-agent learning. Here, we introduce DiffeoMorph, an end-to-end differentiable framework for learning a morphogenesis protocol that guides a population of agents to morph into a target 3D shape. Each agent updates its position and internal state using an SE(3)-equivariant graph neural network, based on its own internal state and signals received from other agents. To train this system, we introduce a new shape-matching loss based on 3D Zernike polynomials, which compares the predicted and target shapes as continuous spatial distributions, not as discrete point clouds, and is invariant to agent ordering, number of agents, and global orientation. To achieve rotation invariance while preserving reflection sensitivity, we include an alignment step that optimally rotates the predicted Zernike spectrum to match the target before computing the loss. We perform benchmarking to establish the advantages of our shape-matching loss over other standard distance metrics for shape comparison tasks. We then demonstrate that DiffeoMorph can form a range of complex shapes from minimally patterned initial conditions. DiffeoMorph provides a general framework for learning distributed control strategies for morphogenesis, swarm robotics, and programmable self-assembly.
♻ ☆ Post-transcriptional Regulation of Stochastic Gene Expression Conditioned on Large Deviations
Kenny Wong, Thomas Mourier, Cameron Hopkinson
Gene expression is a stochastic process that gives rise to large fluctuations in protein levels leading to phenotypic heterogeneity in clonal cell populations; post-transcriptional regulation plays a crucial role in controlling the level of phenotypic variability within a population, which is directly tied to cell-fate decisions. As such, substantial efforts have been directed towards quantitatively modeling the effects of various post-transcriptional mechanisms on the strength of fluctuations in protein levels (noise). However, the corresponding effects of post-transcriptional regulation on the occurrence of rare events corresponding to large deviations are far less explored and have only been considered for a special model. Here, we take a general model of post-transcriptional regulation and apply the partitioning of Poisson arrivals (PPA) framework to map it onto a model that resembles promoter-based regulation of transcription, leading to a general framework to obtain objects of interest in large deviations (i.e. large deviation rate function for quantifying the likelihood of observing rare protein production rates and the corresponding driven process that characterizes the system dynamics conditional on the rare event) for models of post-transcriptional regulation directly from prior results for promoter-based models. The results derived create new avenues to analyze rare events in general models of post-transcriptional regulation pertaining to various different biological settings.

☆ HyPLM Technical Report
Haowei Lin, Cai Zhou, Xiangxin Zhou
Protein language models (PLMs) are standard tools for variant-effect prediction, protein annotation and design, but the compute needed to train and serve them keeps growing. We present HyPLM-Flash, an efficient and open PLM trained by Hyra 2.0, the research agent developed by our team. HyPLM-Flash is a 650-million-parameter masked language model trained on 726 billion tokens, 0.73 times the data of ESM-2 650M and a fifth to an eighth of the data of E1-600M and ESM C 600M. It runs on an inference engine with fused kernels and INT8 matrix multiplication, embedding about 130,000 residues per second on a single NVIDIA H20 GPU, 1.9 to 2.7 times faster than other 600M-class encoders. In a matched evaluation against 28 public checkpoints on 32 tasks, HyPLM-Flash reaches a ProteinGym zero-shot Spearman correlation of 0.409, statistically tied with ESM-2 650M, ESM C 600M and E1-600M, and the highest organismal-fitness score among self-supervised single-sequence models. It gives the best long-range contact precision of all models evaluated, including ESM-2 15B and ESM C 6B, and a lower masked-residue loss than ESM-2 650M, ESM C 600M and E1-600M on held-out sequences and on recently released PDB chains. Matching state-of-the-art accuracy at a fraction of the training and inference cost, HyPLM-Flash sets a new Pareto frontier for protein language models. We have released the model weights and the inference engine at https://github.com/Tencent-Hunyuan/hyra-results/tree/main.
☆ HyInv Technical Report
Haowei Lin, Cai Zhou, Xiangxin Zhou
Inverse folding designs amino-acid sequences for a given protein backbone, and screening needs many candidates that are both diverse and well folded. We present HyInv, a 1.96-million-parameter backbone-conditioned graph network with an encoder-decoder architecture, trained by Hyra 2.0, the research agent developed by our team: pretrained with random-order autoregressive sequence modeling, followed by structure-reward reinforcement learning. At inference, a grouped draft-and-rewrite sampler that uses separate draft and rewrite temperatures improves refolding TM-score at matched diversity. An optimized implementation with BF16 numerics designs 128 sequences for a 402-residue backbone in 50.6 ms on a single NVIDIA H20 GPU, about 52 times faster than the reference sampler, and an exact FP32 path that returns the reference sequences is 8.7 times faster. Across five benchmark datasets and seven dataset-predictor combinations, HyInv reaches a higher average scTM than all ten external baselines at comparable or higher sequence diversity, with large margins over ProteinMPNN and ProteinZero V2. The same default setting supports de novo backbones, pocket-constrained libraries, enzyme redesign with the catalytic site kept, and loop redesign. We have released our code and model at https://github.com/Tencent-Hunyuan/Hyra-results/tree/main/AI4Science/hyinv.
☆ OriDynamics: Towards a World Model for Biomolecular Dynamics
Jiahao Yang, Dong Liu, Hongliang Li, Ji Chen, Yuehua Liu, Daoyun Ding, Duo An, Jun Li, Jiahua Rao, Ziqiao Zhang, Peicong Lin, Shuangjia Zheng
Biomolecular recognition and regulation depend on atomic interactions that change as molecular partners fluctuate and adopt alternative conformations. Modeling these processes requires ensembles that capture both conformational diversity and coordinated atomic fluctuations between interacting molecules. Here we present OriDynamics, a unified framework for all-atom ensemble generation across protein monomers and complexes with small molecules or protein partners. OriDynamics introduces a latent ensemble decomposition that couples broad conformational exploration in a compact structural latent space with local all-atom sampling around generated proposals. Ensemble-level supervision constrains mean positions, fluctuation amplitudes and joint coordinate covariance to capture coordinated atomic variability within and between molecular partners. Across benchmarks of proteins and their complexes, OriDynamics demonstrates superior overall performance in conformational coverage, atomic fluctuation fidelity and interaction geometry compared with leading baselines. Application studies further demonstrate recovery of alternative functional conformations and partner-dependent changes in conformational ensembles alongside sampling of configurations associated with ligand dissociation. These capabilities provide a foundation for a biomolecular world model that represents accessible molecular states and their dependence on interaction context.

☆ ProTEUS: A mutation-resolved world model for closed-loop protein evolution
Yikun Zhang, Minghao Guo, Guangxue Xu, Peter Yichen Chen, Wojciech Matusik, Wengong Jin, Jingjing Chai, Kaiyi Jiang
Protein engineering requires navigating rugged fitness landscapes, where small sequence changes can produce large functional differences, but experimental screening can only sample a small fraction of sequence space. Thus, a central challenge is to efficiently traverse the large combinatorial space of mutations in silico. We introduce ProTEUS, a mutation-resolved world model for closed-loop protein evolution. Pretrained on deep mutational scanning assays, ProTEUS learns a joint representation of sequence, evolutionary, and structural information using MutationFormer, a mutation-biased attention architecture. Variants are represented as states in this joint latent space, and mutations define actions whose functional consequences are predicted. To support exploration of many mutants, we developed a fast, physics-constrained all-atom predictor that utilizes explicit geometric constraint enforcement to achieve >10 fold increase in speed than AlphaFold3 with guaranteed physical validity. Lastly, ProTEUS refines its prediction and understanding of protein activity through closed loop experimental feedback to guide evolution toward a desired phenotype. ProTEUS surpasses established models in both zero-shot and supervised fine-tuning mutation prediction tasks. We experimentally demonstrate its utility in two closed-loop multi-objective engineering campaigns on aldehyde dehydrogenase and levansucrase, yielding variants with >10 fold higher activity than wild type. ProTEUS provides a world-model framework that connects virtual evaluation of mutational outcomes with experimental learning for efficient protein evolution.

♻ ☆ Co-folding model guided by structural proteomics
Alon Shtrikman, Sagie Brodsky, Michal Ran Shchory, Nitzan Simchi, Yaron Ben Shoshan-Galeczki, Eran Seger, Kirill Pevzner
Proximity-inducing drugs, including PROTACs, represent a promising therapeutic modality. Their activity is driven by the geometry of the assembled ternary complex, which determines whether the target is ubiquitinated. Co-folding models perform poorly in these cases due to scarcity of training data and the dynamic nature of such complexes. Structural proteomics can supply the missing geometric information and steer the model towards an accurate prediction. However, current models accept only positive signals, while a differential structural mass-spectrometry experiment also reports negative signals. We introduce AIMS-Fold, an inference-time guidance layer built on Boltz-2 that requires no retraining and accepts repulsive constraints alongside attractive ones. We tested AIMS-Fold on 28 PROTAC, glue and antibody complexes that Boltz-2 fails to predict. From only five generated models, guidance recovers the correct conformation in 64% of cases. The gap does not close with scale: across 100 models and 20 seeds, unguided Boltz-2 reaches 14% while AIMS-Fold reaches 86%. Repulsive constraints alone recover the correct conformation on some systems, occasionally exceeding the matched attractive constraints. Taken together, these results demonstrate the strength of utilizing the full structural proteomics signal, positive and negative, to steer generative models toward the correct conformation, addressing a central problem in rational degrader design.
QM 13
☆ AI-based characterization of lymphoid structures and the tumor microenvironment reveals lower mature tertiary lymphoid structures, lymph-node follicles, and plasma cells after neoadjuvant therapy in lung squamous cell carcinoma
Arne Schmidt, Justin Nieder, Cornelius Boehm, Julika Ribbat-Idel, Marius Teodorescu, Marie-Lisa Eich, Simon Schallenberg, Gabriel Dernbach, Katja Lingelbach, Miriam Haegele, Timo Milbich, Adam Narai, Maria Stoelben, Hans Pinckaers, Lukas Ruff, Kai Standvoss, Christian Watermann, Christiane Kuempers, Sven Perner, Verena Sailer, Jasmin Tiebach, Torsten Goldmann, Sebastian Marwitz, Johannes Braegelmann, Frederick Klauschen, Marija Pezer, Rosemarie Krupar
Mature, germinal centre-bearing tertiary lymphoid structures (TLS) and mature lymph node follicles (secondary lymphoid structures, SLS) sustain the plasma cell response that shapes outcome and immunotherapy benefit in lung cancer. How this loco-regional axis differs between neoadjuvant-treated and therapy-naive tumors is poorly defined. We analyzed H&E whole-slide images of resected tumors and paired proximal and distal lymph nodes from matched neoadjuvant-treated (neoLUSC, n=20) and therapy-naive (naivLUSC, n=20) lung squamous cell carcinoma (LUSC) patients. Aignostics Atlas H&E Tissue Analysis (HETA) models quantified cell densities per tissue compartment and cell-cell co-localization; a dedicated deep-learning model validated against pathologist annotations detected TLS and SLS and graded their maturity. Prognostic value was tested in an independent cohort (n=458; 46 treated). In neoLUSC tumors, stromal plasma cell density was 72% (p=0.004) and stromal lymphocyte density 48% (p=0.015) lower than in naivLUSC; macrophages and granulocytes did not differ significantly. Total and immature TLS did not differ, whereas mature TLS in the tumor (p=0.034) and mature SLS in the tumor-distal lymph node (p=0.003) were lower in neoLUSC patients. Peri-TLS plasma cell density correlated with tumor lymphocyte density in naivLUSC (rho=0.54, p=0.017) but not in neoLUSC tumors (rho=0.07). In the validation cohort, stromal plasma cell density (HR 0.69, p=0.011) and lymphocyte-plasma cell co-localization (HR 0.66, p=0.004) were associated with longer survival, and neoadjuvant status with shorter survival (HR 1.50, p=0.026). Neoadjuvant therapy is thus associated with fewer mature lymphoid structures and weaker coupling of the humoral compartment to the tumor rather than uniformly lower immune readouts. The stromal plasma cell/co-localization axis is a candidate prognostic biomarker.
☆ stMIND: Learning multiscale interaction dynamics from spatial-temporal transcriptomics
Longquan Li, Lin Wan
Interactions within local cellular neighborhoods and across broader tissue scales jointly govern molecular state transitions and morphogenesis during development and regeneration. While time-resolved spatial transcriptomics simultaneously profiles gene expression and spatial coordinates, destructive sequencing samples distinct cells at each time point, leaving intermediate trajectories and multiscale intercellular interactions unobserved. Building upon our interacting mean-field neural stochastic differential equation framework, we present stMIND (Spatiotemporal Multiscale Interaction Neural Dynamics), a continuous-time generative framework that infers coupled molecular, spatial, and proliferation dynamics from unmatched spatiotemporal snapshots. stMIND decomposes cellular expression velocity vector/drift into three interpretable operators: a cell-intrinsic program, a local graph-difference attention operator that aggregates relative expression differences and spatial geometry within a co-evolving physical radius, and a nonlocal mean-field attention operator that captures long-range population context beyond the immediate neighborhood. By evolving dynamic spatial neighborhoods alongside cellular migration and enforcing orthogonality between intrinsic and interaction-driven drifts, stMIND resolves scale-specific intercellular influences through optimal transport in joint expression-position space. In axolotl and zebrafish benchmarks, stMIND leads in expression prediction, anatomical consistency and spatial coherence. Ablations confirm the need to combine disjoint local and nonlocal operators. It reveals a regenerative transition in axolotl telencephalon 10 to 15 days after injury, with a drift energy surge followed by nonlocal dominance and local counterbalance varying by cell type in reactive ependymoglial niches. It tracks developmental vector fields across zebrafish stages.
☆ BioMARS 2.0: a robotic biologist for formulating and adapting biological experiments
Yibo Qiu, Zan Huang, Yilin Qiao, Ziyang Ling, Yi Wang, Zhengyang Lu, Hangke Peng, Haoliang Ye, Ao Tian, Shu'ang Sun, Handi Liu, Yuqing Long, Mohan Yang, Kan Ding, Zhenzhen Zhou, Di Wu, Yu Xiong, Yaxuan Han, Jun'ao Song, Haoran Zhang, Ronald X Xu, Mingzhai Sun
Biological experimentation is not reducible to a single optimization problem, but requires decisions at multiple levels of abstraction: translating scientific knowledge into experimental procedures, adapting culture conditions to measured phenotypes and selecting interventions that test biological hypotheses. Automating experimental execution does not, by itself, determine which experiments should be performed next or how they should change in response to biological feedback. Here we introduce BioMARS, a human-in-the-loop multi-agent robotic system that connects knowledge-guided experimental decision-making with robotic execution and biological feedback. At the procedural level, BioMARS generated literature-grounded protocols and traceable action plans, improving expert-reviewed protocol quality scores across four model backends. At the parametric level, BioMARS used image-derived phenotypes from robotic retinal organoid culture to select subsequent experimental conditions, with higher reported scores in later rounds of the three-round experiment. At the intervention level, BioMARS nominated drug-repurposing candidates from biomedical evidence and advanced six candidates to wet-lab testing by researchers. Together, these studies demonstrate feedback-driven robotic biological experimentation within a framework that supports decisions about experimental procedures, culture conditions and biological interventions.
☆ Reproducible quantum clustering for single-cell transcriptome atlases
Henry Han
Current single-cell clustering pipelines discard most genes and can yield inconsistent clusters across random seeds. We introduce KQE-2, a training-free hybrid quantum-classical framework for reproducible whole-transcriptome clustering. KQE-2 maps cells into entanglement-free quantum states, enabling exact feature evaluation on classical hardware and direct checks on a quantum processor. Theoretically, we prove that removing entanglement is necessary and sufficient to bound single-qubit feature variance locally. Practically, while retraining scVI shifted 14% of cells and altered rare-type markers and drug targets, KQE-2 delivered identical biology across runs. On standard highly variable genes, the quantum encoding alone raised accuracy on a developing cortex from 0.61 to 0.79. KQE-2 scales to 11.4 million nuclei on one server, outperforming the compared methods on eight of ten complete atlases (and within the best method's run-to-run range on two), and leads on six even under standard downstream clustering.

☆ Pulsatile Endocrine Regulation with a Releasable Hormone Pool: Impulsive Feedback Model
Anton V. Proskurnikov, Alexander Medvedev
An impulsive model of endocrine regulation equipped with frequency- and amplitude-modulated pulsatile feedback is extended to study the dynamical effects pertaining to limitations due to the releasable hormone pool. The model describes a three-hormone regulatory loop and is applicable to endocrine systems such as the reproductive, stress, and growth hormone axes. Conditions guaranteeing the existence and uniqueness of a periodic solution with a single feedback-triggered impulse per period (a 1-cycle) are derived, together with monotonicity properties of its characteristics. An explicit Jacobian formula is obtained to assess local orbital stability. Model analysis demonstrates that a reduced hormone-pool replenishment rate produces a lower-amplitude, higher-frequency 1-cycle. ☆ EvoSurv: Evolution-Aware Multimodal Survival Modeling Reveals Complementary Prognostic Signals
Wei Wang, Po-Yu Liang, Jun Bai
Accurate cancer survival prediction remains challenging due to tumor heterogeneity. Although multimodal survival models integrate clinical and molecular data, they often overlook evolutionary information encoded in genomic alterations. We further developed EvoSurv, a multimodal survival framework integrating eTime with clinical variables and RNA expression. Overall, these findings demonstrate that eTime captures survival-associated evolutionary information and highlight its potential to provide prognostic value beyond clinical and transcriptomic predictors.

♻ ☆ Toward Controlling Biology with Language: Offline Learning of Prompt-Conditioned Interventions for Cells, Organoids, and Biobots
Nam H. Le, Douglas Blackiston, Michael Levin, Josh Bongard
Natural language has become an interface to code, images and molecules, but not to living matter: a stimulus applied to cells has no fixed linguistic meaning, and every paired example of an instruction and its outcome costs a wet-lab experiment. We ask how far an existing archive of experiments can take this interface, using xenobots, motile multicellular constructs made of frog cells, stimulated by a single electrical pulse of varying duration. We re-track 155 archived recordings with two independent methods, measure what the pulse does from each bot's own behaviour before and after it, and separate behaviours the pulse controls from behaviours it does not. Longer pulses stop bots and short pulses leave them moving, sometimes faster; turning direction and starting from rest do not depend on duration, and restarting at most weakly. We then replace hand-made behaviour labels with a blind language critic: fresh sessions of a multimodal language model read plots of each bot and score how well its behaviour matches each of 176 instructions. The critic agrees with the measured labels whenever its plots contain the information the labels are defined on, and dose curves learned from its scores match those learned from the measurements. A prompt-to-intervention model trained only on critic scores maps unseen instructions, including negations, to the right end of the dose range on unseen bots, as well as the same model trained on measured labels. The archive therefore supports a calibrated, refusing language interface for the three behaviours a pulse can set, and identifies, rather than hides, the behaviours it cannot.

♻ ☆ PMO-Dock: Benchmarking Docking, Specificity, and Generalization in Molecular Optimization
Gor Simonyan, Tatevik Abrahamyan, Narek Abelyan, Tigran Fahradyan, Hrant Khachatrian
The Practical Molecular Optimization (PMO) benchmark standardized evaluation in molecular optimization, but it is built on simple property-based oracles that do not capture structure-based drug design. As the field has moved to docking-based objectives, evaluation practice has become unstandardized, and different methods report results on different docking tasks, often without a shared protocol for fair comparison or sample-efficiency constraints. To address this, we introduce Practical Molecular Optimization for Docking (PMO-Dock), a benchmark and protocol for docking-based optimization consisting of 25 tasks covering hit generation, lead optimization, and a new specificity task requiring strong on-target binding while penalizing off-target interactions. The protocol separates model development from final evaluation: validation tasks are used for hyperparameter tuning, and test tasks are held out for final evaluation and are not used during development. Evaluation on the test tasks is done under a strict oracle budget to reflect realistic drug-discovery settings. We benchmark four diverse high-performing methods spanning different optimization paradigms, Saturn (reinforcement learning), GenMol (discrete diffusion), Genetic-guided GFlowNet, and ChemLlama (LLM-based evolutionary search). Our results show no universally best method across tasks, additional analyses of hyperparameter transfer (cross-task and cross-category) and sensitivity show that transfer is often limited and varies by method, motivating method-dependent selection strategies. This benchmark provides a concrete standard for evaluating generalization to unseen tasks and sample efficiency under a limited test-time oracle budget.
♻ ☆ Trajectory Inference of Human Aging from Cross-Sectional DNA Methylation Data
Chandan Gupta, Syed Haider, Pietro Liò
DNA methylation (DNAm) serves as one of the most robust molecular biomarkers of biological aging. While conventional epigenetic clocks accurately predict chronological age from high-dimensional CpG profiles, they treat aging as a static regression task, meaning they can only output a single score rather than simulating how an entire profile continuously changes over time. To reconstruct these continuous dynamics, we frame lifelong human epigenetic aging as a trajectory inference problem across discrete age snapshots derived from widely available cross-sectional data. We introduce a two-stage computational pipeline: first, an age-regularized Variational Autoencoder (VAE) maps high-dimensional CpG profiles onto a chronologically ordered latent manifold while preserving a generative decoder bridge back to the original methylation space. Second, we model the continuous movement across this latent space via Regularized Unbalanced Optimal Transport (RUOT) that unifies deterministic drift, random diffusion, and non-conservative mass changes. By resolving this RUOT formulation using the DeepRUOT framework, our model fluidly accommodates population-level density shifts like survivorship bias and cellular attrition without requiring rigid biological priors. Evaluated on a large-scale, 80-year pan-tissue dataset, our model demonstrates robust distribution interpolation and uncovers a prominent late-life surge in the learned growth field that mathematically captures the variance expansion driven by stochastic epigenetic drift. Finally, by decoding continuous latent paths back to individual CpG sites, we reconstruct and empirically verify distinct biological aging archetypes, offering a rigorous, generative paradigm for simulating human molecular aging.

♻ ☆ KineCouple: Substrate-conditioned residual readout for enzyme kinetic prediction
Chun Kang, Yunbo Zhang, Fanhu Zeng, Jian Liu, Siwei Deng, Lei Sha
Accurate prediction of enzyme kinetic parameters can support enzyme engineering and metabolic modeling by complementing experimental characterization. Existing prediction methods often pool residue-level protein features before combining them with substrate features. Such substrate-independent pooling cannot adapt residue weighting to the substrate during aggregation, potentially diluting information relevant to a specific enzyme-substrate pair. Since the contributions of individual residues to catalysis can depend on the substrate, substrate-conditioned aggregation offers a way to emphasize residue features relevant to the prediction. We introduce KineCouple, which uses substrate queries and residue-level keys and values in cross-attention, allowing the substrate to guide residue selection before aggregation. The model preserves the global protein representation and adds a gated correction defined as the difference between the conditioned readout and a uniform readout. ProtT5 and MolT5 remain frozen, and separate models predict kcat and Km on a logarithmic scale. In pooled out-of-fold predictions on the CataPro benchmarks, KineCouple reduces kcat RMSE from 1.333 to 1.330 and increases PCC from 0.492 to 0.496 relative to reproduced CataPro. For Km, RMSE decreases from 0.998 to 0.997 and PCC increases from 0.633 to 0.634. Spearman correlation also improves for both tasks.
♻ ☆ Surf_2_Volume: A Python Toolbox for Cortical Ribbon Based CIFTI to NIfTI Conversion
Shuguang Yang, Ziyi Wang, Yujing Shen, Zhengye Wang, Junyi Li, Yujing Nie, Feizhen Cao, Suiping Wang
Many neuroimaging workflows require NIfTI label volumes, whereas cortical parcellations are increasingly distributed in CIFTI surface space. Existing conversion tools offer several mapping settings. In nearest vertex mapping, a small distance can leave cortical voxels unlabeled, whereas a larger distance can extend labels beyond the intended cortical region. Choosing among settings is difficult when no paired volume atlas is available. We present Surf_2_Volume, a Python toolbox that projects discrete cortical labels within an adjustable cortical ribbon. Users can specify the ribbon threshold directly or use an optional procedure that ranks candidate thresholds using information from the source atlas. This procedure ranks 12 thresholds by differences between each label's proportion of source cortical vertices and its proportion of output voxels, without using a published volume atlas for selection. We evaluated four configurations from two atlas families: Yeo2011 parcellations with 7 and 17 networks, represented by 51 and 114 spatial components, and Schaefer2018 100-parcel atlases with 7 and 17 network assignments. All four selected sets included threshold 0.05, which had the highest macro-Dice and macro-Jaccard among the tested package thresholds. Eleven of the 12 selected outputs had higher macro-Dice than the best tested Connectome Workbench mapping for their atlas. In 24 HCP participants, all 12 selected outputs had higher participant-level mean parcel time-series concordance than every tested Workbench mapping (all within-atlas FDR-adjusted p < 0.001). Surf_2_Volume provides a reproducible way to generate and select volume atlas candidates when only a surface parcellation is available, while retaining alternatives for different coverage and signal-extraction needs.
♻ ☆ Conservative Continuous-Time Treatment Optimization NeurIPS 2026
Nora Schneider, Georg Manten, Niki Kilbertus
Finding optimal treatment plans in continuous time is crucial for personalized medicine. Existing methods, however, do not explicitly optimize treatment plans and instead focus solely on predicting treatment effects. Naive optimization over these treatment-response models can exploit model errors, resulting in plans that appear optimal under the learned model but perform poorly in practice. To mitigate model exploitation, we propose SigCTO (Signature-based Conservative Treatment Optimization), a conservative framework for continuous-time treatment optimization. We derive a path-space regularizer based on signature-kernel maximum mean discrepancy that penalizes treatment plans for which predicted and true responses differ. Under suitable assumptions, the regularizer allows for consistent estimation, and the regularized objective upper bounds the true treatment cost. Experiments on pharmacometric simulators show that SigCTO finds treatment plans with lower ground-truth costs than state-of-the-art baselines. On real-world ICU data, SigCTO predicts treatment cost more accurately than baselines, and the regularizer detects prediction errors.

♻ ☆ Better Protein Function Prediction by Modeling Survivorship Bias
Zhongmou Chao, Poompol Buathong, Ekaterina Selivanovitch, Susan Daniel, Peter I. Frazier
Predicting whether a protein sequence possesses a biochemical property of interest is important for understanding and designing protein functionality. Modern protein sequence databases provide valuable training data for functional prediction, but they exhibit survivorship bias: natural selection favors variants that support organismal survival while eliminating many others before they can be observed, complicating classifier training. This setting motivates positive-unlabeled (PU) learning, which treats observed functional sequences as positive and unobserved sequences as unlabeled rather than nonfunctional. However, existing protein PU methods do not account for cross-sequence heterogeneity in evolutionary accessibility: sequences one nucleotide mutation away from a common variant arise far more readily than distant ones. We introduce PULSE: PU Learning with Surveillance and Evolution, a framework that trains a probabilistic classifier for a target protein function using an evolution-aware observation likelihood. When considering functionality of proteins with high genomic sequence coverage, PULSE combines a probabilistic classifier that estimates whether an amino acid sequence performs the target function with an evolutionary model that estimates the likelihood that its encoding nucleotide sequences emerge and are detected based on mutational accessibility, parental prevalence, and surveillance e!ciency. By coupling these two components, PULSE treats the absence of an evolutionarily accessible sequence as stronger evidence of non-functionality than the absence of one unlikely to arise. Tested on eleven tasks focusing on multiple Spike protein properties spanning five SARS-CoV-2 variants, PULSE ranks first or second on all of them, and achieves the best overall performance among all methods, including standard PU learning, one-class classification methods, and protein language models.

☆ AOP-Smart-Finder: A High-Throughput Human-AI Hybrid Framework for AOP Discovery and Evidence Mapping from Large-Scale Literature
Qinjiang Niu, Lu Yan
The rapid growth of scientific literature and the high heterogeneity of biological terminology and mechanistic descriptions make it difficult for researchers to integrate scattered research evidence, identify connections among biological events, and construct mechanistic networks. Here, we developed a high-throughput human-AI hybrid framework, AOP-Smart-Finder, for biological event discovery, relationship mining, and Adverse Outcome Pathway (AOP) construction and evidence mapping from large-scale literature. This framework integrates Large Language Models (LLMs), AOP-Smart retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) technology, and Python-based automation, supporting two analysis modes: one based on a single compound and the other based on a predefined set of biological events. This framework can either use existing AOPs as knowledge guidance for mechanistic expansion and evidence supplementation, or discover novel event connections and construct mechanistic networks around user-specified research topics. ☆ Agents Propose, Code Verifies, Humans Decide: Verified Agentic Ingestion of Heterogeneous Scientific Tables into Relational Databases
Bharatha Rankothge, Suzanne Hodgkins, Virginia Rich, Michael Kirby
Public scientific datasets rarely arrive analysis-ready. They arrive as messy folders of wide matrices, long tables, spreadsheets, and per-cohort files whose keys and encodings differ, and every reanalysis must reconnect them first. A coding agent can automate this, but its mistakes can look plausible, and without an audit trail, a mistake that corrupts data can go uncaught. We present an AI-assisted ingestion pipeline built on one rule: LLM agents propose, deterministic code verifies, and humans decide. Narrow agents see compact, citable evidence about the files, never the data, and return typed proposals. A verifier with 39 check types tests each proposal against that evidence in two loops, one for the schema's structure and one for its meaning, and a human reviewer approves the schema and the load. Code compiles PostgreSQL DDL, reshapes the data, quarantines rows it cannot place, records provenance, and reconciles every staged row against the database. On four public biomedical datasets, one held out from development, 9 of 20 first proposals failed a check and every failure was caught: the loops repaired eight, and a one-line reviewer edit fixed the ninth. All 45.1 million values loaded with zero reconciliation gap. Discovering a dataset's structure took 4-7 LLM calls and preparing its data at most one more, costing under $0.50 per dataset. In a separate test, each of nine planted data errors was quarantined with a reason or recorded in an audit log.

☆ Symmetry-Induced Conservation Laws, Three-Channel Attribution, and Solid-Phase Recycling in Prebiotic Enantiomeric Excess Amplification
Zeyu Jia, Anlin Gong, Bingmei Wu
Enantiomeric excess ($\mathrm{ee}$) is the primary observable used to quantify chiral amplification across prebiotic chemistry, asymmetric autocatalysis, and multiphase crystallization. Because $\mathrm{ee} = Δ/ M$ is the ratio of net chiral excess $Δ= L - D$ to total chiral mass $M = L + D$, identical increases in $\mathrm{ee}$ can arise either by generating net chiral excess through parity-breaking reactions or by depleting total chiral mass through symmetric phase or oligomer partitioning. Here we establish a pathwise conservation law and a model-free three-channel attribution identity for $\mathbb{Z}_2$-symmetric reaction networks. In any closed network without autocatalytic feedback or epimerization, net chiral excess is conserved along every stochastic trajectory ($Δ(t) = Δ(0)$), locking liquid-phase amplification to cumulative mass loss ($\mathrm{ee}_f = \mathrm{ee}_0 M_0 / M_f$). Re-analyzing seven experimental systems shows that standalone phase removal (Channel R) loses $91.7\%$--$98.9\%$ of chiral solute into racemic sediment in closed batches and washes out under open racemic inflow. Coupling eutectic partitioning (Channel R) with solid/solution racemization recycling (Channel B) converts the precipitated solid into homochiral product ($96.5\%$ recoverable homochiral crystal mass at $96.5\%$ total and $100.0\%$ solid $\mathrm{ee}$), whereas coupling evaporative concentration with liquid-phase autocatalysis crosses the open-flow bifurcation threshold against continuous racemic dilution ($81.9\%$ homochiral yield at $92.9\%$ active chiral $\mathrm{ee}$). We further establish non-crossing parity invariants for dual-monomer networks, derive a closed-form wet--dry amplification law, and resolve a one-dimensional identifiability ridge in chiral mineral adsorption via antipodal crystal-pair protocols.

☆ Efficient and Accurate Zero Shot Generation of Symmetric Protein Complexes NeurIPS 2026
Rory Gao, Yuanzhou Chen, Prajit Rajkumar, Wei Wang
Symmetric organization is a fundamental principle of biomolecular complex assembly. The same principle underlies engineered applications, from vaccine platforms to nanomaterials, making symmetric de novo generation a core capability for computational protein design. We establish a theoretical foundation equating generation of an assembly with underlying symmetry group $G$ to generation within a quotient space over $G$. This directly allows $\mathrm{SE}(3)$-equivariant models to generate symmetric structures with minimal distribution shift. With this insight we introduce ZeUS ($\textbf{Ze}$ro-shot $\textbf{U}$nified $\textbf{S}$ymmetrization), a framework that converts any pretrained $\mathrm{SE}(3)$-equivariant backbone generative model into an efficient generator of symmetric assemblies with no retraining. ZeUS operates on a single $\textit{canonical subunit}$ and utilizes $\textit{phantom subunits}$ to preserve internal model operations, reducing memory and runtime cost by $\mathcal{O}(|G|)$ for finite groups $G$, compared to a full-complex forward pass. We implement ZeUS for IPA-based models (FrameDiff, FoldFlow) and axial-attention-based RFdiffusion, showing high generation efficiency with minimal degradation to generation quality. We then apply ZeUS to generate symmetric complexes of over $10{,}000$ residues with exact, untruncated attention, a scale previously intractable with any model.

♻ ☆ LEMON-ZEST: Evolution-Informed Tokenization for Efficient Protein Language Modeling NeurIPS 2026
Biswajit Banerjee, Claudia Alvarez Carreno, Anton S. Petrov
Protein Language Models (PLMs) have made remarkable progress following scaling laws established in natural language processing across sequence- and structure-based tasks, yet the potential of tokenization remains underexploited. Unlike human language, proteins preserve structure despite extensive sequence variation, a property standard tokenization strategies fundamentally fail to capture. We introduce ZEST (Zoned Encoding of Sequence Traits), an evolution-informed vocabulary derived from conserved regions of multiple sequence alignments. ZEST allows embedding domain-level biological priors directly at the tokenization stage rather than learning them implicitly through scale. ZEST natively compresses sequences to an average token length of 3.3 residues, enabling our model to process $\geq3380$ residues within a standard 1024-token context window. Building on this, we present LEMON (Layered Extraction of Molecular Ordering from Nature), a compact 200M-parameter sequence-based model for detection of remote homology between protein sequences trained on a single H100 GPU for one week. Despite its modest size, LEMON outperforms models of 600M to 3B parameters and is on-par with TM-Vec. Our results demonstrate that evolution-informed tokenization can substitute for massive parameter scaling, opening a new direction for efficient, biologically-grounded protein representation learning. All code and model weights are publicly available (at \href{https://github.com/LDWLab/ZEST}{github.com/LDWLab/ZEST}) under the MIT license.

☆ Causal Outcome Learning with Noisy Proxies
Eric V. Strobl
Modern observational biomedical datasets contain increasingly extensive baseline and outcome measurements, yet detecting a clinically meaningful treatment effect remains difficult because investigators must identify an outcome that captures treatment response while adjusting for confounding distributed across numerous noisy baseline variables. We introduce SCOUT (Score-based Causal Outcome Learning Using Treatment signals), a framework that learns a clinically interpretable, treatment-responsive outcome while adjusting for confounding throughout the learning process. SCOUT constructs low-dimensional adjustment representations of high-dimensional baseline measurements to estimate the average treatment effect (ATE) and conditional average treatment effect (CATE) for the learned outcome. Importantly, the final adjustment space is at most two-dimensional, allowing treatment-effect heterogeneity to be estimated in this space rather than in the full high-dimensional covariate space. SCOUT also introduces a fourth-order statistic for testing treatment-effect heterogeneity that emphasizes unusually large deviations from the ATE. In synthetic and semi-synthetic experiments, the algorithm achieved competitive ATE estimation accuracy and substantially lower CATE estimation error. In an observational study of patients with bipolar disorder, SCOUT identified a clinically recognizable lithium-associated adverse-effect profile, detecting both a significant ATE (Holm-adjusted $p$ = 0.0026) and significant treatment-effect heterogeneity (Holm-adjusted $p$ = 0.033).These results demonstrate how jointly learning outcomes and controlling for confounding can facilitate the discovery of treatment effects in high-dimensional observational data.

☆ Learning Biological Hierarchies in Single-Cell Foundation Models NeurIPS 2026

♻ ☆ Stand-In Effects and Pooling Amplification: Lessons from Biological World Models for Testing Action Effects
Bowen Xu, Boyu Chen, Jiaying Li
Whether an action-conditioned world model uses actions is usually checked by replacing or removing them and measuring lost accuracy. With biological experiments as the guiding case, we show that such diagnostics report action effects where there are none: under autocorrelated designs, future actions stand in for history the model does not see, and a better model learns the stand-in better. When the design law is known, a conditional randomization test written as an e-value is valid in finite samples whatever the model, provided the resampler conditions on the full state the design used; resampling from a simpler law reproduces the false effect. A second failure is specific to world models: when many sites share one action sequence (cells in a microscope field, pixels in a frame), the pooled score turns small model errors along the action contrast into overwhelming evidence against a true effect. In a pixel world, its median log e-value fell from $-17.3$ to $-6832$ as frames grew from 64 to 4096 pixels, while the oracle's rose. Our preregistered analysis of optogenetic ERK experiments failed this way; a tempered statistic, selected on synthetic data and registered before the data were obtained, then rejected the null on a never-opened field ($E\approx1.1\times10^{10}$). Tempering factors estimated from residuals can err by an order of magnitude, whereas a mixture over a fixed grid of factors needs no estimate and loses at most the logarithm of the grid size in log e-value relative to its best member. In a registered test on a diffusion world model for three Atari games released by others, the pooled test rejected in 0 of 96 units in which actions mattered and Kish and contrast tempering in 0 and 1, while the mixture rejected in 96; under an exact null, eight statistics made 0 rejections in 1,536 tests. Every analysis is preregistered, fixed in a dated protocol, or labelled exploratory.

♻ ☆ INTENSE: Information-theoretic discovery of behavioral selectivity of neurons in calcium imaging data
Nikita Pospelov, Viktor Plusnin, Olga Rogozhnikova, Anna Ivanova, Vladimir Sotskov, Ksenia Toropova, Olga Ivashkina, Vladik Avetisov, Konstantin Anokhin
Neurons in freely behaving animals exhibit selectivity for diverse variables. Calcium imaging makes this selectivity hard to read out: behavioral variables covary, and fluorescence is slow and autocorrelated. We introduce INTENSE (INformation-Theoretic Evaluation of Neuronal SElectivity), an open-source framework that screens every neuron against all measured variables on a common statistical scale. INTENSE applies mutual information directly to fluorescence to detect relationships missed by linear correlation, optimizes neural-behavioral delays, performs autocorrelation-preserving circular-shift tests and examines whether apparent mixed selectivity reflects behavioral covariance. In ground-truth simulations, it reduced false associations caused by temporal structure and recovered more true associations than correlation-based screening. In CA1 recordings from 16 freely exploring mice, INTENSE identified neurons selective for position, head direction, object interaction and locomotor state. Accounting for correlated variables reclassified a large fraction of apparently mixed-selective neuron-session observations. INTENSE turns neural and behavioral recordings into testable maps of neuronal selectivity.
♻ ☆ Informing Acquisition Functions via Foundation Models for Molecular Discovery
Qi Chen, Fabio Ramos, Alán Aspuru-Guzik, Florian Shkurti
Bayesian Optimization (BO) is a key methodology for accelerating molecular discovery by estimating the mapping from molecules to their properties while seeking the optimal candidate. Typically, BO iteratively updates a probabilistic surrogate model of this mapping and optimizes acquisition functions derived from the model to guide molecule selection. However, its performance is limited in low-data regimes with insufficient prior knowledge and vast candidate spaces. Large language models (LLMs) and chemistry foundation models offer rich priors to enhance BO, but high-dimensional features, costly in-context learning, and the computational burden of deep Bayesian surrogates hinder their full utilization. To address these challenges, we propose a likelihood-free BO method that bypasses explicit surrogate modeling and directly leverages priors from general LLMs and chemistry-specific foundation models to inform acquisition functions. Our method also learns a tree-structured partition of the molecular search space with local acquisition functions, enabling efficient candidate selection via Monte Carlo Tree Search. By further incorporating coarse-grained LLM-based clustering, it substantially improves scalability to large candidate sets by restricting acquisition function evaluations to clusters with statistically higher property values. We show through extensive experiments and ablations that the proposed method substantially improves scalability, robustness, and sample efficiency in LLM-guided BO for molecular discovery.
♻ ☆ Chem-PerturBridge: a harmonized compendium of small molecule perturbation transcriptomic effects
Artur Szałata, Olga Novitskaia, Maiia Shulman, Matthew Mella, Altynbek Zhubanchaliyev, Fabian J. Theis
Large perturbation models require training data encompassing chemical, cellular, and assay diversity. Current transcriptomic resources for small-molecule modeling, however, are fragmented across technologies, metadata conventions, controls, doses, and preprocessing pipelines. We introduce Chem-PerturBridge, a harmonized multi-dataset resource comprising over 37k compounds, 136 cellular contexts, and 1.25M transcriptomic samples across eight assay types, with standardized identifiers, metadata, and replicate-aware condition-level effects. We use the resource to evaluate matched-condition agreement across datasets and replicate agreement within datasets. Matched same-compound conditions show weak absolute agreement in fine-grained $\mathrm{logFC}$ rankings and magnitudes across most dataset pairs. However, they generally exceed individual same-context different-compound signatures, while often falling below the smoothed centroid of such signatures. Same-compound retrieval is above chance in all pairs. $\mathrm{LogFC}$ direction agreement is high in absolute terms, and exceeds both baselines in most cases. We further evaluate Chem-PerturBridge as a pretraining resource for compound representation learning. Under a compound-held-out OP3 evaluation split, embeddings pretrained on Chem-PerturBridge improve over L1000-only embeddings, Morgan fingerprints, chemical language model embeddings, and the descriptor-free OP3 baseline across metrics. An extensive molecule-holdout evaluation across 11 datasets further shows that models trained on Chem-PerturBridge outperform or match those that are not. Chem-PerturBridge therefore supports both diagnostic evaluation of cross-dataset signature agreement and model-oriented reuse of heterogeneous perturbation transcriptomic data.

♻ ☆ The DynaCell Evaluation Suite for Dynamic 3D Virtual Staining of Live Cells NeurIPS 2026
Alexandr A. Kalinin, Dihan Zheng, Taylla Milena Theodoro, Ivan Ivanov, Eduardo Hirata-Miyasaki, See-Chi Lee, Aofei Liu, Sricharan Reddy Varra, Talon Chandler, Soorya Pradeep, Chad Liu, Manuel D. Leonetti, Carolina Arias, Bo Huang, Shalin B. Mehta
Understanding how cells respond to perturbations over time is central to cell biology and drug discovery. Live-cell imaging captures the dynamics of perturbation-induced phenotypes in individual cells. While fluorescence imaging can visualize specific molecular phenotypes, labeling and repeated illumination often affect cell health. Virtual staining offers an alternative by predicting these fluorescence images from images acquired without fluorescent labels. Although many virtual staining models have been developed, their performance on volumetric time-lapse data remains poorly characterized. We address this gap with an evaluation suite for 3D time-lapse virtual staining of live cells. The suite combines paired 3D time-lapse images acquired with and without fluorescent labels, a set of baseline models, and three metric tiers measuring pixel-level fidelity, organelle segmentation performance, and single-cell phenotype similarity. Using this evaluation suite, we study metric and model behavior across 3 cell types, 2 microscopes, 4 organelles, and 3 perturbation states. We find that regression baselines better predict the spatial localization of target organelles, whereas the generative baseline better captures the distribution of phenotypes across cells. These differences expose trade-offs between structural and phenotypic fidelity that are obscured by single-metric evaluation and suggest how virtual staining models can be developed for diverse downstream tasks such as localization, segmentation, tracking, and phenotypic profiling. We release the curated datasets, baseline checkpoints, and evaluation code as part of DynaCell, a broader framework for image-based profiling of cell-state dynamics.

☆ Protein B-factor modeling with local magnitude
Wanying Bi, Jian Liu, Hongsong Feng
Topological magnitude characterizes the effective size and spatial organization of metric spaces, offering a geometric approach with potential applications in molecular biology and materials science. Previous work has demonstrated its utility in modeling protein flexibility from structure, but global magnitude weighting requires dense linear systems whose computational cost increases with protein size. We introduce a local magnitude representation that retains residue-level structural information through nearest-neighbor environments. The construction is well posed and geometrically invariant, with central weighting distinguished from scalar neighborhood magnitude. Across 364 proteins, the mean within-protein fitting Pearson correlation coefficient (PCC) increases from 0.725 for global magnitude to 0.728 for local magnitude. Blind-prediction experiments on 345 proteins combine each representation with identical auxiliary descriptors and evaluate random forests and gradient boosting under three validation protocols. Local features improve the primary PCC in all six model--protocol comparisons, with a maximum absolute increase of 0.008 in protein-level ten-fold gradient boosting. For a fixed feature bank, local factorization costs $O(Nk^3)$ compared with $O(N^3)$ for global factorization, where $N$ is the number of C$_α$ atoms and $k$ the neighborhood size. Timing measurements show a computational advantage of local magnitude on large structures. Within the tested single-seed configuration and distinct global and local scale banks, these results support local magnitude as a compact representation that preserves useful B-factor information while reducing feature-extraction costs for large proteins.
♻ ☆ Atomic Trajectory Modeling with State Space Models for Biomolecular Dynamics NeurIPS 2026
Liang Shi, Jiarui Lu, Junqi Liu, Chence Shi, Zhi Yang, Jian Tang
Understanding the dynamic behavior of biomolecules is fundamental to elucidating biological function and facilitating drug discovery.While Molecular Dynamics (MD) simulations provide a rigorous physical basis for studying these dynamics, they remain computationally expensive for long timescales.Recent deep generative models accelerate conformation generation but often either discard temporal correlations entirely or struggle to condition on the extended history required for faithful kinetics, a consequence of the effectively non-Markovian nature of partially observed subsystem coordinates.To bridge this gap, we introduce ATMOS, a novel generative framework based on State Space Models (SSM) designed to generate atom-level MD trajectories for biomolecular systems.ATMOS integrates a Pairformer-based state transition mechanism to capture temporal dependencies, with a diffusion-based module to decode trajectory frames autoregressively.We demonstrate that ATMOS achieves state-of-the-art performance in generating conformation trajectories for both protein monomers and protein-ligand systems.This work provides a unified and computationally efficient framework for biomolecular trajectory generation, taking a step toward dynamics foundation models.

♻ ☆ Monroe: A Molecular Foundation Model for In-Context Probabilistic Inference NeurIPS 2026
Blazej Banaszewski, Andrew W. Fitzgibbon
Bioassay activity prediction is often data-limited because drug-discovery datasets rely on time-consuming and expensive wet-lab experiments for data generation and evaluation. This challenge has inspired recent research into molecular foundation models (MFMs), which aim to encode general-purpose chemical knowledge into molecular representations that generalize well in data-constrained scenarios. This paper presents Monroe, a new MFM with several innovations over the existing state of the art: increased scale allowing pre-training on over 81 million molecules from the PM6 quantum chemistry dataset; improved graph representation of stereochemistry; improved training losses including conformer denoising and embedding decorrelation; improved multi-task learning; and the use of a prior-data-fitted model (TabPFNv3) for downstream in-context prediction. Our evaluations use a principled pairwise comparison framework that measures statistically significant performance differences. Across established Polaris benchmarks, Monroe matches or exceeds existing MFMs, while on activity cliff benchmarks, designed to assess utility for molecular discovery, it achieves significant improvements over prior methods. Finally, ablation and transfer experiments show that PFN-based downstream predictors also substantially improve two leading existing models, MiniMol and CheMeleon, yielding new state-of-the-art variants we call MiniMol_PFN and CheMeleon_PFN, suggesting that our downstream adaptation strategy generalizes beyond Monroe. Source code is at github.com/blazejba/Monroe

♻ ☆ BioBlobs: Unsupervised Discovery of Functional Substructures NeurIPS 2026
Xin Wang, Kaiwen Shi, Carlos Oliver
Protein function is often localized to cohesive substructures, such as catalytic triads, binding pockets, and structural motifs, that occupy only a small fraction of a protein's residues. Yet existing predictive pipelines typically aggregate residue-level embeddings and do not explicitly model or infer these functional substructures, leaving the central biological question unanswered: which substructure of a protein is responsible for its function? We introduce BioBlobs, an encoder-agnostic, end-to-end differentiable framework that compresses a protein into a small set of cohesive substructures (blobs) and predicts function from these blobs alone, so that each blob corresponds to a candidate functional region. Across diverse protein function prediction tasks and both sequence-only and structure-aware protein language models, BioBlobs is competitive with strong baselines while operating on only a small fraction of residues. Trained only on protein function labels, BioBlobs recovers expert-curated functional sites in InterPro and experimentally annotated catalytic sites in the Mechanism and Catalytic Site Atlas, demonstrating unsupervised functional substructure discovery and opening a path to large-scale functional site discovery in unannotated proteins. The discovered blobs adapt their spatial scale to the task, ranging from local catalytic sites to entire structural domains. Code is available at https://github.com/OliverLaboratory/BioBlobs.

☆ Health-System-Wide Mapping of Demographic Information in Routine Clinical Histopathology
Shengjia Chen, Ruchika Verma, Kevin Clare, Paula Restrepo, Jannes Jegminat, Eugenia Alleva, Kuan-lin Huang, Aryeh Stock, Brandon Veremis, Thomas Fuchs, Gabriele Campanella
The extent to which demographic-associated information is detectable across organs and disease states in routine clinical histopathology remains unclear. Here, we analyzed 1.3 million hematoxylin and eosin whole-slide images from 257,322 patients across 25 organs, using pathology foundation-model representations and multiple-instance learning to systematically map information associated with sex, age group, and self-reported race. Across 69 organ-demographic combinations, demographic attributes were broadly recoverable, with pan-organ mean AUROCs of 0.828 for sex, 0.733 for age group, and 0.662 for self-reported race, and substantial variation across organs. Demographic-class balancing produced relatively small changes in performance, while evaluation within diagnosis-text clusters showed modest attenuation. Under training with diagnosis-based grouping, demographic-associated signals remained detectable across multiple organs, including sex in gastric tissue (AUROC 0.865), age group in breast (0.816), and self-reported race in skin (0.728). Literature-guided analysis of these settings generated candidate histomorphological and contextual explanations for further investigation. Together, these findings provide a systematic map of demographic-associated information in routine histopathology, informing investigations of its sources, assessments of potential shortcut learning, and the development of more interpretable computational pathology models.

☆ Navigating Selection Bias in Genome-wide Association Studies (GWAS)
Ciarrah-Jane S Barry, Tahereh Gholipourshahraki, Zoltán Kutalik, Bjarni J Vilhjálmsson, Esben Agerbo
Selection bias arises when inclusion in a genetic study depends on traits, or causes of traits, that are associated with genotype. It is present in volunteer-based biobanks, case-control designs and studies of disease progression, and genome-wide association study (GWAS) summary statistics from affected samples can propagate this bias into post-GWAS analyses, such as polygenic scores (PGS), Mendelian randomization (MR) and genetic correlation analyses. Despite this broad potential impact, selection bias is sometimes ignored in genetic studies. Many methods exist to detect and correct it, but they rely on different assumptions and require specific data. We organise ten methods applicable to GWAS by the decisions an analyst faces: whether to diagnose or correct, which data are available (individual-level or summary-level), and which form of selection is operating (non-representative participation, case-control ascertainment, index events, sex-differential participation or survival to recruitment). For each method, we describe its principle and assumptions, and we compare methods addressing the same form of selection by their assumptions, data requirements and limitations. A companion GitHub repository implements nine methods in R, prepares input for the external SPREG software, and provides worked examples on simulated data with known true effects for each form of selection, one of which we present in full. An interactive tool supports method selection. We conclude with recommendations for bias-aware study design, sensitivity analysis and reporting.

☆ KineCouple: Substrate-conditioned residual readout for enzyme kinetic prediction
Chun Kang, Yunbo Zhang, Fanhu Zeng, Jian Liu, Siwei Deng, Lei Sha
Accurate prediction of enzyme kinetic parameters can support enzyme engineering and metabolic modeling by complementing experimental characterization. Existing prediction methods often pool residue-level protein features before combining them with substrate features. Such substrate-independent pooling cannot adapt residue weighting to the substrate during aggregation, potentially diluting information relevant to a specific enzyme-substrate pair. Since the contributions of individual residues to catalysis can depend on the substrate, substrate-conditioned aggregation offers a way to emphasize residue features relevant to the prediction. We introduce KineCouple, which uses substrate queries and residue-level keys and values in cross-attention, allowing the substrate to guide residue selection before aggregation. The model preserves the global protein representation and adds a gated correction defined as the difference between the conditioned readout and a uniform readout. ProtT5 and MolT5 remain frozen, and separate models predict kcat and Km on a logarithmic scale. In pooled out-of-fold predictions on the CataPro benchmarks, KineCouple reduces kcat RMSE from 1.333 to 1.330 and increases PCC from 0.492 to 0.496 relative to reproduced CataPro. For Km, RMSE decreases from 0.998 to 0.997 and PCC increases from 0.633 to 0.634. Spearman correlation also improves for both tasks.
♻ ☆ The effect of limb kinematics on the speed of a legged robot on granular media
Chen Li, Paul B. Umbanhowar, Haldun Komsuoglu, Daniel I. Goldman
Achieving effective locomotion on diverse terrestrial substrates can require subtle changes of limb kinematics. Biologically inspired legged robots (physical models of organisms) have shown impressive mobility on hard ground but suffer performance loss on unconsolidated granular materials like sand. Because comprehensive limb-ground interaction models are lacking, optimal gaits on complex yielding terrain have been determined empirically. To develop predictive models for legged devices and to provide hypotheses for biological locomotors, we systematically study the performance of SandBot, a small legged robot, on granular media as a function of gait parameters. High performance occurs only in a small region of parameter space. A previously introduced kinematic model of the robot combined with a new anisotropic granular penetration force law predicts the speed. Performance on granular media is maximized when gait parameters minimize body acceleration and limb interference, and utilize solidification features of granular media.

♻ ☆ ImmSET: Sequence-Based Predictor of TCR-pMHC Specificity at Scale
Marco Garcia Noceda, Matthew T Noakes, Andrew FigPope, Daniel E Mattox, Bryan Howie, Harlan Robins
T cells are a critical component of the adaptive immune system, playing a role in infectious disease, autoimmunity, and cancer. T cell function is mediated by the T cell receptor (TCR) protein, a highly diverse receptor targeting specific peptides presented by the major histocompatibility complex (pMHCs). Predicting the specificity of TCRs for their cognate pMHCs is central to understanding adaptive immunity and enabling personalized therapies. However, accurate prediction of this protein-protein interaction remains challenging due to the extreme diversity of both TCRs and pMHCs. Here, we present ImmSET (Immune Synapse Encoding Transformer), a novel sequence-based architecture designed to model interactions among sets of variable-length biological sequences. We train this model across a range of dataset sizes and compositions and study the resulting models' generalization to pMHC targets. We describe a failure mode in prior sequence-based approaches that inflates previously reported performance on this task and show that ImmSET remains robust under stricter evaluation. In systematically testing the scaling behavior of ImmSET with training data, we show that performance scales consistently with data volume across multiple data types and compares favorably with the pre-trained protein language model ESM2 fine-tuned on the same datasets. Finally, we demonstrate that ImmSET can outperform AlphaFold2 and AlphaFold3-based pipelines on TCR-pMHC specificity prediction when provided sufficient training data. This work establishes ImmSET as a scalable modeling paradigm for multi-sequence interaction problems, demonstrated in the TCR-pMHC setting but generalizable to other biological domains where high-throughput sequence-driven reasoning complements structure prediction and experimental mapping.

♻ ☆ Simple models outperform single-cell foundation models on downstream benchmarks
Huan Souza, Pankaj Mehta
Single-cell RNA sequencing (scRNA-seq) data exhibit strong and reproducible statistical structure. This has motivated the development of large-scale foundation models, such as TranscriptFormer, that use transformer-based architectures to learn a generative model for gene expression by embedding genes into a latent vector space. These embeddings have been used to obtain state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance on downstream tasks such as cell-type classification, disease-state prediction, and cross-species learning. Here, we ask whether similar performance can be achieved without utilizing computationally intensive deep learning-based representations. Using simple, interpretable pipelines that rely on careful normalization and linear methods, we obtain SOTA or near SOTA performance across multiple benchmarks commonly used to evaluate single-cell foundation models, including outperforming foundation models on out-of-distribution tasks involving novel cell types and organisms absent from the training data. Our findings highlight the need for rigorous benchmarking and suggest that the biology of cell identity can be captured by simple linear representations of single cell gene expression data.

☆ Kinetic models of a K channel with 2 stage voltage sensor activation and an opening step
S. R. Vaccaro
The activation of a K channel with 2 voltage sensors and a separate opening step may be described by a 4 state kinetic equation. If $k_{o}$ and $k_{c}$ are the opening and closing rates, and $α$ and $β$ are the voltage sensor rates such that $k_{o}$ $\ggα,k_{c}\ggβ$ and $k_{o}$ $\gg k_{c}$, the kinetic equation may be reduced to a 3 state model where the effective backward rate from the activated-open state is $2βk_{c}/(k_{o}+k_{c})$. However, if a K channel voltage sensor activates in 2 stages, and there is a separate opening step, the occupation probabilities of the states of the channel satisfy a 10 state kinetic equation. When the time constants of the characteristic equation for each voltage sensor differ by an order of magnitude, and $k_{o}$ $\gg k_{c}$, the open state probability is proportional to $n^{2}$, where $n$ satisfies a rate equation, and the derived rates have a similar voltage dependence to the rates $α_{n}$ and $β_{n}$ from voltage clamp data.

☆ Geometric Relevance Is Not Sufficient: Evaluating Focused Post-Training Supervision in Diffusion Docking NeurIPS 2026
Michael Brown, Maryam Rahimimovassagh, Mehdi Yazdani-Jahromi, Sanjeda Sara Jennifer, Ivan Garibay
Diffusion-based molecular docking requires learning a corrective score field across a broad corruption distribution, yet individual pose errors can differ substantially in their structural consequences. This creates a post-training conflict between preserving broad score-field coverage and concentrating supervision on corrections that appear more relevant to docking geometry. Using DiffDock, a fixed-budget study compares broad continuation with torsional supervision focused on Cartesian consequence or conditional target consistency, and with a broad/focused mixture. Evaluation separates fixed score fidelity, generated-pose quality, best-of-10 sampling capacity, and confidence-selected Top-1 docking performance. At <5 Angstrom, broad continuation had the highest mean Top-1 success among trained policies, and focused or mixed supervision showed no consistent advantage under the tested budget. The starting model had better fixed score fidelity, whereas the evaluated broad-continuation model had higher mean Top-1 success with the same aggregate best-of-10 recovery. Focusing supervision on structurally consequential or more consistently specified corrections did not consistently improve returned-pose success over broad continuation.

☆ Static Recovery Is Not Dynamic Stability: Dynamics-Aware Benchmarking of Protein Motif Scaffolding NeurIPS 2026
Emile de Bruyn, Moritz Schäffler, Jannik Schneider, Sebastian R. Schmidt, Chanwoong Hwang, Aleena Siji, Utkarsh Upadhyay, Stefan Bauer, Alexander Schug, Stefan Kesselheim, Artur Yakimovich, Alina Bazarova
Protein motif scaffolding benchmarks assess whether generative models can build scaffolds around functionally important residues. Their conclusions depend on which motifs are tested and which criteria define success. Existing benchmarks typically use small hand-curated motif sets and static refolding criteria, but protein function often depends on conformational ensembles and transitions. Here, we address both benchmark scope and success criteria. We introduce BILBY-PRO, a systematically derived benchmark of structurally conserved functional motifs from PROSITE-linked experimental structures, comprising 220 cases from 174 PROSITE motif-pattern entries. Evaluating six recent scaffold-generation models reveals distinct patterns in target coverage and unique-solution yield: RFDiffusion and Proteina solve more targets, whereas ESM3 produces more unique successful scaffolds on the targets it solves. We further introduce BILBY-MD, a reference-based all-atom molecular dynamics evaluation framework. Applied to statically solved designs from the original MotifBench benchmark, it compares motif drift, scaffold drift, motif-contact preservation, and motif-dihedral free-energy landscapes against reference simulations. Of 55 statically solved model-problem pairs, 31 satisfy our MD-supported criterion and only 8 pass all checks. Compact, self-contained motifs are often MD-supported, whereas interface-like or context-dependent motifs fail more frequently. Static refolding success therefore provides useful evidence of local motif recovery but does not establish dynamical fidelity of the full scaffold. BILBY highlights three complementary axes of motif-scaffolding evaluation: target coverage, unique-solution yield, and MD-supported dynamical fidelity.

☆ Hybrid Quantum-Classical Annealing for Protein Side-Chain Optimization: A Feasibility Study
Valeria B. C. Visani, Simone Reale, Alfonso Gautieri, Elisabetta Di Nitto
Hybrid quantum-classical annealing solvers are moving from research laboratories toward industrial use, but their advantage over classical optimization appears to be confined to a narrow class of problems. A recent systematic benchmark of the D-Wave hybrid solver against industry-leading classical solvers, across several classes of optimization problems, found it to be most competitive on binary quadratic programs, while noting that few industrial problems take this form. Therefore, identifying real-world industrial problems that can be modeled with this structure is a key challenge. We propose protein side-chain optimization, a core step of computational protein design, as one of these problems: it is natively a binary quadratic program, and its application context requires pools of low-energy solutions, which annealers naturally return. In this feasibility study, we test whether the competitiveness of the hybrid solver against industry-leading classical solvers extends to this problem. On 40 instances derived from 20 experimentally determined protein structures with up to about 200 amino acids and 2000 binary variables, we compare the results achieved with a hybrid solver, run for 5 seconds, against those of the commercial solver Gurobi, run for 10 seconds, 1000 seconds, and 4 hours. The hybrid solver reaches the certified optimum whenever one is available, matches Gurobi's performance on smaller instances, and outperforms it on larger instances, finding lower-energy solutions. These results show that the competitiveness of the hybrid solver on binary quadratic programs, so far observed only on a synthetic benchmark, extends to a real-world problem. This motivates comparisons with classical heuristics specific to protein side-chain optimization.

☆ Interpretable many-body interaction laws for collective motion from few trajectories
Timon S. Gutleb
Models of collective motion usually reduce social interactions to pairwise forces, despite empirical evidence for many-body effects. We introduce the directional atomic cluster expansion (dACE), which adapts the atomic cluster expansion (ACE) of interatomic potentials to self-propelled agents. dACE provides a systematically improvable, exactly equivariant expansion of an agent's acceleration that splits uniquely into 1-, 2-, 3-body and higher contributions, along with a self term for the agent's own past motion that is fitted so that it does not absorb the interactions. On simulated collectives in two and three dimensions with known rules, dACE recovers the interaction law body order by body order from little data, while the neural model SocialNet, the current closest competitor, needs more data, has much larger errors on many-body or chiral rules and is restricted to 2D. On real data sets of tracked zebrafish and golden shiners, the laws learned by dACE agree better between disjoint halves of the data than SocialNet's. The learned laws are interpretable, and in the shiners the 3-body term opposes the pairwise response as neighbourhoods get dense. Finally, we show that next-step prediction accuracy mostly measures self-propulsion and noise rather than the interactions, and suggest more robust combinations of error measures for interaction learning.

☆ Pattern selection and biomass shifts in a vegetation model with nonlocal competition and Allee activation
Federico Dimatz, Marcelo N Kuperman
We study a single-species vegetation model in which a strong Allee effect acts locally and competition acts through a nonlocal kernel, and we describe the kernel with the one-parameter family of q-Gaussians, which goes from compact support to power-law tails. The local activation introduced by the Allee effect allows a finite-wavelength instability even when the Fourier transform of the kernel is positive, so Gaussian and heavy-tailed kernels can produce patterns. We derive in closed form the coefficients of the hexagonal amplitude equations and verify them against independent numerical steady states. The quadratic coefficient $Q$ results from the balance between the curvature of the local growth and the competitive inhibition at the pattern scale, and at onset its sign depends only on the fraction of the local activation that diffusion removes at the critical wavenumber. The same coefficient controls the morphology, since spots, labyrinths and gaps appear with pairwise competition only as $Q$ changes sign, and the amplitude equations predict the choice between hexagons and stripes in every simulated state in which the cubic truncation holds. It also controls the total biomass, which increases for spots and decreases for gaps.
☆ Leveraging deep generative modelling at the agent level in discrete-event simulations
Margherita Molaro, Matthew M. Graham, Oliver Watson, Rachel E. Murray-Watson, Martin Chalkley, Joseph H. Collins, Bingling She, Asif U. Tamuri, Timothy B. Hallett
Emulators are often invoked to facilitate parameter inference in computationally intensive agent-based simulations (ABSs). Most approaches, however, require a large sample of pre-existing outputs on which such emulators can be trained. For computationally intensive simulations, this sample can be prohibitively expensive to generate. We propose a new approach to leverage emulators at the agent level in event-based ABSs to reduce the computational requirements of the simulation itself. We demonstrate this in a computationally intensive ``multi-disease'' ABS, Thanzi La Onse, which self-consistently simulates a wide range of medical conditions in a population. By applying conditional tabular generative adversarial networks to replace the modelling of one of the simulated causes of ill health, road traffic injuries (RTIs), we are able to improve its simulation run-time by 86.6% (95% bootstrap confidence interval [85.2, 88.1]), while recovering the evolution of cause-specific mean disability adjusted life years with a 13% error, despite the low assumed incidence of fatal RTIs (~2%). Crucially, we do so while retaining the full ABS framework, and correctly recovering multi-disease dynamics. This represents a crucial step towards enabling full-model emulators for very computationally intensive simulations, and making large ABS amenable to rigorous parameter inference and a full understanding of parameter sensitivity.
