☆ PharmAgents2: Towards Autonomous End-to-End Drug Discovery
Haichuan Tan, Bowen Gao, Tao Huang, Jiaxin Tan, Xin Hong, Baoguo Yao, Yifei Wang, Zhichao Deng, Hanji Du, Dapeng Jiang, Yiheng Li, Weiwei Ma, Xin Ma, Yuqi Shi, Zixiang Wang, Kelin Wu, Tianxiang Wu, Wenxuan Xie, Jie Zang, Huiyu Zhang, Mojia Zhang, Yuying Zhang, Ya-Qin Zhang, Wei-Ying Ma, Yanyan Lan
Drug discovery requires continuous scientific decision-making across a vast research space. ☆ How Deep is the Signal? Time-Context up to 30 Years for Colorectal Cancer Prediction in a Primary-Care EHR Model
Achille Raviez, Lan Wang, Brendan Delaney
EHR foundation models read medical events within a fixed context window, and long context has so far been measured only in tokens. How far back in time a model must look to capture the earliest predictive signal is unknown. We introduce time-context, the calendar lookback window before the prediction point, and provide, to our knowledge, the first systematic evaluation of decade-scale time-context for an EHR foundation model. We pretrained a decoder-only transformer (34.6M parameters) on 1.4B coded events from 3.6M primary-care patients in North West London and fine-tuned it to predict incident colorectal cancer under a 12-month pre-diagnostic buffer. A frozen cohort of 1,008 cases and 9,582 matched controls, all registered for at least 30 years, was evaluated at 15 nested lookback windows from 2 to 30 years, so that every truncation is compared within the same patients. Discrimination rose from an AUROC of 0.551 at 2 years to 0.590 at 18 years, then plateaued, with about 80% of the gain reached within 10 years; an XGBoost baseline converged to the same level (peak AUROC 0.589). A simulation further suggests that rotary position embeddings (RoPE) over elapsed time alias beyond about 20 years at the default base frequency, motivating a retuned $θ$. Time-context is a measurable design axis for clinical prediction models, distinct from token-context, and should be reported.

☆ Personalized health coaching via coachable health age and counterfactual wearable trajectories
Simon A. Lee, Zeinab Esmaeilpour, Menglian Zhou, Marius Guerard, Sohrab Sidhu, Alexandru Solot, Hamid Palangi, Pramod Rudrapatna, Ming-Zher Poh, Max A. Xu, Daniel McDuff, Dimitrios Antos, Yuzhe Yang, Jeffrey N. Chiang, Anthony Z. Faranesh, Lindsey Sunden, Mark Malhotra, Shwetak Patel, A. Ali Heydari, Ahmed A. Metwally
Personalized health coaching requires moving beyond retrospective wearable summaries toward quantitative, actionable behavioral prescriptions. We introduce Coachable Health Age (CHA), an interpretable health-age metric built from modifiable physiological and behavioral signals passively captured by consumer wearables. Across the WEAR-ME (N=2,780), All of Us (N=22,748), and longitudinal Fitbit (N=1,464) cohorts, CHA aligns with established cardiometabolic health constructs and improves prognostic discrimination for incident cardiovascular and metabolic disease beyond age, sex, and body mass index. ☆ From Shape to Species: The Use of Persistent Homology in Restructuring the Taxonomy of \textit{Acropora} corals

♻ ☆ scDNM-VAE enables directly inspectable deep clustering of single-cell RNA-seq data through signed dendritic gating
Melih Agraz, Deniz Karapinar, Aysel Topsir, Qianying Cao, Erol Egrioglu, Gaurav Choudhary
Deep clustering models for single-cell RNA sequencing often assign cells through latent or centroid-based mechanisms that are difficult to inspect. We introduce scDNM-VAE (single-cell Dendritic Neuron Model Variational Autoencoder), a deep clustering framework that combines a variational autoencoder with a dendritic neuron-inspired head. Cluster assignments are governed by learnable signed synaptic weights and thresholds: the weight sign determines the direction of a gate's response to a latent coordinate, its magnitude controls steepness, and the weight-threshold pair determines the transition location. The trained clustering function can therefore be inspected directly without fitting a post-hoc explanation model. We benchmark scDNM-VAE on four datasets spanning immune, cortical, cardiac, and hematopoietic cells against scVI followed by KMeans and an MLP-DEC ablation. scDNM-VAE performs better than scVI on PBMC3k, comparably on the Human Heart Cell Atlas and Paul15, and worse on Zeisel, while producing biologically coherent marker-gene signatures. Ablating each cluster's three highest-magnitude synaptic dimensions causes numerically greater reassignment than random-dimension ablation across all datasets, but the margins are modest and negligible on Zeisel. These results show that signed dendritic gating supports competitive clustering with a parameter-inspectable decision function, while indicating that decision-relevant information is distributed across the latent space.

♻ ☆ scTREAT: Latent Diffusion for Single-Cell Drug-Response Prediction
Zhaokang Liang, Shuyang Zhuang, Xiaoran Jiao, Weian Mao, Hao Chen, Chunhua Shen
Generating drug-induced transcriptional responses requires a model to account for cellular background together with molecular identity and dose. We present scTREAT, the first diffusion framework to factorize state and drug guidance for single-cell drug-response prediction. A shared latent expression space connects baseline and response, while Guided Decomposable Attention (GD-Attn) injects a baseline representation and a dose-conditioned molecular embedding. Independent channel dropout exposes four conditioning states during training; inference combines three conditional predictions with separately specified state and drug coefficients, and dose modulates drug-guidance strength. On condition-level pseudobulk profiles derived from the Tahoe-100M single-cell atlas, scTREAT achieves the best value on 10 of 12 metrics for unseen cell line-drug combinations (UC) and 11 of 12 for unseen drugs (UD). These results demonstrate the effectiveness of combining shared latent expression modeling with explicit state and drug conditioning for response prediction.

☆ Discrete Backbone Elasticity and Curvature-Dependent Torsion Statistics in Proteins
Yiquan Wang, Mingyu Bian, Jiayuan Ma
Coarse-grained models of protein conformational dynamics use $\mathrm{C}_α$ geometry to describe the probabilities of regular helices, ordered turns and disordered conformations. To test how much of this order arises from local chain elasticity, we connect discrete Hasimoto frame transport to neighboring bending mismatch, deriving a curvature-product torsion interaction and a six-parameter generalized von Mises density. Fitted on 194 chains from the ATLAS molecular dynamics database and tested on 1744 sequence-nonredundant proteins, this baseline reduces negative log-density loss from 1.490 to 1.160 nats, torsion-sign error from 12.26 to 2.15 percentage points, and helix-fraction error from 21.84 to 10.41 points. Ablations and circular-moment projections against reference generative models motivate extensions to anisotropic stiffness and two partition-function-weighted elastic states, further lowering test loss to 0.790 nats and helix error to 1.65 points. The fixed hierarchy also improves predictions across 72 trajectories of peptides $\mathrm{Fs}$ and $(\mathrm{AAQAA})_3$ and miniprotein $\mathrm{HP35}$ spanning varied starting conformations and force fields. Remaining angular errors in these trajectories are associated with atomic context at shared bends, as bend-matched peptide frames adopt opposite torsion signs with distinct hydrogen-bond patterns and a proline-containing $\mathrm{HP35}$ turn remains at negative torsion. Introducing a bilinearly coupled $\mathrm{C}_α$--$\mathrm{N}$ orientation angle largely reparameterizes the existing bend-dependent harmonics, with an empirical $L^2$ Gram projection placing 96.7\% to 99.6\% of its added coefficient-function squared norm within the original six-function span. These results link discrete Hasimoto elasticity to statistical backbone potentials and clarify the scope and limits of local $\mathrm{C}_α$ geometry.
♻ ☆ Predicting protein structures at any folding stage from sequence
Lisheng Zhang, Qingsong Li, Zinan Xie, Yanting Yang, Qikui Yang, Peng Lu, Zhican Fu, Wentao Cui, Runtong Hou, Zhengwei Xie
Protein structure prediction has largely focused on native conformations, leaving the continuum of unfolded states, folding intermediates, and native structures difficult to access. We present FoldScape, a sequence-conditioned deep learning framework for generating protein conformational ensembles at specified folding fractions $Q$. Trained on approximately one million conformations from 10,000 protein sequences, FoldScape combines coarse-grained folding and all-atom mechanical-unfolding trajectories. It reconstructs three-dimensional $C_α$ chains through hierarchical sliding-window regressions of inter-residue distances, chain-level assembly, and a rectified-flow decoder. By conditioning on folding progress, FoldScape generates conformations from unfolded states through intermediates to native-state structures without protein-specific molecular dynamics at inference. In matched-sample comparisons, FoldScape achieves median conformational-space coverage of 0.989, 0.722, and 0.877 along radius of gyration, fraction of native contacts $Q$, and secondary-structure fraction, respectively, versus 0.05, 0.05, and 0.10 for seed-varied AlphaFold 3 ensembles. Coverage advantages are consistent across the evaluated proteins and robust to binning choices. Reconstruction accuracy remains approximately stable across the evaluated stages of a folding trajectory, and stride-one crop-and-superpose inference extends predictions to chains several times longer than the single-pass limit. Analyses of mechanical-unfolding trajectories show residue-resolved pathways, reproducible extension-conditioned observables across pulling velocities, and geometrically coherent segments in partially unfolded chains. FoldScape extends sequence-based structure prediction beyond a single folded endpoint to controllable conformational-ensemble generation across protein folding.
♻ ☆ PeptSol: A Translational Benchmark for Protected-Peptide Solubility in Drug Manufacturing NeurIPS 2026
Alexander Arjun Lobo, Marc Saouda, Urvi Awasthi, Leonid Zhukov
Liquid-phase peptide synthesis (LPPS) offers a scalable route to peptide manufacturing, but route development still depends on empirical solvent screens because each protected intermediate must be soluble for reaction and recoverable for purification. The protecting groups, terminal modifications, and noncanonical residues that govern this behaviour are poorly represented in existing peptide property benchmarks, limiting the ability to develop and rigorously evaluate molecular representation models for LPPS-relevant prediction tasks. We introduce PeptSol, an open benchmark of 1388 hand-curated protected-peptide solubility measurements with chemistry-aware splits. The measurements span only 84 parent sequences and 101 protected structures, and 92.5% come from a single historical programme. The dataset, evaluation protocol, and baselines establish a standard for measuring progress on molecular representations for synthesis-aware peptide developability.

☆ A low-rank decoder bottleneck bounds reliability in foundation-model perturbation prediction NeurIPS 2026
Zheyu Zhang, Yuanhao Huang, Fan Feng, Jie Liu
Foundation-model predictors of perturbation response are evaluated primarily by aggregate accuracy. Using their predictions to prioritize experiments requires assessing each prediction's reliability. We audit the state-transition (ST) decoder used in STATE across three encoders (SE-600M, scGPT, and Tahoe-x1) and four cell lines under CRISPRi. The decoder's first attention layer has a low-rank output. Projecting this output onto 4-32 fixed directions estimated from training activations preserves mean per-perturbation Pearson delta correlation (PDC). This low-rank attention bottleneck bounds the decoder's reliability by distance to training responses: PDC declines as the cosine distance from a perturbation's observed response to its nearest training response increases. We propose predicted-NN-distance, the cosine distance from a predicted response to its nearest training response, as a per-prediction confidence score. The score has a mean absolute Spearman correlation of 0.81 with PDC, close to the 0.88 obtained with the observed response. Thresholds calibrated on this score transfer across encoders and cell lines, and the score remains predictive of PDC for drug perturbations.

♻ ☆ Agentic Literature Extraction Augments Targeted Protein Degradation Databases
Yaochen Rao, Farzaneh Jalalypour, N. M. Anoop Krishnan, Rocío Mercado
Predictive models in medicinal chemistry depend on structured assay data locked in primary publications. This bottleneck is acute in targeted protein degradation (TPD), where each assay record must combine compound identity, degradation target, recruiter, assay context, and endpoint values reported across sections, tables, and supplementary files. Existing TPD databases are manually curated and often lack assay context. We present an expert-in-the-loop large language model workflow in which a cross-validated prompt-refinement module adapts extraction instructions from scarce expert annotations. In a controlled surrogate model comparison, PROTAC degradation activity models trained on AutoTPD+ saw a 8.9-18.2% error reduction relative to models trained on the original PROTAC-DB data.

♻ ☆ Sensitivity Analysis of Cryo-Electron Microscopy Reconstructions via the Log-Likelihood Hessian
Aaditya V. Rangan, Wai-Shing Tang, Kexin Zhang, Pilar Cossio
Cryo-electron microscopy (cryo-EM) is a technique in structural biology which is used to produce high-resolution 3D reconstructions of molecules. One common step in the cryo-EM pipeline involves ab-initio single-particle reconstruction (SPR). This is a type of inverse-problem which takes as input a collection of 2D-images -- each a projection of a molecule from an unknown viewing-angle -- and attempts to reconstruct the 3D-volume representing the underlying molecular density. Most methods for solving SPR search for a solution which optimizes a posterior likelihood of generating the observed image-data, given the reconstructed volume. Within this framework, it is natural to study the Hessian of the log- likelihood: the eigenvectors and eigenvalues of the Hessian determine how the likelihood changes with respect to perturbations in the solution, which can give insight into the sensitivity of the solution to aspects of the input, such as the orientation distribution of image viewing-angles. In this paper, we describe a simple strategy for estimating the smallest eigenvalues and eigenvectors (i.e., the 'softest modes') of the Hessian of the log-likelihood for the ab-initio single-particle reconstruction problem. This strategy involves rewriting the log-likelihood as a 3D-integral. Once we have estimated the softest modes, we can use them to determine which parts of the reconstructed volume are trustworthy, and which are unreliable, and how this unreliability might depend on the data-set and the imaging parameters. We believe that this kind of analysis can be used alongside more traditional strategies for sensitivity analysis, as well as in other applications, such as free-energy estimation.
♻ ☆ Bridging the Modality Bottleneck in Pathology MIL through Molecularly Guided Feature Adaptation
Yucheng Xing, Pei Liu, Jingying Ma, Ruping Hong, Jiangdong Qiu, Tianyu Liu, Kai He, Ling Huang, Mengling Feng
Multiple instance learning (MIL) is the dominant framework for whole-slide image analysis in computational pathology, typically combining a frozen patch encoder, a projection layer, and a slide-level aggregator. While encoders and aggregators have been extensively studied, the projection layer remains a largely morphology-only bottleneck. This limits endpoints such as biomarker status and survival, which are governed by a molecular state that is not fully captured by H&E morphology. We introduce Molecularly Informed Staining Transform (MIST), a plug-in replacement for the MIL projection layer that uses paired spatial transcriptomics only during training to construct virtual molecular stains. MIST clusters gene expression profiles into cross-modal prototypes, anchors them in the frozen foundation model feature space, and uses them to reorganize H&E patch features along molecularly guided axes. It requires no transcriptomics at inference and can be inserted before standard MIL aggregators. We evaluate MIST across 23 downstream tasks and 8 MIL aggregators. MIST improves 240 of 256 configurations over the standard projection layer, with an average gain of +3.5%, observed consistently across endpoint types: +5.2% on survival prediction, +3.3% on tissue subtyping, and +2.6% on biomarker prediction. Ablations confirm that gene-derived prototypes are the primary source of the gains, while spatial, biological, and pathological analyses show that cross-modal prototype affinities capture spatially coherent molecular programs from H&E alone.

☆ iGC: Antibody Optimization via Reinforcement Learning over a Learned Somatic Hypermutation Prior
Thao Nguyen, Robert G. Alberstein, Ewa Nowara, Homa MohammadiPeyhani, Holly R. Steach, Sarah A. Robinson, Yuval Shechter, Donald W. Lee, Jason A. Vander Heiden, Franziska Seeger, Andrew Leaver-Fay, Richard Bonneau, Joshua Yao-Yu Lin
Antibody affinity maturation is a natural optimization process in which B cells acquire mutations through somatic hypermutation (SHM), undergo selection for improved antigen binding, and iteratively evolve toward higher affinity. Computationally modeling this process requires capturing both biologically plausible mutations and antigen-specific selection. However, existing mutation models do not directly optimize binding, while affinity optimization often neglects realistic mutation trajectories. We introduce iGC (in silico Germinal Centers), a two-stage reinforcement learning (RL) framework for antigen-specific antibody optimization. Stage 1 learns an SHM mutation prior from parent-child pairs in B-cell lineage trees and adapts it to a target affinity-maturation dataset. Stage 2 initializes an RL policy from this prior and optimizes antigen binding with Kullback-Leibler (KL) regularization toward the frozen prior. To prevent reward exploitation, including repetitive high-reward substitutions and structurally tolerated framework mutations, we introduce a Reward-Tax Meta-Gradient objective. RewardNet smooths the affinity landscape, while TaxNet penalizes reward overexploitation, jointly defining a learned meta-reward for policy optimization. We evaluate iGC on mutation prediction, SHM consistency, affinity optimization, and mutation diversity. The mutation prior more than doubles the strongest baseline in mutation-site prediction. Following RL optimization, iGC maintains SHM sequence structure. It also achieves a strong exploration-exploitation balance, yielding competitive reward-diversity products across candidate-selection cutoffs. These results demonstrate that iGC integrates biologically plausible mutation generation with antigen-specific affinity selection, providing an in silico framework for iterative antibody affinity maturation.

☆ Empirical Bayes Flow Matching for Continuous Cryo-EM Heterogeneity NeurIPS 2026
Daniel Aibinder, Azmi Haider, Dan Rosenbaum
We address the problem of learning generative priors over high-dimensional latent variables from indirect, noisy observations, with a focus on continuous heterogeneity in cryo-electron microscopy (cryo-EM). In this setting, 3D atomic structures $x \in \mathbb{R}^{3N_a}$ are never directly observed and must be inferred from 2D projection images $y$ generated by a complex, non-linear forward operator. We propose Empirical Bayes Flow Matching (EB-FM), a method that learns a flow matching prior $p_θ(x)$ directly from observations using a Monte Carlo stochastic approximation Expectation-Maximization framework. EB-FM alternates between an E-step that performs approximate posterior sampling from $p_θ(x \mid y)$ via guided flow-based generation while maintaining persistent latent estimates through stochastic approximation, and an M-step that updates the flow model using these stabilized latent variables as training targets. This approach eliminates the need for pretraining on clean data and naturally accommodates non-linear forward models. We validate our method on controlled inverse problems using two-moons and MNIST data, and demonstrate its effectiveness on simulated cryo-EM data with known poses, showing that EB-FM can recover continuous distributions of protein conformations directly in atomic-coordinate space.

☆ Ablating Design Choices for Protein Ensemble Generators Across Structural Regimes
Eoin Quinn, Oliver Bent
Generative models of protein conformational ensembles inherit much of their design from structure prediction for folded proteins. Ordered and disordered proteins, however, occupy distinct structural regimes, and we investigate how design choices hold up across them. Using a minimal C$α$ flow-matching model, we ablate choices of architecture, sequence conditioning, rigid-body alignment and training source, and evaluate each variant against experimental SAXS and NMR chemical-shift data partitioned into ordered, disordered, multi-domain and transitional proteins. Mixture-of-experts routing, a static pair representation and a disorder probe have no detectable effect, a smaller protein language model performs worse on ordered proteins but not on disordered ones, and alignment in the loss degrades agreement with SAXS. Replacing coarse-grained simulations of disordered proteins with fragment-assembled ensembles improves agreement with chemical shifts at a cost to SAXS, while an equal mixture of the two keeps most of the gain at a smaller cost. This points to the training data, rather than the architecture, as the main constraint.

☆ Predicting protein structures at any folding stage from sequence
Lisheng Zhang, Qingsong Li, Zinan Xie, Yanting Yang, Qikui Yang, Peng Lu, Zhican Fu, Wentao Cui, Runtong Hou, Zhengwei Xie
Protein structure predictors reliably identify native conformations, but do not describe the continuum connecting unfolded ensembles to folded states. Here we present FoldScape, a sequence-conditioned deep learning framework that generates C$α$ conformational ensembles at a specified folding fraction $Q$. FoldScape reconstructs three-dimensional chains through hierarchical, sliding-window regressions of one-dimensional inter-residue distances, followed by chain assembly and sampling with a rectified-flow decoder. It is trained on multi-scale coarse-grained folding trajectories and all-atom mechanical-unfolding trajectories. Across 20 human proteins (36-857 residues), at matched sample size, FoldScape covers about an order of magnitude more of the folding coordinate than seed-varied AlphaFold 3 ensembles, robustly across proteins, order parameters and binning choices. AlphaFold 3 better anchors the immediate native vicinity, which FoldScape does not reach, making the approaches complementary. FoldScape reconstruction accuracy remains approximately constant along $Q$, from collapsed coil to near-native globule, rather than deteriorating away from the native state. A stride-one crop-and-superpose scheme extends inference to chains several times longer than a single chain-level forward pass. We also show that training trajectories resolve mechanical unfolding at residue resolution, that elongation-conditioned ensembles exhibit reproducible continuous observables across pulling velocities, and that partially unfolded chains form geometrically coherent segments. FoldScape thus combines broad coverage of folding stages with stable reconstruction accuracy, without per-protein simulation.

♻ ☆ A Comprehensive Benchmark of Computational Models for Antibody--Antigen Binding Affinity
Xinyan Zhao, Yi-Ching Tang, Victor J Cantu, Junseok Lee, Adam E Stogsdill, Ibraheem M Hamdi, Ashwin Kumar Ramesh, Zhiqiang An, Xiaoqian Jiang, Yejin Kim
Computational antibody design requires benchmarks that measure functional binding rather than similarity to natural sequences. ☆ Trail systems self-organize through individual route optimization
Brendan Wallace, Andrew Berdahl
As animals, including humans, walk, they compact the vegetation and ground beneath them, reducing the cost of movement for subsequent travelers. From this positive feedback emerge networks of trails which allow for efficient movement and store generational-scale information about the environment. Models simulating this process show how individuals following local cues produce trail systems with many of the features of real-world networks. However, little has been done to investigate walkers with more complete knowledge of their environment and the abilities to plan their behavior intelligently. Here we introduce a simple general model for trail formation, based on minimizing cost of movement by individuals. As in real environments, our simulations produce a striking variety of complex trail networks. We show that these trail systems optimally connect space according to the spatial needs of the walkers, that the geometry of those trail systems matches theoretical predictions and that the systems they generate may fall into multiple attracting states. As technological advances allow us to quantify trail systems across diverse habitats and species, our model provides a theoretical foundation for empirical research on animal trails and for understanding the emergence of human transportation networks, with implications for urban design.
♻ ☆ Maternal Anxiety During Pregnancy and Offspring Information Processing Across Development: A Theory-Guided Predictive-Processing Reappraisal of Two Longitudinal Cohorts
Bea R. H. Van den Bergh, Martin G. Frasch
Prenatal maternal distress has been associated with differences in offspring brain and behavioural development, but is rarely framed computationally. Predictive-processing (PP) accounts propose that perception, cognition, and action arise from interactions between input and internally generated predictions, partly governed by precision. We reappraised eleven publications from two non-clinical prospective cohorts, selected using prespecified criteria, to assess whether they form a coherent PP-compatible developmental pattern. Eight Leuven publications spanned adolescence to adulthood. Externally cued response inhibition showed no detectable group differences in modestly powered samples, whereas differences emerged when task goals, stimulus relevance, expected outcomes, and response policies had to be generated or maintained endogenously. At age 28, higher prenatal anxiety was associated with lower radial and mean diffusivity in the left superior posterior corona radiata, lower Vocabulary scores, weaker medial prefrontal-left inferior frontal and prefrontal-left supramarginal gyrus connectivity, and greater unpleasantness of induced dyspnoea. In three Tilburg ERP publications (infancy and early childhood), higher prenatal anxiety was associated with increased processing of repeated standard tones, fearful relative to happy vocalizations, and neutral rather than pleasant or unpleasant pictures. Maternal mindfulness showed the opposite association for the repeated standard. Across cohorts, findings were compatible with differences in how relevance and processing resources are allocated to predictable, threat-related, ambiguous, contextual, feedback-related, and bodily information. Because the publications did not estimate precision, prediction errors, or priors, this is a theory-guided, post-hoc reappraisal that generates discriminating hypotheses rather than a formal computational test.

♻ ☆ Attribution-based diagnostics for single-cell foundation models
Farnoush Rezaei Jafari, Tristan Lazard, Kenza Bouzid, Shruthi Bannur, Jeremiah Wander, Ashley Mae Conard, Stephanie Hyland, Lorin Crawford, Daniel Coelho de Castro
Single-cell RNA foundation models (scFMs) are increasingly used for in-silico biological discovery, yet predictive performance alone does not reveal whether their decisions rely on biologically meaningful signals or model-specific shortcuts. We systematically investigate post-hoc attribution of downstream predictions across two tasks: cell-type annotation using pretrained Cell2Sentence and scGPT models, and perturbation prediction using scGPT+STATE in a controlled setting. For cell annotation, we use attribution to assess whether predictions rely on established marker genes and how their relevance varies with model architecture, expression rank, and the surrounding cellular context. For perturbation prediction, we compare attribution patterns with known regulatory ground-truth in a synthetic setting to assess what they reveal about the model's learned computations. Across these tasks, attribution reveals biological feature use, contextual interactions, contributions from different input pathways, learning progression, associations with prediction quality, and potential pitfalls that are not fully captured by standard performance metrics. Experiments with complete and limited regulatory knowledge further demonstrate that conclusions drawn from attribution depend strongly on the choice of biological reference, baseline, and null model. Together, these findings highlight the value of attribution as a flexible post-hoc diagnostic for understanding model behavior, identifying potential issues, and informing the development of scFMs.
♻ ☆ DoAtlas-2: A Foundation for Self-Evolving Causal Biomedical Discovery
Yulong Li, Rong Xia, Yuxuan Zhang, Jianxu Chen, Xiwei Liu, Haochen Xue, Maosheng Li, Yuhang Liu, Yibo Yuan, Yutong Xie, Chong Li, Jionglong Su, Hagai Rossman, Eran Segal, Imran Razzak
We introduce DoAtlas-2, a foundation for self-evolving causal biomedical discovery that organizes knowledge around causal mechanisms and advances through external evidence from human populations. DoAtlas-2 integrates 771 research resources covering more than 720,000 participants in 48 countries, from longitudinal clinical phenotypes, medical imaging, and continuous physiological signals to eight molecular layers, together with an evidence network of approximately 4.7 million literature-derived records over 93,566 concepts and 149,383 candidate causal relations. DoAtlas-2 autonomously formulates research questions from evidence gaps and unresolved mechanisms, prespecifies their causal designs, and generates validated analyses. Supporting, challenging, and unresolved results continuously revise mechanistic interpretations, the causal evidence state, and the discovery frontier, so that DoAtlas-2 self-evolves within a closed loop of hypothesis generation, empirical testing, and renewed discovery. DoAtlas-2 has systematically evaluated 2,031 research questions. In the Human Phenotype Project (HPP), it formulated 4,014 candidate pathway questions across vascular, early-glycemic, and hepatic-metabolic systems, and screening of the first 1,079 yielded statistical support for 756. Representative studies identify blood pressure as a convergence node linking adiposity, hepatic, and lipid phenotypes to vascular outcomes, and show that an adiposity-inflammation-blood-pressure pathway is largely attenuated by joint adjustment for body mass index (BMI) and smoking. The discovered vascular network constitutes a completely interpretable predictive foundation, admitting exact attribution of every prediction and closed-form mediation effects. DoAtlas-2 thereby unifies causal mechanism discovery, population-evidence testing, and interpretable prediction within one continuously evolving foundation.

♻ ☆ Molecular notation can act as a shortcut in AI benchmark evaluation
Yosuke Kikuchi, Yasuhiro Yoshikai, Shumpei Nemoto, Ayako Furuhama, Takashi Yamada, Hiroyuki Kusuhara, Tadahaya Mizuno
High predictive accuracy does not necessarily mean that an AI model relies on information relevant to its scientific task. Molecular prediction illustrates this concern: the same molecule can be represented by different SMILES strings, potentially altering model behavior. We combined a systematic PubMed literature survey with experiments on chemical language models. SMILES preparation could not be identified from articles or Supplementary Information in 71 of 174 studies (40.8%). Across six benchmarks, 58.7% of identity-preserving input pairs used different SMILES strings. Notation differences changed reconstruction and latent representations, while separately trained classifiers often achieved similar scores. In two datasets, notation was associated with labels and changed predictions of a fixed model, showing how benchmark scores can reward notational shortcuts. Balancing notation during classifier training reduced prediction differences but sometimes lowered performance on original inputs. These findings support evaluating predictive performance alongside consistency under identity-preserving changes in input representation.

♻ ☆ A Comprehensive Benchmark of Computational Models for Antibody--Antigen Binding Affinity
Xinyan Zhao, Yi-Ching Tang, Victor J Cantu, Junseok Lee, Adam E Stogsdill, Ibraheem M Hamdi, Ashwin Kumar Ramesh, Zhiqiang An, Xiaoqian Jiang, Yejin Kim
Computational antibody design requires benchmarks that measure functional binding rather than similarity to natural sequences. Here we present AbBiBench, which treats the antibody--antigen complex as the evaluation unit and harmonizes 216,560 experimental measurements spanning 13 antibodies and 9 antigens. We compare 20 computational approaches without task-specific training or fine-tuning. The analyses show that global structure-conditioned inverse folding yields the strongest and most consistent affinity rankings, whereas antibody-specific fine-tuning does not consistently improve performance. Across models, rankings are more useful for prioritizing likely affinity gains than for excluding weak variants. In a subsequent case study, multi-objective evaluation of model-generated F045-092 CDR-H3 variants for H1N1 binding revealed a trade-off between predicted binding potential and evolutionary sequence plausibility; all 21 selected candidates produced H1-reactive ELISA signals. AbBiBench provides an open framework for developing and evaluating function-aware antibody design methods.
♻ ☆ The Little Scientist: What Does the Scientific Method Buy an LLM Agent?
Travis Smith
Scientific discovery emerges from cycles of hypothesis, implementation, empirical testing, and feedback. Can this process be automated? We approach automated algorithm design through the lens of the scientific method, where an LLM-based agent goes through each step of the process in an iterative fashion. Results: We present The Little Scientist, a framework that maps the scientific method onto an LLM agent in a loop: a Scientist agent iterates hypothesis, prediction, code, test, and reconciliation inside an evaluation environment that returns structured diagnostics, and a Kuhn agent injects paradigm-shifting reframings at plateaus. On DNA motif discovery, the process wrote an algorithm from scratch (DALE) that outperforms STREME across 132 ENCODE transcription factors on the shuffled-negative benchmark (mean AUROC 0.842 vs. 0.803) while running 11x faster. We then asked why this method worked, and that question became the paper's scientific core: a preregistered, controlled teardown of the framework's own scaffolding consisting of ten ablation arms against one-shot bandit nulls. The answer overturned the premise. Seven of ten loop arms fail to beat a bandit drawing samples from the same model and prompt, the full scaffold performs below blind sampling at 7-8x the token cost, and a floor-prompt arm with no framework at all wins on both score and value per token. On a held-out genomic benchmark, the best loop artifact and the best bandit draw agree to within 0.002, and the DALE algorithm from the initial experiment scored similarly. The convergent picture is that LLM agents do not reason their way past what training placed in the weights. Autoresearch, on this evidence, is sampling, selection, and remixing, which is the bet the Evolve-family architectures made when they chose populations and external selection over scratchpad reasoning, and why those choices were right.

♻ ☆ Hindsight Relabeling is All You Need for Reach-Avoid Learning NeurIPS 2026
Kevin Li, Marinka Zitnik
Offline goal-conditioned reinforcement learning methods have shown promise for reach-avoid tasks, where an agent must reach a target state while avoiding undesirable regions of the state space. Existing approaches typically encode avoid-region information into an augmented state space and cost function, which prevents flexible, dynamic specification of novel avoid-region information at evaluation time. They also rely heavily on meticulously designed reward and cost functions, limiting transferability to novel environments and specifications. We introduce RADT, a decision transformer approach for offline, reward-free, goal-conditioned, avoid-region-conditioned RL. RADT encodes goals and avoid-regions directly as prompt tokens, allowing any number of avoid-regions of arbitrary size to be specified at evaluation time. Using only suboptimal offline trajectories from a random policy, RADT can learn reach-avoid behavior in a completely data-driven manner using our novel avoid-region hindsight relabeling approach, without the need for reward/cost supervision. We benchmark RADT against existing offline goal-conditioned RL models across 17 tasks, environments, and experimental settings. RADT generalizes in a zero-shot manner to out-of-distribution avoid-region sizes and counts, outperforming baselines that require retraining. In one such zero-shot setting, RADT achieves 35.7% improvement in normalized cost over the best retrained baseline while maintaining high goal-reaching success. We also apply RADT to cell reprogramming in biology, demonstrating its versatility.

☆ Automatic Generation of Novel Highly-Functional Molecular Sequences
Vincent R. Ragusa, Chrisoph Adami
While multiple methods now exist to generate functional sequence variants from existing annotated examples, it is unclear how well such methods can be used to generate sequences that exceed the functionality of those contained in the example set. Using two complete fitness landscapes, we quantify how effectively generative tools can search sequence space and find rare, novel, highly functional sequences, focusing on variational auto-encoders and information-theoretic methods. We find that an information-theoretic method that does not require training and runs significantly faster than standard Machine-Learning/Artificial Intelligence (ML/AI) methods also is significantly more effective in finding rare highly-functional sequences. We discuss the application of this novel method to discover rare polypeptides with anti-bacterial properties that are specific to bacterial, but not eukaryotic, cell membranes.

☆ RangeFinder: Predicting Ligand Property Ranges from Protein Pockets NeurIPS 2026
Alan Nadelsticher Ruvalcaba, Chris Alvin, Supratik Mukhopadhyay, Adam Bess
Identifying ligands for a protein target requires searching a vast chemical space for a comparatively small set of compatible molecules. Existing machine learning approaches primarily address this problem by generating candidate ligands or scoring individual protein-ligand pairs, but provide limited information about the broader chemical space compatible with a binding pocket. We introduce RangeFinder, an SE(3)-equivariant transformer pocket-level model, trained and evaluated on BioLiP2 data, that predicts ranges over nine physicochemical ligand properties directly from pocket structure, chemistry, and evolutionary context. Using an intersection-over-union objective and evaluation metric (range-IoU), RangeFinder achieves a range-IoU of $0.464 \pm 0.002$, outperforming all deployable baselines while requiring only $12.2$ ms per pocket. We also quantify our method's ability to reduce the ligand search space while retaining compatible binders; in this regard, RangeFinder achieves a Reduction-Recall F-Score of $0.543 \pm 0.011$ --- once again outperforming all deployable baselines. Ablation experiments indicate that RangeFinder's performance depends more strongly on the density and quality of ligand supervision than on any individual input modality or specialized training strategy. Together, these results position RangeFinder as an inexpensive and effective molecular filtering prior, with demonstrated potential in narrowing the ligand search space of target pockets before docking, generation, or synthesis. The code for this project can be found at: https://github.com/alannadels/RangeFinder.

☆ ProteoCraft: Protein Design as Proposal-Verification Search
Fang Wu, Hongbin Lin, Bingchen Duan, Yuchen Wang, Li Erran Li, Yanjie Huang, Mingxuan Cao, Yijun Ma, Yunxiang Mo, Yanchao Li, Kuan Pang, Juangui Xu, Hanqun Cao, Dongwei Jiang, Xinyue Xu, Xiangru Tang, Zehong Wang, Jian Ma, Kejun Ying, Guanlue Li, Mingran Cao, Fanjin Wang, Tianfan Fu, Shuangjia Zheng, Yao Shu, Jure Leskovec, Yejin Choi
Protein-protein interactions underpin nearly all biological processes. Yet, designing proteins with desired interactions remains challenging due to the structural complexity governing molecular recognition. This study views protein design as a search optimization problem over a combinatorial sequence space under expensive structural evaluation. Toward this goal, we introduce a novel tree-based search framework, ProteoCraft, that employs approximate structural gradients from diffusion-based structure predictors as effective proposal policies to specify where the search should explore. Complete no-gradient inference of folding models separately assigns values to the resulting sequences, determining what is good. ProteoCraft allocates this verification budget through a coarse-to-fine paradigm: multi-site edits first explore distinct sequence basins, then verified promising states undergo strict single-site refinement. Across fourteen de novo binder-design settings spanning eight target proteins, five immune-relevant TCR-facing targets, and four localized CDR redesign tasks, ProteoCraft improves design quality and high-confidence yield. These improvements persist under independent AF2-IG and Rosetta-based interface evaluation. Prospective experimental validation further yielded an 82.9% binding hit rate for PD-L1 miniproteins, with a best measured KD of 0.879 nM, and confirmed binding of designed IFNAR2 VHH. Our code is available at https://github.com/smiles724/ProteoCraft_v1.
☆ El Agente Fármaco: A Scalable AI Scientist for Drug Discovery
Marcel Müller, Mohammad Ghazi Vakili, Yoshihiro Ogura, Ella Ashoori, Shimeng Wang, Alessandro S. Nascimento, Eddie Calleja, Yutong Zhao, Yimeng Liu, Ella Miray Rajaonson, Aiwei Yin, Jiaru Bai, Yunheng Zou, Abraham C. Stern, Varinia Bernales, Alán Aspuru-Guzik
Computational drug discovery offers a growing range of tools for proposing and evaluating molecules, but combining them and deciding what to do next still requires substantial expert effort. Here, we introduce El Agente Fármaco, an AI scientist that uses modern large language model (LLM) technology to turn discovery goals expressed in everyday language into complex, scalable computational screening workflows. The agentic harness selects scientific tools, reviews their results, and adjusts subsequent steps to the target and the user's objectives. Our approach centers on a shared scientific memory: Fármaco Atlas, which stores molecules, structures, and results together with their provenance -- a record of how they were obtained. The agent uses targeted database queries to retrieve the evidence needed for each decision. A single request can launch calculations for an entire collection of molecules, without requiring a separate LLM decision for every compound. This design limits the information the model must process as screening collections grow, while keeping the underlying results available for inspection and further analysis. The integrated tools support molecule generation, prediction of binding and molecular properties, and searches for compounds available for purchase. In a retrospective comparison with an expert-led campaign, the agent proposed chemically distinct candidates with competitive docking scores and higher calculated drug-likeness under explicitly imposed selection criteria. In a 22-task benchmark against plain tool calls and Codex, the agent achieved the highest observed mean model-judged score, with lower median cumulative token use and peak context. Fármaco provides a foundation for flexible, scalable drug discovery workflows that connect scientific reasoning with an enduring, traceable record of computational work.

♻ ☆ Bolek: A Multimodal Language Model for Molecular Reasoning
Frederic Grabowski, Jacek Szczerbiński, Maciej Jaśkowski, Kalina Jasińska-Kobus, Paweł Dąbrowski-Tumański, Tomasz Jetka, Bartosz Topolski
Molecular property models increasingly support high-stakes drug-discovery decisions, but their outputs are often difficult to audit: classical predictors return scores without rationale, while language models can produce fluent explanations weakly grounded in the input molecule. We introduce Bolek, a compact multimodal language model that grounds natural-language reasoning in molecular structure by injecting a Morgan fingerprint embedding into an instruction-tuned text decoder. Bolek is fine-tuned on molecular alignment tasks, including molecule description, RDKit descriptor prediction, and substructure detection, and on downstream reasoning over 15 TDC binary classification tasks using synthetic chains-of-thought anchored in concrete molecular features. Across these tasks, Bolek outperforms its Qwen3-4B-Instruct base on all endpoints in yes/no mode and on 13 of 15 in chain-of-thought mode, raising mean ROC/PR AUC from 0.55 to 0.76. It also outperforms TxGemma-9B-Chat on 13 of 15 binary classification tasks despite being less than half its size. Bolek's explanations are more grounded than those of the baseline LLMs: it cites numerical descriptors 10-100x more often per chain-of-thought, and the cited values agree strongly with RDKit for key descriptors such as TPSA, MolLogP, and MolWt (Spearman rho = 0.87-0.91). Generalisation extends beyond the training panel: on 15 unseen TDC classification endpoints, Bolek matches TxGemma on five, and it produces non-trivial rank correlations on three held-out regression endpoints despite never seeing downstream regression during training. These results suggest that targeted modality injection and reasoning supervision tied to verifiable molecular features can yield compact, auditable molecular reasoning models.

☆ From FAIR to AI-FAIR Metadata-First Infrastructure for AI-Enabled Science
Elizabeth G. Campolongo, Eric R. Sokol, Marty Downs, Gina Glenne, Gregory E. Maurer, Sydne Record, Dustin R. Rubenstein, Matthew J. Thompson, Christine Laney, Hilmar Lapp, Max Tarjan, Tanya Berger-Wolf
Artificial intelligence (AI) technologies and their capabilities have advanced rapidly over the past few years, leading to widespread interest and adoption in the biological and ecological sciences. However, the data infrastructure and computational resources that provide the foundation for AI workflows in ecology and biodiversity science remain fragmented. Moreover, the flow of value remains largely unidirectional: data move from collectors, through repositories and aggregators into models, and finally to research products or management actions. The derived value, such as corrected taxonomies, annotations, quality flags, and insights, rarely returns to source communities. We propose an extension of the original FAIR principles to address the needs of AI-ready infrastructure: the AI-FAIR metadata criteria, which place AI-enabled science in the context of a bidirectional Question-Data-Model triangle. This framework is designed to facilitate large-scale content-based analysis across diverse collections and ensure that the scientists generating the data benefit from the AI-enabled advances built on their data. Alongside these criteria, we introduce an agentic evaluation workflow that both rates existing datasets on the AI-FAIR and traditional FAIR criteria and provides actionable feedback where critical metadata is missing or incomplete. This modular dataset evaluation tool, paired with a domain-appropriate checklist, translates these principles into a reusable assessment of machine-learning readiness, AI-readiness for specified tasks, traceability, and ethical and CARE-aligned stewardship. Through this approach we aim to create a community-informed system to close the model data loop, so that AI also enriches data to further enable scientific discovery.

☆ Coupling cellular trajectories and RNA kinetics on continuous expression manifolds with ManifoldVelo
Zhen Zhou, Jiachen Li, Yuan Liu, Xiaoyong Pan, Hong-Bin Shen
Jointly recovering pseudotime and RNA velocity from static single-cell transcriptomes requires reconciling global trajectory structure with local expression changes, particularly in complex cycles and branching differentiation. Here we introduce ManifoldVelo, a topology-conditioned framework that jointly learns cellular coordinates, continuous RNA trajectories and regulatory kinetics. Derivatives of unspliced and spliced RNA trajectories are matched to transcription-splicing-degradation dynamics, including between observed cells, allowing expression reconstruction, cellular ordering and kinetic estimation to inform one another. ManifoldVelo recovers pseudotime across all evaluated single-cell trajectory types, including cyclic, multibranch and smooth non-branching trajectories. ManifoldVelo achieves the best pseudotime recovery among compared methods in multipeak cyclic simulations and the highest directional agreement for spliced RNA velocity in both multipeak cyclic and branching simulations. Real-data analyses support phase prediction across experimental plates and differentiation trajectories consistent with pancreatic developmental stages and marker expression. Its central advantage is the joint recovery of temporal position and local dynamics across distinct trajectory structures within a reusable dynamic reference. New cells are assigned pseudotime and velocity predictions from spliced RNA alone, without refitting the reference model. ManifoldVelo provides a foundation for dynamic cell atlases that transfer learned temporal and kinetic information to new samples.

☆ HistoTx: Early fusion of H&E images and spatial transcriptomics at varying spatial transcriptomics resolution with self-supervised learning ICML 2026
Alice Driessen, Emil Malmsten, Steffen Wolf, Bernd Illing, Isinsu Katircioglu, Adriano Martinelli, Rolf Stahel, Solange Peters, Enriqueta Felip, Sanjay Popat, Krisztian Homicsko, Raphael Gottardo, Marianna Rapsomaniki, Fei Tang
Spatial transcriptomics maps gene expression across tissue sections and, when integrated with whole slide images, jointly captures both morphological architecture and molecular activity to provide a rich, multimodal view of tissue biology. ML approaches combining spatial transcriptomics and WSIs typically learn a mapping from morphological features to molecular profiles via contrastive alignment or supervised fine-tuning of pathology foundation models. In both cases, transcriptomics serves as a training signal rather than a true input modality, leaving inference fundamentally image-driven. We propose an early-fusion vision transformer architecture in which transcript tokens are constructed at arbitrary spatial resolution, merged with image patch tokens, and jointly processed through a shared transformer stack for deep cross-modal interaction. Starting from a pretrained vision-only pathology foundation model, we train HistoTx by continuing self-supervised pretraining on paired image and spatial transcriptomics data and demonstrate that: 1) at inference with image only, performance remains on par with the baseline on most tasks while improving on tasks that inherently benefit from molecular knowledge, such as gene expression prediction; 2) when both modalities are available at inference, jointly providing image and transcript inputs outperforms either modality alone.

☆ A Scientific Claim Stability Framework for Evaluating MRI Morphometry Pipelines NeurIPS 2026
Duy-Cat Can, Dang-Duat Tran, Anh-Khang Nguyen, Quoc-Bao Tran, Trung-Hieu Do, Van-Tu Nguyen, Duy-Thanh Vu, Oliver Y. Chén, Hoang-Quynh Le
MRI morphometry pipelines are often evaluated by measurement agreement or downstream accuracy, yet their scientific value depends on the conclusions they support. Two pipelines can produce similar feature-level results yet support different conclusions about aging, cognitive decline, or neurodegeneration. We introduce Scientific Claim Stability (SCS), a claim-centric evaluation framework that converts region-of-interest (ROI) morphometry tables into auditable claim cards and evaluates their support under changes in dataset, processing pipeline, atlas, feature family, and target. Across 10,370 MRI scans from three datasets and five cohorts, SCS generates 2,783 claim cards and evaluates 89 claim-family pairs. The evaluation distinguishes stable, transferable, and fragile claims. FreeSurfer 8-FastSurfer comparisons show high evidence concordance and low anatomical-support overlap under the primary covariate specification. Acquisition-adjusted analyses show that the magnitude of support disagreement depends on the model specification. Structured review by specialists in geriatrics, neuroscience, and radiology, alongside auxiliary ChatGPT and Gemini reviewers, assesses claim plausibility, clarity, faithfulness, usefulness, and caution. SCS provides a reproducible way to audit the evidential stability of morphometry claims without assuming a universal gold standard.

☆ The DynaCell Evaluation Suite for Dynamic 3D Virtual Staining of Live Cells NeurIPS 2026
Alexandr A. Kalinin, Dihan Zheng, Taylla Milena Theodoro, Ivan Ivanov, Eduardo Hirata-Miyasaki, See-Chi Lee, Aofei Liu, Sricharan Reddy Varra, Talon Chandler, Soorya Pradeep, Chad Liu, Manuel D. Leonetti, Carolina Arias, Bo Huang, Shalin B. Mehta
Understanding how cells respond to perturbations over time is central to cell biology and drug discovery. Live-cell imaging captures the dynamics of perturbation-induced phenotypes in individual cells. While fluorescence imaging can visualize specific molecular phenotypes, labeling and repeated illumination often affect cell health. Virtual staining offers an alternative by predicting these fluorescence images from images acquired without fluorescent labels. Although many virtual staining models have been developed, their performance on volumetric time-lapse data remains poorly characterized. We address this gap with an evaluation suite for 3D time-lapse virtual staining of live cells. The suite combines paired 3D time-lapse images acquired with and without fluorescent labels, a set of baseline models, and three metric tiers measuring pixel-level fidelity, organelle segmentation performance, and single-cell phenotype similarity. Using this evaluation suite, we study metric and model behavior across 3 cell types, 2 microscopes, 4 organelles, and 3 perturbation states. We find that regression baselines better predict the spatial localization of target organelles, whereas the generative baseline better captures the distribution of phenotypes across cells. These differences expose trade-offs between structural and phenotypic fidelity that are obscured by single-metric evaluation and suggest how virtual staining models can be developed for diverse downstream tasks such as localization, segmentation, tracking, and phenotypic profiling. We release the curated datasets, baseline checkpoints, and evaluation code as part of DynaCell, a broader framework for image-based profiling of cell-state dynamics.

☆ Visualization and Characterization of Gene Group Distribution Patterns Across Genomes
Yanying Wu
Genes of the same family often lie close together on a chromosome, yet how far apart family members sit, and whether their spacing follows any pattern, has rarely been measured consistently across genomes. We built an interactive web application that places the members of any of the 1,396 FlyBase gene groups on the banded ideogram of the fruit fly (Drosophila melanogaster) and lists each member's neighbours. While browsing it, we found strikingly symmetric gene sets in which consecutive members are almost evenly spaced. In the RAB GTPases, two 5.08 Mb spacings differ by only 634 bp. We also found that distances between neighbouring members fall into two populations: short-range (median 1.8 kb) and long-range (median 2.4 Mb). Although an arm-matched random control suggests that the even spacing (without a minimum-distance filter) as well as the long-range population arise largely by chance, the short-range population turns out to be a genuine clustering signal. We then extended the analysis to human and six other model organisms (mouse, zebrafish, Caenorhabditis elegans, Saccharomyces cerevisiae, Arabidopsis thaliana and maize), and every genome showed the same two populations. The mixture curve of the two is nearly constant across chromosomes in some genomes (Type I: fly, maize, Arabidopsis, C. elegans) but varies across chromosomes in others (Type II: human, mouse, zebrafish, yeast). Thus, across eight eukaryotes, family members lie in two distance populations. The scale of the short-range population follows the spacing of genes in each genome, and the long-range population matches random placement in fly and is consistent with it in the other genomes. Our results also show that visual patterns such as even spacing must be tested against chance before they are interpreted. Our tool and approach give a simple way to explore and compare gene clustering across species.

☆ One Mistake, Three Layers: Persistence Rules Under Depth Correlation
Maryam Rahimimovassagh, Michael Brown, Sanjeda Sara Jennifer, Niloofar Yousefi
Probing frozen representations layer by layer is now standard practice, but reporting when a signal first becomes decodable requires a rule for reading a depth curve, and the obvious rule is wrong. On synthetic curves with known ground truth, requiring a probe score to exceed a null-calibrated threshold for three consecutive stages reports an onset nineteen stages early under transformer-like depth correlation, with every resample returning an onset. The failure follows from correlation between neighboring layers, a hazard known in neuroimaging but not, to our knowledge, checked for depth curves. We give an estimator whose error rate is calibrated rather than assumed. Null trajectories are built by permuting labels within each evaluation unit and holding each permutation fixed across depth, preserving the correlation structure the rule must survive; thresholds are per-layer order statistics; and the persistence requirement is calibrated rather than chosen. Applied to pocket information in a frozen co-folding trunk, stratifying 1,820 post-cutoff targets by protein-family and pocket familiarity, the five-stage rule fires on 4 to 7 per cent of null trajectories against a nominal 5 per cent and the three-stage rule on 12 to 17: the synthetic failure reproduces on real data. Pocket membership is decodable above threshold at all 48 blocks in all four regimes, so the onset cannot discriminate them; the pre-declared accumulation depth does, and is latest for familiar families with structurally novel pockets in every feature set. That regime is also the weakest, by a margin surviving controls for length, pocket measure and alignment depth. Separately, a widely used public co-folding checkpoint loads against its own code with 128 trunk parameters missing; those would be randomly initialized, and every representation extracted would be partly noise while appearing normal.
☆ BEND 2.0: Benchmarking the Capabilities Needed to Design DNA
Andreas Bjerregaard, Rachael DeVries, Dionysia Danai Brilli, Panagiotis Antoniadis, Yan Li, Anders Krogh, Wouter Boomsma, Ole Winther, Frederikke Isa Marin
Genomic foundation models are increasingly used to propose DNA sequences and edits. But successful design requires capabilities that current benchmarks do not measure. Here we introduce BEND 2.0 (BENchmark for DNA language models), which tests how well models predict the effects of DNA changes and whether these predictions can guide sequence design. Across eight tasks built on five experimental datasets, we find that model choice and how representations are accessed both affect performance. Linear readouts fitted to frozen representations can match or exceed models' native outputs, and functional post-training substantially improves the models' capabilities. Linear readouts transfer well across experimental assays and genes, suggesting that models capture functional patterns beyond the data used to fit individual predictors. At the same time, strong prediction of average effects can conceal weaker prediction of context-specific effects, and accurate prediction of combined mutation effects does not imply accurate prediction of their interactions. In design tasks, model predictions can reduce the number of candidates needed to find a desired response, but still leave substantial room for better design choices, particularly for context-specific and multi-cell objectives. Our benchmark reveals that genomic models now display signs of general utility but have yet to saturate on performance in even simple tasks.

♻ ☆ ORBIT-FMIB: Tracking Order-Resolved Epistatic Information Through ESM-2
Maryam Rahimimovassagh, Waldemar Karwowski, Ivan Garibay, Niloofar Yousefi
Protein foundation models support mutation-effect and structural prediction, but predictive performance alone does not reveal which forms of biological interaction information remain accessible through model depth. We ask whether ESM-2 retains higher-order epistatic information as strongly as first- and second-order information across its representation hierarchy, introducing ORBIT-FMIB, a diagnostic framework combining Walsh-based interaction decomposition with subset-conditioned neural dependence estimation. The method is validated on synthetic landscapes with known interaction structure before being applied to the dense four-site GB1 fitness landscape using frozen ESM-2 representations. An initial production run suggested ESM-2 retains higher-order epistatic information less well than lower-order information ($Δ_{\mathrm{HO-LO}}=-0.107$). An independent replication of the complete measurement grid, under matched GPU hardware and identical critic seeds, substantially reduced this contrast ($Δ_{\mathrm{HO-LO}}=-0.017$), and its sign was unstable across otherwise-defensible evaluation-pairing choices applied to the same trained critics ($-0.011$ to $+0.015$). We therefore do not currently have robust evidence that ESM-2 selectively loses higher-order epistatic information, nor that retention is equal across orders; the directional question remains open. The measurement protocol itself, including its documented removal of a positional-subset shortcut in pooled critics, remains validated and is unaffected by this finding. ORBIT-FMIB is offered as a diagnostic framework for probing interaction structure in protein foundation models; this study's own replication result illustrates why such probing requires adequately-powered reproducibility checks before its output is treated as a biological finding.

☆ Comparative Strategies for Handling Imbalanced QSAR Regression: A Case Study on hERG Activity Prediction
Ming-Yen Sung, Hsin Liu, Tung-Sheng Cho, Cheng-Wei Lee, Yufeng J. Tseng
Data imbalance frequently occurs in QSAR modeling, yet most studies focus on classification tasks, and few address regression tasks, as it is challenging to determine which regions of the data are sparse or dense. Data-level and algorithm-level imbalance-handling strategies can have different effects on model performance. In this study, we systematically compare various strategies across GCN and fingerprint-based machine learning models. To evaluate model performance more meaningfully, we partitioned the target region into several subregions based on both domain knowledge and data distribution. We focused on the region of greatest practical interest. Our results demonstrate that selecting models based on this interest-region metric leads to more reliable performance than using the total region metric. We also observed that different imbalance-handling strategies can have divergent effects depending on the model type. These findings highlight the importance of region-aware evaluation in regression QSAR modeling and provide practical guidance for selecting appropriate strategies for imbalanced datasets.
☆ Stand-In Effects and Pooling Amplification: Design-Based Tests of Whether Biological World Models Use Actions
Bowen Xu, Boyu Chen
Action-conditioned world models are increasingly trained on data from randomized experiments, and whether their predictions respond to actions is usually checked by replacing or removing the actions and measuring the loss in accuracy. We show that such diagnostics report action effects where there are none: under autocorrelated designs, future actions stand in for history the model does not see, and a better model learns the stand-in better. When the design law is known, a conditional randomization test written as an e-value is valid in finite samples whatever the model, provided the resampler conditions on the full state the design used; resampling from a simpler law reproduces the false effect. We identify a second failure, specific to world models: when many sites share one action sequence (cells in a microscope field, pixels in a frame), the natural pooled score turns small model errors along the action contrast into overwhelming evidence against a true effect. In a pixel world, its median log e-value fell from $-17.3$ to $-6832$ as frames grew from 64 to 4096 pixels, while the oracle's rose. Tempering the score by a design effect estimated on training data changes the bet, not the validity. Our own preregistered analysis of optogenetic ERK experiments failed in exactly this way; a tempered statistic, selected on synthetic data and registered before the data were obtained, then rejected the null on a never-opened field ($E\approx 1.1\times10^{10}$). Every analysis is preregistered, fixed in a dated protocol before it was run, or labelled exploratory, and every reported result is generated from hashed result files.

☆ Cell lines that share a well are not independent observations in the Tahoe-100M drug screen
Xuanrui Huang
Pooled drug screens treat and sequence dozens of cancer cell lines together in one well. Tahoe-100M, the largest, is used to train and benchmark models that predict how unmeasured cell lines respond to drugs, and its benchmarks, reproducibility ceilings and statistical tests treat the cell lines of a well, and random halves of a well's cells, as independent observations of a drug's effect. Because the drug is applied to the well, this is harmless only if the lines of a well share nothing beyond the drug's reproducible effect. We tested this with pairs of untreated control wells on 13 plates, with replicate wells whose labels we checked, and with two plates that repeat each other well by well. In each test the cell lines of a well share a component of expression that a replicate well does not reproduce, also when the two wells occupy the same inferred position; where drugs are present, it cannot be told apart from an interaction of plate and drug, but in untreated wells it can. In replicate wells at different positions whose labels pass the check, about a third of what two halves of a well have in common (35%, 95% interval 22-58%, 9 compounds) is not reproduced by the replicate well. The well effect inflates results that take something from a well and judge it in the same well: retrained versions of two published models that take a measured response as input lose about a quarter to a third of their score when the input comes from a replicate well instead of the predicted well (22-43% under the control-well reference), halves of a well overstate how well the experiment reproduces, and a test across the lines of a well calls 5 of 13 plates significant when two untreated wells are compared. Many labels of strongly responding replicate wells, those of plate 13 above all, fail the label check. Evaluations, ceilings and tests on such data should treat the well as the experimental unit.

☆ Accurate automated photo-identification of Near Eastern and European fire salamanders
Maayan Yesharim, Hava Goldstein, Gabi Kolodny, Assaf Ben-Levy, Tamar Kis-Papo, Dor Gavish, Rotem Vidan, Nirit Lavie-Alon, Olga Rybak, Yoav Ram
Photo-identification underpins non-invasive capture-recapture monitoring of amphibians, but manual identification becomes slow and error-prone as reference libraries grow. Focusing on the Near Eastern fire salamander (Salamandra infraimmaculata), we developed a pipeline based on zero-shot local-feature matching (SuperPoint and LightGlue) without species-specific training and evaluated it on $2{,}440$ field photographs from Haifa and Tel Dan in northern Israel. We isolated the yellow spot pattern, scored image similarity by the number of high-confidence keypoint matches, which reduced a confound whereby many-spotted individuals accumulate spurious matches, and assigned identities across the database by two-tier clustering. Pipeline-assisted expert review corrected misidentifications, reducing the number of identities by almost a quarter. On held-out individuals, the pipeline ranked the correct individual first for $95.2\%$ of queries and recovered identities with an ARI of $0.958$. Without modification, it reached $95.1\%$ and $91.7\%$ top-1 accuracy on two datasets of the European fire salamander (Salamandra salamandra) and recognised $93.7\%$ of recaptures in one of them, compared with $38$-$77\%$ for five established programs and $93.9\%$ for visual inspection. Zero-shot local-feature matching thus offers amphibian monitoring a re-identification tool that needs no training data, transfers between related species, scales to databases of over a thousand individuals, and can audit existing databases before capture-recapture analysis.
☆ From Tool State to Scientific Action: Auditing Decision Provenance in Biomedical Research Agents NeurIPS 2026
Xingyu Yi, Xinya Zhang, Yunshen Zhou, Haoyu Bao

♻ ☆ Establishing the Minimal Clinically Important Difference (MCID) for Smartphone-Derived Gait Measures in Multiple Sclerosis
Mike D Rinderknecht, Bernhard Fehlmann, Dimitar Stanev, Cedric Simillion, Ernst Bos, Letizia Leocani, Agne Kazlauskaite, Gary Cutter, Helmut Butzkueven, Licinio Craveiro
Background: Digital health technologies allow for frequent, remote gait monitoring in people with multiple sclerosis (MS). However, to differentiate daily variability from actual disease progression in longitudinal data, established minimal clinically important differences (MCID) are required. Currently, there is limited literature defining these thresholds for digital gait metrics. Objective: To establish MCIDs for digital gait measures reflecting progression in MS. Methods: Digital gait measures were captured via daily, remote, smartphone-based Two-Minute Walk Tests in CONSONANCE (NCT03523858), a phase 3b study of ocrelizumab in progressive MS. Using an anchor-based approach, median changes from baseline at Week 96 on digital gait measures were computed for patients showing clinically meaningful worsening on either Timed 25-Foot Walk, Ambulation Score, Expanded Disability Status Scale, or 12-item Multiple Sclerosis Walking Scale. These changes were subsequently triangulated to derive the MCID estimates. Results: 243 patients with progressive MS (female: n=125 (51%); mean [SD] age: 49.3 [9.3]; mean [SD] EDSS: 4.8 [1.4]) had digital gait data available at baseline and Week 96. Median changes were generally consistent across anchors. Triangulated MCIDs are: Step Velocity = -0.16 m/s, Step Velocity Scaled to Walking Time = -0.18 m/s, Step Duration = 0.06 s, Step Length = -0.07 m, Total Number of Steps = -28, and Total Distance Walked = -24 m. Conclusion: These MCIDs provide a framework for interpreting meaningful gait changes and integrating digital measures into MS outcome evaluation. Beyond facilitating novel clinical trial endpoints to evaluate treatment efficacy, they enable objective, real-world monitoring to advance personalized patient care.
♻ ☆ Reading Between the Reads: A Decade On, Revisiting Sequencing Protocol Annotation in the Sequence Read Archive
Jamie J. Alnasir
Background: Experimental metadata are essential for interpreting archived sequencing data. In 2015, Alnasir and Shanahan examined the annotation of fragmentation, adapter ligation and enrichment in the Sequence Read Archive (SRA). This study revisits those measurements using the original SQL keyword definitions on a September 2026 NCBI metadata snapshot reconstructed in the historical SRAdb schema. Results: The contemporary database contained 41,593,302 experiments, compared with 419,620 in 2013. In library_construction_protocol, the proportion matching all three historical keyword groups declined from 4.06% to 1.42%. The protocol field was NULL in 67.65% of contemporary experiments. Searching design_description alongside the protocol field increased combined three-step keyword detection to 1,197,367 experiments (2.88%), with most additional matches occurring where the protocol field was NULL. Coverage varied across sequencing library strategies, ranging from 0.26% for AMPLICON to 7.69% for RNA-Seq. Independent comparison of 150 sampled experiment records against source XML confirmed agreement in all historical keyword classifications. Conclusions: The original criteria identify a smaller proportion of experiments in the designated protocol field than a decade earlier. Additional preparation-related terminology is detectable in general experiment descriptions, exposing the limitations of searches restricted to one metadata field. Differences among sequencing strategies complicate historical comparisons as preparation methods evolve. The findings support more consistent protocol annotation and the need to distinguish keyword occurrence from the adequacy of experimental documentation.

♻ ☆ How to make the most of your masked language model for protein engineering NeurIPS 2026

A plethora of protein language models have been released in recent years. Yet comparatively little work has addressed how to best sample from them to optimize desired biological properties. We fill this gap by proposing a flexible, effective sampling method for masked language models (MLMs), and by systematically evaluating models and methods both in silico and in vitro on actual antibody therapeutics campaigns. ☆ Canonic hemoglobin-oxygen equilibrium: Reassessing the role of cooperativity
A. E. Allahverdyan, S. G. Gevorkian, A. Harutyunyan, Y. Sh. Mamasakhlisov, V. Stepanyan
Hemoglobin-oxygen equilibrium is normally studied within the grand canonical ensemble, which assumes that each hemoglobin is immersed in a reservoir of unbound oxygen molecules of the plasma. We show that this assumption is incorrect inside the RBC (red blood cell or erythrocyte), where the hemoglobin concentration is larger than the concentration of unbound oxygen in plasma. We suggest a better model, where a single hemoglobin and a few oxygen molecules around it reach a canonical equilibrium at a fixed volume (determined from the RBC structure) and a fixed temperature. The basic models of hemoglobin-oxygen equilibrium -- Pauling's model and Monod-Wyman-Changeux model -- can be reformulated for this canonical situation. They predict cooperative interaction energies, which are significantly lower than predictions of the same models in the grand canonical ensemble. Larger cooperative energies, in particular those predicted by the grand canonical ensemble, lead to instabilities (cascade processes) in oxygen release within the canonical approach. Oxygen-binding fluctuations within this approach are sizably smaller than those in the grand canonical situation. These results suggest that the hopping diffusion of oxygen from one hemoglobin to another may play a role in oxygen diffusion within the RBC.

☆ PeptSol: A Translational Benchmark for Protected-Peptide Solubility in Drug Manufacturing NeurIPS 2026
Alexander Arjun Lobo, Marc Saouda, Urvi Awasthi, Leonid Zhukov
Liquid-phase peptide synthesis (LPPS) offers a scalable route to peptide manufacturing, but route development still depends on empirical solvent screens because each protected intermediate must be soluble for reaction and recoverable for purification. The protecting groups, terminal modifications, and noncanonical residues that govern this behaviour are poorly represented in existing peptide property benchmarks, limiting the ability to develop and rigorously evaluate molecular representation models for LPPS-relevant prediction tasks. We introduce PeptSol, an open benchmark of 1388 hand-curated protected-peptide solubility measurements with chemistry-aware splits. The measurements span only 84 parent sequences and 101 protected structures, and 92.5% come from a single historical programme. No foundation model consistently outperforms a baseline combining chemical fingerprints with explicit molecular descriptors, and model rankings vary across prediction tasks and data splits. The dataset, evaluation protocol, and baselines establish a standard for measuring progress on molecular representations for synthesis-aware peptide developability.
