♻ ☆ Machine-Learned Compact Subspace Generation for Quantum Selected Configuration Interaction within Density Matrix Embedding Framework
Ashish Kumar Patra, Anurag K. S. V., Ruchika Bhat, Sai Shankar P., Rahul Maitra, Jaiganesh G
Sample-based quantum diagonalization (SQD), an extension of quantum selected configuration interaction (QSCI), is a promising hybrid quantum-classical paradigm for molecular ground-state energies. By replacing variational optimization with quantum sampling, QSCI avoids barren plateaus and directly reconstructs correlated wavefunctions. However, existing configuration recovery mainly enforces symmetry constraints without targeting the most physically relevant configurations, often yielding oversized subspaces and costly classical diagonalization. We introduce QSCI-RBM, a compact subspace generation protocol based on Restricted Boltzmann Machines (RBMs), and integrate it within Density Matrix Embedding Theory (DMET). The RBM learns the distribution of dominant determinants from hardware samples, generating high-probability configurations. Sampling was performed on IBM Heron R1 (\texttt{ibm\_torino}) for CH$_5$NO, R2 (\texttt{ibm\_fez}) for C$_2$H$_4$, and R3 (\texttt{ibm\_boston}) for the larger H$_2$O benchmark and the protein-ligand system. After validating on these benchmarks of increasing active-space size, we simulate the inhibitor Carmofur bound to the SARS-CoV-2 main protease (M\textsuperscript{pro}), fragmented into ten DMET impurities. Both DMET-QSCI-RBM and a DMET-SQD baseline reach chemical-potential self-consistency within four $μ$-iterations. At the converged chemical potential, DMET-QSCI-RBM lies within $0.05\,\mathrm{mHa}$ of DMET-FCI while accessing on average only $0.22\%$ of the symmetry-preserving configuration space, versus $0.55\,\mathrm{mHa}$ at $33.0\%$ for DMET-SQD, leading to a $\sim$153-fold subspace reduction. RBM-assisted expansion thus yields far more compact hardware-derived determinant spaces at equal or better accuracy, reducing classical overhead for scalable quantum embedding of biological systems on real hardware.

♻ ☆ Spontaneous Mutations from Terahertz Proton Tunneling
Noah Bray-Ali
Protons in the bonds between base pairs of the double helix store the code of life by breaking the dyad symmetry that swaps the sense strand with its complementary partner. When these bonds break during replication and transcription, pairs of protons switch sides, restoring dyad symmetry and destroying genetic information. The observed rate of such spontaneous mutations is obtained in the sudden approximation for bond breaking provided protons in bonds between bases tunnel across the gap with terahertz frequency.

QM 7
☆ FACET: Feasibility-Automaton Complete Enumeration and Truncation-Free Scoring of Molecular Formulas from MS/MS
Xujun Che, Depeng Xu, Xiuxia Du
Determining the neutral molecular formula of a precursor ion from its tandem mass (MS/MS) spectrum is the first step in identifying an unknown small molecule. Existing methods trade recall, whether the correct formula is among the candidates, against the cost of examining each candidate. Methods that examine each candidate in depth must first shorten the list, by limiting element counts or truncating it, and that is where the correct formula is lost. End-to-end regressors are fast but guarantee neither validity nor recall. We present FACET, which separates deciding which formulas to consider from deciding which one to choose. A feasibility automaton builds a formula one element count at a time and admits exactly the formulas that fit the precursor mass and satisfy the ring-and-double-bond and valence constraints. Run as an exhaustive search, it enumerates the complete feasible set, so every candidate is valid and recall is fixed by the chemical constraints rather than by a trained model. A small neural scoring head then ranks every feasible formula from one pass of a pretrained spectrum encoder, so no candidate is discarded. On the NPLIB1 benchmark FACET reaches top-1 accuracies of $0.859$ with the adduct known and $0.729$ with it unknown, compared with $0.766$ and $0.660$ for the strongest established method, and its recall is the highest. On the external CASMI 2022 set its recall is $1.000$, and with the adduct unknown it is the most accurate of the methods compared.
☆ Can agents reliably design therapeutic antibodies?
Asli Alpman, Nick Bhattacharya, Aaron Solomon, Hunter Elliott
Recent work has demonstrated agents driving protein design workflows, either through orchestration of specialized tools or direct manipulation of sequences. Yet the fidelity with which agents follow protein design instructions and the factors contributing to their effectiveness remain understudied. We examine agentic antibody design under two settings: when explicit molecular-editing instructions are provided and when agents receive only previously-scored sequences and are prompted to propose improvements. We call the first task-guided and the second black-box design. For the task-guided setting, we curate ten expert-validated affinity-improvement tasks and show that LLM judges closely reproduce programmatic evaluations of instruction following. We then study two augmentations: access to antibody-relevant tools and to previously scored design history. Tools improve instruction following (IF) and sequence space exploration while reducing optimization performance, whereas design history reduces IF but improves optimization performance at the expense of exploration. Finally, we combine taskguided and black-box design within an in silico optimization loop and show that our agentic optimizer performs comparably to an established Bayesian optimization baseline.
☆ Governing Equations of Human Sleep Macro-Structure:Symbolic Discovery from 1,106 Nights
Junhui Gao
Human sleep is organized into a small number of cycles per night: deep sleep dominates early cycles, REM lengthens toward morning, and light sleep clusters at cycle boundaries. These regularities have long been described qualitatively but never distilled into closed-form laws from large-scale data. A total of 1,106 nights (Sleep-EDF 153 + SHHS 953) are segmented into REM-anchored cycles, and four cycle-indexed statistics follow simple closed-form equations: cycle length declines nearly linearly (R^2=0.996), REM duration follows an inverted U (R^2=0.873), deep-sleep (N3) run length decays exponentially (R^2=0.987) with a time constant of 2.94 cycles, consistent with the homeostatic Process S of the two-process model, and light-sleep (N1) run length follows a U shape (R^2=0.956), confirming a "long-short-long" pattern across the night. The laws are reproduced by per-night differentiable fitting (142/142 and 773/773 nights converge), by unconstrained symbolic regression (PySR), and by a 500-night generative synthesizer built from them. Their robustness is established against the two main methodological threats: bootstrap CIs computed by resampling whole nights (1,000 replications) exclude zero for every shape term, and a five-level scan of the REM-merge segmentation threshold preserves every qualitative form. These appear to be the first closed-form laws governing the macro-structure of sleep, distilled and tested across an age-mixed sample (healthy young adults and community-based older adults).

♻ ☆ Flow Matching for Count Data
Ganchao Wei, John Pearson
High-dimensional count data arise in applications such as single-cell RNA sequencing and neural spike trains, where mappings between distributions across successive batches or time points form critical components of data analysis. The recent success of diffusion- and flow-based deep generative models for images, video, and text motivates extending these ideas to count-valued settings, but many existing methods either treat each count as a categorical state or transform counts into a continuous space, neither of which is natural or efficient when the count range is large. We propose count-FM, a flow-matching framework for count data based on a continuous-time birth-death process with local unit jumps. Count-FM learns marginal transitions efficiently in count space through simulation-free training of conditional transition rates, allowing transport between arbitrary count-distributed source and target populations. In simulation, count-FM variants achieve strong sample quality while using substantially fewer parameters. We further apply count-FM to scRNA-seq and neural spike-train data for unconditional generation, transport, and conditional generation. Across these tasks, count-FM yields improved sample quality, greater modeling efficiency, and interpretable transport paths.
♻ ☆ Static and Dynamic Functional Brain Networks Reveal Distinct Connectivity Alterations Across the Alzheimer's Disease Spectrum
Nicolas Rubido, Venia Batziou, Marwan Fuad, Vesna Vuksanovic
Brain network analysis using functional MRI has advanced our understanding of cortical activity and its disruption in neurodegenerative disorders underlying dementia. Dynamic (time-varying) brain networks, which capture spatial and temporal patterns of regional cortical co-activity, remain relatively unexplored across the Alzheimer's disease (AD) spectrum. We analysed age- and sex-matched static and dynamic functional brain networks from resting-state fMRI in 315 individuals with AD, mild cognitive impairment (MCI), and cognitively normal healthy controls (HC) from ADNI-3. Networks were constructed using the Juelich brain atlas, with static connectivity estimated from full time series and dynamic connectivity via a sliding-window approach. Group differences were assessed at link and node levels using non-parametric statistics and bootstrap resampling. Time series from regions identified in these analyses were then used in Random Forest classification to test whether the alterations carried discriminative information at the individual level. HC and MCI showed largely similar node-level static and dynamic connectivity, whereas AD exhibited pronounced alterations in network organisation and temporal variability. Stable (stationary) differences emerged in connectivity between white matter regions and parietal and somatosensory cortices, whereas temporally varying differences consistently involved the amygdala and hippocampal formation. Node centrality analysis suggested that white matter connectivity differences are predominantly local. Our results highlight shared and unique connectivity patterns in static and dynamic networks, emphasising the importance of incorporating dynamic information into brain network analyses of the Alzheimer's spectrum.
♻ ☆ AnySleep: a channel-agnostic deep learning system for high-resolution sleep staging in multi-center cohorts
Niklas Grieger, Jannik Raskob, Siamak Mehrkanoon, Stephan Bialonski
Sleep is essential for health, yet studying its dynamics requires manual sleep staging, a labor-intensive step in research and clinical care. Across centers, polysomnography (PSG) recordings are traditionally scored in 30-s epochs for pragmatic, not physiological, reasons and vary in electrode count, montage, and subject characteristics. These constraints challenge harmonized multi-center studies and the discovery of robust biomarkers on shorter timescales. We present AnySleep, a deep neural network that scores sleep from any electroencephalography (EEG) or electrooculography (EOG) data at adjustable temporal resolutions. We trained and validated the model on over 20,000 overnight recordings (> 200,000 h of EEG and EOG) from 28 datasets across multiple clinics to promote robust generalization across sites. The model attains state-of-the-art performance and surpasses or equals established baselines at 30-s epochs. Performance improves with more channels, yet remains strong when EOG is absent, or only EOG or single EEG derivations (frontal, central, or occipital) are available. On sub-30-s timescales, the model captures short wake intrusions consistent with arousals and improves the prediction of pathophysiological conditions (obstructive sleep apnea, narcolepsy type 1, insomnia) over 30-s scoring. We make the model publicly available to facilitate large-scale studies with heterogeneous electrode setups and accelerate biomarker discovery in sleep.

♻ ☆ Quantifying structural uncertainty in chemical reaction network inference
Yong See Foo, Adriana Zanca, Jennifer A. Flegg, Ivo Siekmann
Dynamical systems in biology are complex, and one often does not have comprehensive knowledge about the interactions involved. Chemical reaction network (CRN) inference aims to identify, from observing species concentrations over time, the unknown reactions between the species. Existing approaches such as sparse regularisation largely focus on identifying a single, most likely CRN, without addressing uncertainty about the network structure. However, it is important to quantify structural uncertainty to have confidence in our inference and predictions. In this work, we explore how effective sparse regularisation methods are for quantifying structural uncertainty. Locally optimal solutions to sparse regularisation are mapped to CRN structures; however, it is unclear whether this approach encompasses all plausible CRNs. We find that inducing sparsity with nonconvex penalty functions results in better coverage of the plausible CRNs compared to the popular lasso regularisation. To validate our approach, we apply our methods to real-world data examples, and are able to simultaneously recover reactions proposed across multiple literature sources for a reaction system. Our emphasis on network-level probabilities enables a novel, hierarchical representation of structural ambiguities in the space of CRNs. This representation translates into alternative reaction pathways suggested by the available data, thus guiding the efforts of future experimental design.

☆ Physiological Trajectories and Observation Patterns in ICU Mortality Prediction: Transportability Under Changing Measurement Conditions
Benjamin T. Pike
Objective: To distinguish the transferable predictive value of physiological trajectories from that of observation patterns in ICU mortality prediction, and to assess whether measurement timing affects performance independently of observation quantity. Materials and Methods: In-hospital mortality was predicted at 48 hours using MIMIC-III, MIMIC-IV, and eICU. Nested representations progressively integrated latest-value snapshots (R1), longitudinal physiological summaries (R2), and measurement frequency and timing features (R3). Models fitted on 7,762 MetaVision patients were evaluated on 21,682 MIMIC-IV patients and 43,878 eICU patients across 204 hospitals. The effect of measurement timing was assessed while holding the number of retained observations constant. Results: Physiological trajectories improved external prediction in eICU (R2-R1 log-loss difference -0.00922, 95% CI -0.01148 to -0.00704). Observation-pattern features improved locally developed eICU models (R3-R2 -0.00257, 95% CI -0.00370 to -0.00146), but not clearly after transfer from MetaVision (-0.00012, 95% CI -0.00206 to +0.00208). R3 was favoured in 10 of 30 source bootstrap refits. At matched observation counts, retaining measurements spread throughout the window reduced log loss relative to random retention by 0.03280 for R3 versus 0.00703 for R2. Removing the final 12 hours increased log loss relative to count-matched random removal by 0.01947 for R3 versus 0.00822 for R2. Under reduced availability, the local R3 increment changed from beneficial to harmful. Discussion: Physiological trajectories transferred more consistently, while observation-pattern features were particularly sensitive to the timing and availability of measurements. Conclusion: Clinical prediction models should be evaluated not only in new patient populations but also under the measurement conditions that supply their inputs.

☆ A richness-sensitive Acoustic Biodiversity Index from species-level detections: classifier benchmarking, false-positive control and validation against standard soundscape indices in an African savanna and an urban zoo
Muhammad Saeed, Dmitry Mikhaylov, Mohamed H. Alhosani, Yasser F. Al Wahedi, Rory Pilossof, Ahmed Arabi, Walid Shaaba
Acoustic indices are widely used as rapid proxies for biodiversity, but they summarise the distribution of sound energy rather than the species producing it and often track richness poorly. We revise and test an Acoustic Biodiversity Index (ABI) built from species-level detections. Its primary form, ABI_E, is Shannon diversity of detections normalised by the regional species pool expected at the site and season (H / ln S_ref); temporal stability T is reported as a separate component. We first benchmarked four classifiers on a strongly labelled tropical dataset (1,216 calls, 24 classes) with recording-grouped cross-validation. BirdNET embeddings + logistic regression performed best (macro-F1 0.750 +/- 0.029), and random frame-level splits inflated accuracy from 0.41 to 0.70. We then applied BirdNET v2.4 with a location and season filter to recordings from a riverine savanna in the Zambezi Valley, Zimbabwe, and from three habitats at Emirates Park Zoo and Resort, Abu Dhabi, United Arab Emirates. A stricter post-hoc location filter removed 517 high-confidence detections of 9 implausible species, all at the zoo. After diel-matched rarefaction to equal effort (6 h), the savanna site held 21 species (Hill number 1D = 9.83; ABI_E = 0.38) against 1.3-13 species (ABI_E = 0.03-0.21) at the zoo sites. Across 149 recorded hours, ABI_E correlated strongly with richness (Spearman rho = 0.93), whereas six standard indices did not (best: ACI, rho = 0.48). The stability term was lower at the savanna site (0.39) than at the zoo (0.65-0.67), showing that it penalises the natural diel cycle of bird activity. Species-level indices with explicit false-positive control, a declared regional reference pool and effort standardisation provide a more interpretable biodiversity indicator than energy-based acoustic indices.

☆ A two-stage diffusion-driven morphogenesis model with evolving connectivity
Alexander Safonov
Morphogenesis of diatom silica shells involves a sequence of topological transitions: initially separate ribs grow and branch, then merge through bridges, leaving a regular network of pores. Existing models based on diffusion-limited aggregation or reaction-diffusion reproduce either branching or pore formation, but not both within a single framework. Here we propose a two-stage model of diffusion-driven morphogenesis with evolving connectivity. The model is formulated on a fixed finite element mesh with a density field on an active layer. Stage 1 reproduces dendritic growth, where a stimulus based on the local diffusive flux exceeds a threshold. Stage 2 uses an inverted stimulus, where high local flux suppresses precipitation, stabilizing pores between the dendrites. The update rules are analogous to sensitivity in density-based topology optimization and to strain energy density in bone remodeling. The model predicts distinct morphological regimes on each stage, controlled by the respective thresholds. For a reference calculation, the predicted pore network is regular, with approximately uniform spacing, and pores are absent near the domain edges, consistent with observations on diatom valves. The model provides a unified framework for describing both stages of silica morphogenesis and suggests a route for comparing computational predictions with cryo-electron tomography data.

☆ Co$^2$ODE: Conditionally Coarse-Grained Model Discovery for Rewiring in Biochemical Networks
Theodore de Pomereu, Fabian Fröhlich
Biochemical networks propagate the signals that regulate cellular behaviour, and which routes carry them depends on cellular context: a shift in context rewires the network's effective dynamics. Mechanistic simulators are misspecified here because the effective dynamics emerge from an interaction structure that is only partially known. Model discovery instead learns the dynamics from data, but sparse, correlated measurements poorly identify a full network model. What matters is narrower: the portion the context rewires. We introduce conditionally coarse-grained ODEs (Co$^2$ODE), which model that portion as a sparse neural or symbolic ODE: a conditional mask selects the markers carrying the rewiring, and a variational autoencoder compresses the remaining markers into a learnt closure term. On single-cell phosphoproteomic data spanning 30 cancer-relevant overexpressions, Co$^2$ODE compresses a 30-marker panel into a 10-dimensional state while matching the predictive accuracy of a neural ODE fit to the full panel. The closure absorbs what the mask drops, hence the accuracy is insensitive to which markers are kept. Adding a prior score to the loss shifts the selected markers at no cost in fit, indicating the data are not interventional enough to identify the underlying biology.
☆ Recurrence in Sequence Space: How Epistasis Mimics Shared Ancestry
Pietro Chiarantoni, Mina Mahboubi, Jason Lamanna, Vincenzo Carnevale
Sequences in a protein family do not explore the sequence space freely: folding, packing, and binding couple residues, so the set of viable sequences is irreducibly correlated, a phenomenon referred to as epistasis. We show that such correlations lower the \emph{intrinsic dimension} $d_{\rm IN}$ of sequence space, counteracting the concentration of measure and broadening the distribution of pairwise distances. Consequentially, they make it likely that independently evolving lineages \emph{recur}, approaching one another by chance long after they last shared an ancestor. Using Kac's lemma we show that the logarithmic mean recurrence time grows linearly with the intrinsic dimension, $\log τ_{\rm rec} \propto {d_{\rm IN}}$ for typical sequences, so strongly constrained (low-dimensional) families recur often and generate sequence similarity that carries no genealogical signal. We establish this first in a solvable Ising-like model with tunable correlation, then in a real protein family using Potts models and Markov chain Monte Carlo, where $d_{\rm IN}$ remains predictive of recurrence even in multi-basins landscapes. Because recurrence is a property of the evolutionary process rather than of any estimator, it bounds the recoverability of deep history for distance-based and likelihood methods alike.
♻ ☆ Open-Source Framework for the Visualization, Quantification, and Analysis of 4D Flow and High-Dimensional Magnetic Resonance Images
Andras Lasso, Julia Iacovella, Csaba Pinter, Kevin K. Whitehead, Sehdev Pressley, Danish Vaiyani, Matthew Daemer, Katherine Hurm, Analise M. Sulentic, Christian Herz, Ryan T. Black, Seda Aslan, Christopher W. Roy, Mark A. Fogel, Jing Huang, Matthew A. Jolley
Background: Four-dimensional (4D) flow cardiovascular magnetic resonance (CMR) captures 3D anatomy and blood velocity across the cardiac cycle, but current post-processing software is closed, varies across platforms, and sits outside open-source image-computing frameworks. We developed an open-source framework for the import, visualization, and quantification of 4D flow and other high-dimensional CMR within SlicerHeart, an extension for 3D Slicer. Methods: Import 4D Flow reads vendor DICOM, applies preprocessing, and stores velocities as a time-resolved vector field; Analyze 4D Flow provides measurement planes, streamline and Doppler-like visualization, segmentation propagation, and quantification. Underlying both, we extended the Insight Toolkit to store five- and higher-dimensional arrays. Flow quantification was compared with a commercial package (cvi42) and 2D phase contrast in 13 patients with congenital heart disease across 108 paired regions of interest (Bland-Altman analysis and intraclass correlation coefficients, ICCs). Results: The modules integrate with 3D Slicer's segmentation, registration, and rendering tools and export velocity fields and geometry for computational modeling. Against 2D phase contrast, forward volume bias (-2.2 mL) and mean absolute percentage error (21.8%) matched cvi42 (-2.1 mL; 21.3%). Conclusions: This open, extensible framework brings 4D flow CMR into an established image-computing ecosystem and agrees closely with a commercial reference; every step can be inspected, reproduced, and improved.

♻ ☆ Simulation-free Unbalanced Dynamic Optimal Transport with General Growth Penalty
Junda Ying, Yuxuan Wang, Bowen Yang, Peijie Zhou, Lei Zhang
Inferring cellular dynamics from unpaired single-cell snapshots requires modeling both state transitions and population growth or death. Unbalanced dynamic optimal transport (UDOT) addresses this by penalizing growth along transport paths, making the choice of growth penalty a key way to encode biological priors on proliferation and apoptosis. However, existing UDOT solvers either rely on computationally expensive NeuralODE simulations or depend on analytical solutions of conditional paths, restricting their efficiency solely to quadratic penalties, i.e. Wasserstein-Fisher-Rao (WFR) geodesics. To enable an efficient UDOT solver for general growth penalties, we first show that concave growth penalties lead to degenerate solutions where growth and transport are separated. We then introduce \textbf{S}imulation-free \textbf{U}nbalanced \textbf{D}ynamic \textbf{O}ptimal transport (SUDO), a simulation-free framework for UDOT with general non-quadratic convex growth penalties. SUDO learns the conditional paths and transport costs, solves the induced semi-coupling problem, and subsequently leverages unbalanced flow matching to achieve a simulation-free solution. On WFR benchmarks, SUDO matches the accuracy of efficient, analytical solution-driven algorithms while outperforming simulation-based methods in computational speed. Beyond WFR, SUDO supports asymmetric penalties that encode proliferation-dominant priors and produce more plausible trajectories and growth estimates on synthetic and single-cell datasets.
♻ ☆ Neuromorphic Navigation in Static and Dynamic Environments with Dual-Timescale Neuron-Astrocyte Memory
Yuliya Tsybina, Evgenia Antonova, Sergey Shchanikov, Vsevolod Kulagin, Alexey Mikhaylov, Victor Kazantsev, Vyacheslav Demin, Susanna Gordleeva
Biological agents navigate complex environments by combining long-term memory of successful actions with short-term suppression of recently visited locations-a capability difficult to replicate in artificial systems. We introduce a neuromorphic navigation system based on a spiking neuron--astrocyte network (SNAN), in which spike-timing-dependent plasticity (STDP) reinforces traversed action sequences on a long timescale, while astrocytic calcium transients suppress moves into recently visited states on a short timescale. This dual-timescale memory biases the agent toward unexplored regions without reward signals or explicit global statistics. In grid worlds where the agent observes only its current position, astrocytic modulation increased goal completion from 45.9% to 99.9% and shortened median routes 5.8-15-fold on commonly solved tasks. Compared with seven classical strategies, including Tremaux's and Tarry's algorithms and tabular Q-learning, SNAN yields the shortest routes among reliable strategies in open and sparse static mazes while storing 2.8-4.6 times fewer variables than memory-based baselines. In dynamic mazes, its decaying memory retains no obsolete passage marks, so SNAN becomes more efficient as the topology changes. After ten learning cycles, STDP makes SNAN the best reliable strategy. The astrocytic modulation mitigates the exploration-exploitation trade-off through local state suppression, a form of working memory we term Topological-Context Memory. To validate hardware feasibility, we map STDP to a memristive VTEAM model and implement a subset of the network on a crossbar array, achieving order-of-magnitude gains in speed per area and energy per decision over CPU implementations. These results establish astrocyte-inspired dual-timescale memory as a compact, hardware-compatible principle for neuromorphic navigation.
♻ ☆ The Mean First-Reaction Time on a Thin Filament in a 3-D Domain
Denis S. Grebenkov, Theodore Kolokolnikov, Alan E. Lindsay, Michael J. Ward
We study the mean first-reaction time (MFRT) of a Brownian particle in a bounded three-dimensional domain $Ω$ with a reflecting boundary $\partialΩ$, that contains a thin reactive filament. The filament is a tube of cross-sectional diameter $\varepsilon\ll 1$ around a smooth closed curve $Γ$, and its surface is partially reactive with reactivity $b$. As $\varepsilon\to 0$ the filament reduces to a line source in the outer problem. Its cross-section enters only through a logarithmic reactive capacitance $d_c(b)$, so that the MFRT is O$(-\log[\varepsilon d_c])$. This contrasts with the $O(\varepsilon^{-1})$ scaling for spatially localized traps of radius $\varepsilon$. Using matched asymptotic expansions, we derive a Fredholm integral equation on $Γ$ for the line density of the reactive flux. It sums all powers of $ν\equiv -1/\log[\varepsilon d_c]$ and involves two geometric quantities: the regular part $R$ of the Neumann Green's function, which encodes the interaction of $Γ$ with $\partialΩ$, and an intrinsic self-energy of $Γ$ that depends only on its shape. The framework extends to multiple filaments, splitting probabilities, a spatially heterogeneous diffusivity, and the principal eigenvalue of the Laplacian. For the unit sphere we solve the integral equation with a simple trapezoidal Nyström scheme, and we validate the predictions for rings, linked rings and torus knots against full 3-D finite-element solutions.
♻ ☆ Structural identifiability of partially-observed stochastic processes: from single-particle trajectories to total particle density data
Arianna Ceccarelli, Alexander P. Browning, Ruth E. Baker
The increasing availability of experimental data has intensified interest in calibrating stochastic models, raising fundamental questions about parameter identifiability. Structural identifiability determines whether parameters can be uniquely recovered from idealised data, a prerequisite to allow for parameter estimation in real-world scenarios. However, there is a lack of methodologies generally applicable to analyse the structural identifiability of such stochastic processes. Therefore, we develop a methodology for a class of spatio-temporal stochastic processes. We investigate how structural identifiability depends on the type of available data, distinguishing between single-particle trajectories and total particle density measurements. For trajectory data, we use a particle-based description that explicitly represents single-particle dynamics. For population-level data, we derive a partial differential equation representation, that describes the evolution of total particle density, and apply a differential algebra approach, common to ordinary differential equation analysis. We further introduce Taylor-series-expansion and Fourier-transform approaches to study the initial condition. We apply our methodology to an example model with a specified class of initial conditions. We show that the model is globally structurally identifiable given infinitely many, infinitely long single-particle trajectories, but only locally structurally identifiable from total particle density data, demonstrating that parameter identifiability depends on the type of data available.

♻ ☆ Open Biomedical Knowledge Graphs at Scale: Construction, Federation, and AI Agent Access with Samyama Graph Database

☆ AI-driven structural informatics reveals diverse glycosylation machinery across double-stranded DNA viruses

☆ D2ML: Models for Direct-to-Biology Small Molecule Screening NeurIPS 2026
AkshatKumar Nigam, Neri Amara, Hillary Dequina, Dyana Kenanova, Seth Malmersjo, Fiorella Ruggiu, Raymond V. Fucini, Jack Sadowsky, Stig K. Hansen, Imran S. Haque
Direct-to-biology (D2B) screening enables rapid testing of newly synthesized molecules without purification. However, uncertain concentrations in crude mixtures and limited purified-compound measurements complicate the use of these data in machine learning. Here we show that models tailored to the structure of D2B data can improve prediction and guide the discovery of more potent and selective compounds. Synthesis and testing identified compounds that extended the measured Pareto front, achieving better combinations of potency and selectivity than those in the training data. ATLAS provides the experimental setting for this approach, supporting synthesis and screening of 1,000-10,000 model-selected compounds in two- to three-week cycles. Together, these results show how assay-aware learning can use the full D2B assay sequence to guide multi-objective molecular optimization.

☆ Contribution of chromosome-naïve transposon-seeded network of DNA templates to intracellular pools of protein dimers of liquid-liquid phase-separated condensate-forming proteins

♻ ☆ Core physical principles of allostery
Eric Rouviere, Olivier Rivoire, Rama Ranganathan
Allostery is a basic property of proteins that represents the functional coupling of distantly positioned amino acids. In different manifestations, this property underlies signal transmission, cooperative binding, regulation, catalysis, and evolvability---the elementary features of molecular processes in living cells. While thermodynamic models have provided excellent abstract phenomenological descriptions and classifications of allostery, comparable descriptions and classifications at a more microscopic level, where the nature of physical interactions is specified, are lacking. Starting from minimal assumptions, we derive the essential physical principles of allostery and show that previously described forms of allostery can be viewed as specific implementations of a combination of two core principles: actuation of a soft mode and switching between two states. Organizing allostery by these principles may provide a simpler and more universal language to describe the physics of allostery in proteins.
♻ ☆ CMADiff: Cross-Modal Aligned Diffusion for Controllable Protein Generation
Changjian Zhou, Xinyue Zhang, Yuexi Qiu, Jiaqing Zhang, Heng-Da Cheng, Jia Song, Wensheng Xiang
AI-assisted protein design has emerged as a critical tool for advancing biotechnology, as deep generative models have demonstrated their reliability in this domain. However, most existing models primarily utilize protein sequence or structural data for training, neglecting the physicochemical properties of proteins.Moreover, they are deficient to control the generation of proteins in intuitive conditions. To address these limitations,we propose CMADiff here, a novel framework that enables controllable protein generation by aligning the physicochemical properties of protein sequences with text-based descriptions through a latent diffusion process. The implementation and code are available at https://github.com/HPC-NEAU/PhysChemDiff.

♻ ☆ When Does Context Help? A Systematic Study of Target-Conditional Molecular Property Prediction
Bryan Cheng, Jasper Zhang
We present the first systematic study of when target context helps molecular property prediction, evaluating context conditioning across 10 diverse protein families, 4 fusion architectures, data regimes spanning 67-9,409 training compounds, and both temporal and random evaluation splits. Using NestDrug, a FiLM-based architecture that conditions molecular representations on target identity, we characterize both success and failure modes with three principal findings. First, fusion architecture dominates: FiLM outperforms concatenation by 24.2 percentage points and additive conditioning by 8.6 pp; how you incorporate context matters more than whether you include it. Second, context enables otherwise impossible predictions: on data-scarce CYP3A4 (67 training compounds), multi-task transfer achieves 0.686 AUC where per-target Random Forest collapses to 0.238. Third, context can systematically hurt: distribution mismatch causes 10.2 pp degradation on BACE1; few-shot adaptation consistently underperforms zero-shot. Beyond methodology, we expose fundamental flaws in standard benchmarking: 1-nearest-neighbor Tanimoto achieves 0.991 AUC on DUD-E without any learning, and 50% of actives leak from training data, rendering absolute performance metrics meaningless. Our temporal split evaluation (train up to 2020, test 2021-2024) achieves stable 0.843 AUC with no degradation, providing the first rigorous evidence that context-conditional molecular representations generalize to future chemical space.

♻ ☆ Predicting Dose-Response Success from Primary Screening Data
Sean Lim
High-throughput drug screening uses inexpensive primary screens to select compounds for more costly follow-up dose-response experiments, which estimate compound potency (pXC50) by determining the concentration required to produce a specified biological effect. However, many promoted compounds fail to yield a reportable potency, even when activity is subsequently confirmed. We investigated whether inexpensive three-point primary-screening data could predict whether a more costly 11-point dose-response experiment would yield a reportable pXC50. Using a dataset of 32,971 compound-assay pairs with three-point primary-screening and 11-point dose-response measurements, we trained random-forest models to predict successful potency quantification. Our model achieved an AUROC of 0.939 +/- 0.002 and generalized to previously unseen chemical scaffolds and assay-mechanism families. We found that primary-screen response features were more predictive of dose-response quantifiability than molecular structure-based features. Successful potency quantification was also strongly associated with primary-screen response amplitude. Across eight chronological test releases, the model retrospectively retained 90% of reportable potencies while selecting only 55.7% of historically promoted compound-assay pairs for follow-up profiling. These findings suggest that predicting potency quantifiability from primary-screening data could improve the efficiency of compound prioritization and reduce unnecessary dose-response experiments.

☆ A heterogenetic theory of living systems
Alessandro Sarti, Giovanna Citti
The problem of the dynamics of living systems and their mathematical representation essentially unfolds on two levels. Firstly, the principles of autopoiesis and operational closure describe how a living system produces and maintains itself. Secondly, living phenomena are recognised as historical, involving the evolution of dynamics and spaces of possibility that change over time. Currently, no differential model can incorporate both of these aspects. We propose adopting the differential heterogenesis framework, which enables us to define differential operators that change in space and time, producing event-based and historical dynamics. In this construction, heterogeneous operator assemblages will be constrained by the principles of autopoiesis and operational closure, providing a theoretical framework for event-based and historical living systems. Finally, we provide a specific example of assembly with Gray-Scott kinetic equations, along with numerical simulations and results.
☆ Learning developmental dynamics from lineage context
Gokul Gowri, Nic Fishman, William N. Colgan, Minming Wang, Yusuke Kijima, Sachin A. Kumar, Aleksandr Zimin, Luke W. Koblan, Marinka Zitnik, Zachary D. Smith, Stephen Bates, Philippe Rigollet, Jonathan S. Weissman
A central goal of biology is to predict the dynamics of multicellular systems. Lineage context (the states of a cell's sisters, cousins, etc.) could inform these predictions, but how best to leverage it remains unclear. Here we introduce lineage inpainting, a generative modeling approach that uses lineage context with tree-structured transformers and stochastic interpolants to model cell state dynamics across lineage trees. Applied to a lineage-traced transcriptional atlas of mouse embryogenesis, it recovers dynamics not resolved by gene expression alone, including multiple transcriptional convergence events where distinct lineages are drawn to a common state. Despite being fit on unperturbed development, lineage inpainting can generate hypotheses about perturbation responses by simulating counterfactuals. These simulations agree in many cases with observed developmental phenotypes, including a single-cell measurement of the Tbx5 knockout response. Our results show that lineage information provides a rich source of context for learning multicellular dynamics.
☆ Yoga biomechanics through energy optimization: diverse kinematics and low-dimensional kinetics
Sriram Sekaripuram Muralidhar, Manoj Srinivasan
Yoga is widely practiced for improving physical and mental health, yet the biomechanical demands of individual yoga poses remain poorly characterized. Existing experimental studies typically examine only a small number of poses or muscles, limiting their ability to guide pose selection for rehabilitation or exercise prescription. Here we present a computational framework for predicting whole-body joint torques and contact forces from two-dimensional images of yoga poses. The framework combines manual pose digitization, a sagittal-plane MuJoCo human model, and constrained metabolic energy minimization subject to static equilibrium. We applied the framework to 55 sagittally symmetric yoga poses from Light on Yoga, generating a dataset of joint kinematics, contact locations, and predicted joint torques and contact forces. These poses collectively span nearly the full physiological range of motion of the major joints while exhibiting a wide range of predicted joint torques, with the greatest average demands on the lower extremities and lumbar region. Principal component analysis revealed a striking contrast between kinematics and kinetics: nearly all ten dimensions were required to represent the joint-angle data, whereas the first three principal components captured over 90% of the variance in joint torques. Thus, yoga poses exhibit substantial diversity in body configurations but comparatively low-dimensional patterns of predicted joint loading. Predicted joint torque rankings were also largely insensitive to uncertainty in the relative weighting of joint metabolic costs. This framework enables systematic biomechanical analysis of yoga poses and provides a foundation for quantitatively evaluating their mechanical demands and informing future rehabilitation strategies.

☆ PCR-ALERA: A PCR-ELISA analogue but antibody-free DNA detection system
Miles Bonenberger, Roland Krämer
PCR-ALERA is a new concept for DNA detection that combines DNA amplification by polymerase chain reaction (PCR) with an activator-linked enzyme reactivation assay (ALERA). PCR-ALERA resembles the commercial, semi-quantitative PCR-ELISA assay that offers high sensitivity and high-throughput screening in microplates. PCR-ALERA overcomes a major drawback of PCR-ELISA since it does not require the time-consuming handling of expensive antibodies. In the first step of the assay, commercially available modified nucleotides were incorporated during PCR amplification of the target DNA. A selenocysteine containing peptide (the activator) was linked to the amplified DNA by click chemistry. This peptide has been optimized for the highly efficient activation of the latent protease papain by selenolate-triggered disulfide cleavage. The DNA-peptide hybrid was then immoblized in microplate wells and incubated with latent papain. The latter was readily activated and converted a fluorogenic substrate to a fluorescent product with high catalytic turnover. Overall assay time is about 4 hours including PCR.

☆ Beyond $χ^2$: Ensemble null tests for reliable scientific discovery
Adrian E. Bayer, David N. Spergel
Unrecognized systematic errors can survive standard checks and undermine scientific discoveries. Null tests and experimental controls can expose these failures, but averaging measurements or reducing them to scalar statistics can hide important structure. We propose ensemble null tests that compare multivariate distributions with references from observations or simulations. We demonstrate their benefits across science. In cosmic microwave background simulations, they detect systematic effects missed by conventional $χ^2$ tests. In Gaia data, they detect velocity asymmetry as sensitively as an informed search. In a CRISPR screen, they expose a control mismatch in DNA damage and reveal which cellular profiles drive it. As scientific datasets grow, ensemble testing turns repeated measurements into a broader search for unknown unknowns, strengthening the empirical foundation for scientific discovery.

☆ The Verification Wall in Microbial Metabolomics NeurIPS 2026
Takahiko Koyama
Computational methods now propose structures for gut microbial metabolites faster than any laboratory can confirm them. We argue that verification, not generation, is the binding constraint, and that better models will not relieve it. Confirming a structure to the field's own standard means having the molecule in hand. Automation can lower what that costs per compound, but the cost stays proportional to the number of structures and never amortizes across them. Two substitutes are on offer and neither removes that requirement. In-silico spectral prediction ranks candidates instead of confirming one, and the strongest current model puts the right structure first 33.5% of the time on a scaffold-split benchmark. Any assignment resting on spectral similarity is, by the field's own definition, made without a reference standard. Microcrystal diffraction does return a structure, but it needs the compound to crystallize out of a trace mixture, which is a different problem rather than an easier one. We sorted 10,645 computationally inferred metabolite structures by what verifying each would take. Between 5% and 37% have no verification route identified under our analysis. That spread reflects where the thresholds are set rather than a measured count of unverifiable compounds, and the inaccessible ones cluster among the most speculative entries. Those figures understate the problem, because the gut makes far more compounds than any database records. HMDB holds only what someone has already thought worth cataloguing, and structure generators are bounded by nothing at all. The claim is easy to disprove. Show a route that confirms structures as fast as they are proposed. Until someone does, two problems stay unsolved. Nobody knows how to spend a verification budget that will always be too small, or what a biologist may conclude from a structure that was never confirmed.

☆ Confidence Filtering in Video-Based Gait Assessment: Measurement Error versus Selection Shift
David Regev, Eran Beeri Bamani
Video-based gait assessment often keeps only high-confidence pose observations, which can lower measurement error but change the movement assessed. We compared confidence selection with budget-matched random selection in a Parkinson-related cohort (n = 96) and an NF2-related replication cohort (n = 18). In the Parkinson-related cohort, confidence selection produced a small but consistent shift in the observed movement summary (+0.05 SD, 95% CI 0.03 to 0.07), while held-out Mini-BESTest error stayed flat (-0.04 points, -0.13 to 0.05); a policy preserving motion-group proportions reduced the shift, while NF2 showed no shift and its predictor did not beat a constant baseline. Because this shift mixes measurement error with selection shift, we decompose and simulate both on the cohort's window structure. Filtering lowers local error when error depends on confidence, but biases the endpoint once confidence tracks movement more than error. In the Parkinson-related data, confidence was only weakly tied to movement (rho = 0.14). There, filtering is harmless if well-tracked windows are unbiased. If instead systematic error only redistributes within participants, filtering can add up to 0.58 degrees. Unchanged clinical error from such small-cohort predictors therefore cannot show that quality filtering preserves the assessed movement.

☆ Temporal and spatial couplings in the dynamics of human walking and running
Mustafa Mert Ankarali
Rhythmic behaviours such as walking and running are commonly modelled as dynamical systems around a stable limit cycle. To keep such models tractable, it is usually assumed that each step depends only on the previous one, that the limit cycle is fixed, and that the sagittal and frontal planes can be studied separately. Here, we test these assumptions in seven public datasets of human gait (71 participants), including 16 people who both walked and ran. We fit linear step-to-step maps and validate them by cross-validation, holding out more data where more data exist. At first sight, gait has memory: the state two steps back lowers the prediction error by up to 29\%. Yet most of this temporal coupling vanishes once the operating point may drift slowly and measurement noise is modelled; gait remains limit-cycle-like, around an operating point that moves. Running keeps more memory than walking in the same people. Spatial coupling between the planes is present in most participants, mainly from sagittal to frontal, but weak. This supports planar models of healthy gait, while a repeatable, task-dependent coupling index may serve as a measure of gait quality. The simple models are wrong, but useful enough.
☆ GraphTFactor: A graph-based approach for proteins transcription factor prediction
Domenico Amato, Salvatore Calderaro, Giosuè Lo Bosco, Riccardo Rizzo, Filippo Vella
Transcription factors (TFs) play an important role in regulating gene expression, making their identification a fundamental task in computational biology. Experimental results show that GraphTFactor outperforms state-of-the-art methods on benchmark datasets, demonstrating the effectiveness of graph-based representations for TF prediction while providing interpretable insights into the sequence regions contributing to the model's predictions. To support reproducibility and practical adoption, we release an open-source software package and an interactive web application that let users easily perform TF prediction on custom sequences.

☆ Exact mean first-passage time and a Poisson-smoothing crossover in renewal shot noise
Yuxin Wang, Jiajun Zhang
Threshold crossings in exponentially relaxing shot noise depend on the interplay between incoming impulses and the residual signal left by earlier arrivals. For renewal input, existing high-threshold product approximations of the mean first-passage time (MFPT) leave open when evaluation at a single index preserves the mean with vanishing relative error. Here we derive an exact finite-threshold MFPT series for exponentially marked renewal shot noise and recast it as an average over an auxiliary Poisson index. When the waiting-time density exhibits short-time power-law behavior, $w(t)\sim ct^{κ-1}$, the dominant series contributions shift from the Poisson center by an amount of order $x^{1-κ}$, whereas the averaging kernel has a fluctuation width of order $\sqrt{x}$, with $x=λb$. The competition between these scales yields a crossover at $κ=1/2$, where the deterministic-index product is asymptotically exact in ratio above this boundary, requires a finite multiplicative correction at criticality, and exhibits a growing logarithmic discrepancy below it. A moving-saddle approximation also yields an asymptotically exact MFPT ratio under general strictly positive, finite-mean waiting times. Numerical comparisons across Gamma, Weibull, and generalized-Gamma renewal laws illustrate the approximation regimes and their finite-threshold accuracy. Together, these results provide an exact finite-threshold benchmark for the mean first-passage time and reveal how short-gap renewal statistics determine the asymptotic accuracy of high-threshold approximations.

♻ ☆ A Closed-loop Framework to Discriminate Models Using Optimal Control
Laurent Pagnier, Melvyn Tyloo, Akshita Jindal, Pragati Thakur, Kyle C. A. Wedgwood
Predicting the response of an observed system to a known input is a fruitful first step to accurately control the system's dynamics. Despite the recent advances in fully data-driven algorithms, the most interpretable way to reach this goal is through mechanistic mathematical modeling. Here, we leverage optimal control and propose a closed-loop iterative method to choose among a set of candidate models the one that most accurately predicts an observed system. ☆ What to randomize matters: a controlled study of volume, axis and representation in low-data molecular generation
Neerav Kaushal, Alisa Teige, Ajay MNV Penmatsa
Chemical language models can struggle to generate new molecules when trained on small datasets, where a model may reproduce training examples rather than learn a useful generative distribution. Randomized SMILES augmentation varies atom traversal, whereas Sequential Attachment-based Fragment Embedding (SAFE) also allows the order of fragment blocks to vary. How these distinct sources of representational variation affect low-data molecular generation has not been clearly studied. Here, using 1,000 seed molecules to train an autoregressive sequence model, we compare canonical SAFE with randomized SAFE at increasing corpus sizes, isolate atom-order and fragment-order axes of SAFE randomization, and compare fully randomized SAFE with randomized SMILES at molecule-by-molecule matched training corpora. Three findings emerge. First, randomization of SAFE alone raises the genuine yield of novel molecules more than 450-fold, turning a model that merely reproduces its training set into a practical molecular generator. Second, the two axes of randomization act jointly rather than additively: atom order alone reaches a tenth, and fragment order alone a seventh, of the genuine yield obtained when both vary, which is four times their combined contribution. Third, at matched corpus size and compute, randomized SAFE delivers 2.8 times the genuine yield of randomized SMILES. Welch tests with Games-Howell post-hoc comparisons resolve every difference on the primary outcomes. In the low-data regime, thus, what one randomizes rather than only how much determines whether augmentation produces new molecules or merely new spellings of the training set.

♻ ☆ Spontaneous Mutations from Terahertz Proton Tunneling
Noah Bray-Ali
Protons in the bonds between base pairs of the double helix store the code of life by breaking the dyad symmetry that swaps the sense strand with its complementary partner. When these bonds break during replication and transcription, pairs of protons switch sides, restoring dyad symmetry and destroying genetic information. The observed rate of such spontaneous mutations is obtained in the sudden approximation for bond breaking provided protons in bonds between bases tunnel across the gap with terahertz frequency.

♻ ☆ Bridging the Simulation-to-Experiment Gap with Generative Models using Adversarial Distribution Alignment
Kai Nelson, Tobias Kreiman, Sergey Levine, Aditi S. Krishnapriyan
A fundamental challenge in science and engineering is the simulation-to-experiment gap. While we often possess prior knowledge of physical laws, these physical laws can be too difficult to solve exactly for complex systems. Such systems are commonly modeled using simulators, which impose computational approximations. Meanwhile, experimental measurements more faithfully represent the real world, but experimental data typically consists of observations that only partially reflect the system's full underlying state. We propose a data-driven distribution alignment framework that bridges this simulation-to-experiment gap by pre-training a generative model on fully observed (but imperfect) simulation data, then aligning it with partial (but real) observations of experimental data. While our method is domain-agnostic, we ground our approach in the physical sciences by introducing Adversarial Distribution Alignment (ADA). This method aligns a generative model of atomic positions -- initially trained on a simulated Boltzmann distribution -- with the distribution of experimental observations. We prove that our method recovers the target observable distribution, even with multiple, potentially correlated observables. We also empirically validate our framework on synthetic, molecular, and experimental protein data, demonstrating that it can align generative models with diverse observables. Our code is available at https://kaityrusnelson.com/ada/.
♻ ☆ A Co-Design Approach to Physically-Informed Molecular Docking for Quantum Annealers
Francesco Micucci, Matteo Barbieri, Gabriella Bettonte, Domenico Bonanni, Anita Camillini, Anna Fava, Daniele Gregori, Andrea R. Beccari, Gianluca Palermo
Molecular docking is a crucial step in computer- aided drug design, involving the identification of ligand poses that are physically plausible within a protein pocket. Recent studies have shown that this problem can be formulated as a geometric weighted subgraph isomorphism, which can then be encoded as a QUBO and thus solved on D-Wave quantum annealers. In this work, we adopt a co-design approach, addressing molecular docking at the formulation and encoding levels, and examining how hardware topology affects solution quality on current quantum annealers. We propose new QUBO formulations that extend the state-of-the-art ones by including van der Waals, Coulomb, hydrogen bonding, and hydrophobic interactions. Experimental results show that incorporating physicochemical terms improves the average docking quality by more than 18% compared to the purely geometric baseline on D-Wave quantum annealers with the Pegasus topology, and by up to 29% on those with the Zephyr topology. The choice of topology, along with an improved encoding design, yields significant gains in embedding efficiency and the number of valid solutions. Our designs reduce the number of physical qubits required by up to 14.5% and decrease the chain break fraction by an order of magnitude, thereby enhancing confidence in the resulting solutions.

♻ ☆ Latent Style-based Quantum Wasserstein GAN for Drug Design
Julien Baglio, Yacine Haddad, Richard Polifka
The development of new drugs is a time-consuming and expensive process, with average costs estimated to be up to around $2.5 billion. The first step in this process is the design of the new drug, for which de novo drug design, assisted by artificial intelligence, has blossomed in recent years and has revolutionized the field. In particular, generative artificial intelligence has delivered promising results in drug discovery and development, reducing costs and time to solution. However, classical generative models, such as generative adversarial networks (GANs), can be difficult to train and are prone to mode collapse. Quantum computing may be an avenue to overcome some of these issues, providing compact models with far fewer trainable parameters, thereby enhancing the generalizability of GANs. We propose a new latent style-based quantum Wasserstein GAN (QGAN) for drug design, combining a variational autoencoder that maps molecular structures into a latent space with a hybrid quantum GAN. Our pipeline is benchmarked against classical models of the industry-relevant MOSES suite. Style-based noise reuploading at every rotational gate and a Wasserstein gradient penalty are used to improve trainability and to mitigate mode collapse. Training and large-scale sampling with circuits of up to 15 qubits are performed on noiseless quantum simulators to validate our architecture. We present an inference-only demonstration on an IBM Heron quantum computer using 5 qubits, sampling 2,500 new molecules, to investigate quantum noise effects. Our default QGAN, using 110 trainable generator parameters, reaches statistically comparable overall performance and statistically significant gains on selected drug-relevant metrics relative to both the MOSES classical latent GAN baseline, which contains about 700,000 trainable parameters for the generator, and a classical latent GAN with matched generator capacity.

☆ Open-Source Framework for the Visualization, Quantification, and Analysis of 4D Flow and High-Dimensional Magnetic Resonance Images
Andras Lasso, Julia Iacovell, Csaba Pinter, Kevin K. Whitehead, Sehdev Pressley, Danish Vaiyani, Matthew Daemer, Katherine Hurm, Analise M. Sulentic, Christian Herz, Ryan T. Black, Seda Aslan, Christopher W. Roy, Mark A. Fogel, Jing Huang, Matthew A. Jolley

☆ The Mean First-Reaction Time on a Thin Filament in a 3-D Domain
Denis S. Grebenkov, Theodore Kolokolnikov, Alan E. Lindsay, Michael J. Ward
We study the mean first-reaction time (MFRT) of a Brownian particle in a bounded three-dimensional domain $Ω$ with a reflecting boundary $\partialΩ$, that contains a thin reactive filament. The filament is a tube of cross-sectional diameter $\varepsilon\ll 1$ around a smooth closed curve $Γ$, and its surface is partially reactive with reactivity $b$. As $\varepsilon\to 0$ the filament reduces to a line source in the outer problem. Its cross-section enters only through a logarithmic reactive capacitance $d_c(b)$, so that the MFRT is $\mathcal{O}(-\log[\varepsilon d_c])$. This contrasts with the $\mathcal{O}(\eps^{-1})$ scaling for spatially localized traps of radius $\eps$. Using matched asymptotic expansions, we derive a Fredholm integral equation on $Γ$ for the line density of the reactive flux. It sums all powers of $ν\equiv -1/\log[\varepsilon d_c]$ and involves two geometric quantities: the regular part $R$ of the Neumann Green's function, which encodes the interaction of $Γ$ with $\partialΩ$, and an intrinsic self-energy of $Γ$ that depends only on its shape. The framework extends to multiple filaments, splitting probabilities, a spatially heterogeneous diffusivity, and the principal eigenvalue of the Laplacian. For the unit sphere we solve the integral equation with a simple trapezoidal Nyström scheme, and we validate the predictions for rings, linked rings and torus knots against full 3-D finite-element solutions.
☆ Diagnosing parameter unidentifiability in stochastic dynamics with an application to flocking models
Natsuki Kurita, Hiroshi Kori
Although flocking behaviour has been studied extensively in active matter physics and mathematical biology, methods for assessing parameter estimability remain underdeveloped. For models formulated as stochastic differential equations (SDEs), we develop a Hessian-based diagnostic framework that detects unidentifiable directions and indicates whether, and how, estimation precision can be improved. The framework utilizes the submartingale structure of the loss function's Hessian and tracks its minimum eigenvalue over time. Under convergence of the time-averaged Hessian, we prove that this eigenvalue grows linearly for identifiable models, but only sublinearly, as $o(t)$, for unidentifiable ones; for a specific type of parameter redundancy, this $o(t)$ bound is tightened to $o(\sqrt{t}\log t)$. Applying the framework to stochastic flocking models, we demonstrate that, in identifiable settings, the magnitude of the eigenvalue reflects the attainable estimation precision. We further uncover a physical mechanism of unidentifiability: as the system approaches the ordered state, the parameters become algebraically degenerate, even though the model contains no trivial redundancy. Identifiability therefore depends on the dynamical regime, not only on model structure. Our framework offers a practical tool for reliable parameter estimation, not only in flocking models but in SDE models across diverse fields.

☆ Who Called? V33DA: A Physically Verified Multimodal Benchmark for Vocal Attribution in Zebra Finch Groups NeurIPS 2026
Maris Basha, Yuhang Wang, Xiaoran Chen, Longbiao Cheng, Luca Yapura, Anja T. Zai, Mathieu Salzmann, Richard Hahnloser
Deciding which member of an animal group produced a vocalization and where that vocalization was produced are central to studying social communication but are rarely evaluated with physically verified ground truth. Existing resources either localize sounds without identifying the caller, or recognize individuals from isolated vocalizations without reliance on the 3D candidate geometry. We close this gap with V33DA, a dataset-centered multimodal benchmark for spatial vocal attribution in social zebra finches: it comprises 33,625 verified events coupling 5-channel audio with three camera views, calibrated 3D pose for every visible candidate, and per-bird radio telemetry across 10 individuals and three experiments. Caller identity is verified from an on-body accelerometer-derived vibration signal; this channel is withheld from benchmark models and used only by an on-body reference ceiling. Given a detected vocalization and the visible candidate birds, the task is to select the caller. We evaluate it under three regimes: session-disjoint testing on familiar birds from held-out recording dates, held-out-experiment transfer to a new cohort, and leave-one-individual-out evaluation on a bird whose vocalizations are held out from training. Reference methods expose a consistent gap between in-domain accuracy and transfer to unseen individuals: methods that can exploit familiar vocal identity perform well on known callers but degrade sharply on unseen ones, whereas candidate-aware methods preserving explicit spatial reasoning transfer substantially better. The on-body reference stays near-perfect across regimes, confirming the internal consistency of the accelerometer-verified labels. We release V33DA with fixed protocols and code for the evaluated methods, and V33DA++, an unevaluated companion set of verified overlapping-call events and +/-2 s context windows.

☆ Automatic Construction of Strong Simple Baselines
Caleb N. Ellington, Jiaqi Wang, Sohan Addagudi, Elijah Cole
To rigorously establish the value of a novel deep learning method, it must be compared against appropriate baselines. As deep learning has become increasingly common in the physical and life sciences, there have been numerous cases in which apparent progress has turned out to be an artifact of omitted or missing baselines. In this work, we investigate whether this problem can be mitigated by using large language models to automate the construction of baselines. We assemble 36 supervised classification and regression tasks from peer-reviewed studies in Nature, Science, and Cell-family journals that investigate novel deep learning methods for scientific applications. We then use a language model to construct simple baselines for each task. These automatically constructed baselines are strong, recovering a median of 95.5% (mean 85.7%) of the gap between a trivial predictor and the score of the deep learning model. The median simple baseline matches or exceeds the performance of the deep learning model in 12 studies. We further examine the variation across scientific domains and publication venues and we characterize the effects of test-time compute and access to different levels of study context. As human peer review has struggled to keep up with the rapidly increasing volume of research articles in recent years, our results suggest that automated baseline construction is a practical and scalable check on deep learning contributions in the physical and life sciences.
♻ ☆ Kinetic Proofreading Activation Times Beyond the Weibull Limit
Thomas Mourier, Cameron Hopkinson, Kenny Wong
T cells can detect a few foreign peptide-major histocompatibility complexes (pMHCs) among a far larger number of self pMHCs. Kinetic proofreading explains this discrimination: a receptor that has bound its ligand must complete nKP signaling steps, each at rate kp, before it activates, and dissociation at any step resets its progress. When many receptors are engaged at once, the time to the first activation has been approximated by a Weibull law taken from the short-time limit of this process. The Weibull law is convenient, but it does not contain the dissociation rate koff, even though koff is what proofreading uses to tell ligands apart. Using the exact first-passage law of the underlying continuous-time Markov chain together with stochastic simulations, we show that the relative error of the Weibull law is set by a single dimensionless number $Λ$, the Weibull-predicted activation time measured in units of nKP + 1 bound-state lifetimes. For $Λ$ < 1 the first receptor to activate does so before its ligand has ever dissociated, and the Weibull law holds. As $Λ$ grows, activation relies increasingly on repeated unbinding and rebinding. The true activation time then exceeds the Weibull prediction by up to 2900-fold on our parameter grid, and the effective Weibull shape falls to one, so activation becomes memoryless instead of timer-like. We derive a closed form that keeps koff by treating the successful pass through the proofreading chain separately from the time lost to failed attempts. It matches exact medians to within 2% in 19 of 25 parameter sets, with a worst case of about 6%, and reproduces the loss of timer-like behavior. Because $Λ$ is computed from the Weibull prediction itself, it shows in advance how far that formula can be trusted; where it cannot, the new expression requires only a one-dimensional root find.

♻ ☆ pyTransRate: reference-free quality assessment of de novo transcriptome assemblies with current aligners and quantifiers
Matthew MacManes
Summary: The original software TransRate scores a de novo transcriptome assembly by mapping the reads it was built from back to it, with no reference required. Its last release (1.0.3) depends on Ruby 2.2 and on aligner and quantifier versions that can no longer be installed on most systems. pyTransRate is a Python reimplementation of the TransRate method built on snap-aligner 2.0.5 and salmon 2.7.0. In 5,031 paired comparisons (39 assembly-level metrics on each of 129 public assemblies), it reproduces the original sequence metrics and read counts exactly and ranks assemblies consistently with 1.0.3 (Spearman rho = 0.94 for assembly score, 0.98 for good-mapping rate), although its read-based scores are not numerically interchangeable with the original. It also reads gzip- and bgzip-compressed input directly. Availability and Implementation: pyTransRate is implemented in Python 3 and is freely available under the MIT license at https://github.com/macmanes-lab/pytransrate. It runs on Linux and macOS; snap-aligner and salmon are installed from Bioconda. Supplementary information: Supplementary data are available online. ☆ A unified coarse-grained neural network potential for the dynamics of biomolecular complexes
Antonio Mirarchi, Stephen E. Farr, Adrià Peréz, Gianni De Fabritiis
The behavior of biomolecular complexes is governed by chemically specific interactions and often involves rare events that atomistic molecular dynamics struggle to capture within accessible timescales. Here we introduce AMARO v2.0, a coarse-grained neural network potential that retains all heavy atoms and treats solvent implicitly. Hydrogen-aware bead types and total-charge conditioning provide a shared representation for proteins, drug-like molecules, and their complexes, including ternary protein-protein-ligand systems. The model preserves folded proteins and captures key features of protein conformational free-energy landscapes. Across 262 complexes spanning 11 targets, the learned potential retains most ligand poses, although quaternary structure reorientation is observed for the HIF2A ternary complex. In targeted case studies, AMARO v2.0 distinguishes the two protonation states of trypsin-benzamidine and reaches the crystal binding pose in 3.3-fold more trajectories than all-atom molecular mechanics at a fixed estimated GPU-time budget. The same framework also recovers a reference binding pose in TYK2 and supports binding free-energy estimation for both trypsin-benzamidine and TYK2-EJM31, although the resulting values are more energetically favorable than the experimental references. SHP2 and CDK8 systems expose remaining limitations in binding-pose recovery and site specificity, respectively. Together, these results support AMARO v2.0 as a shared heavy-atom potential for biomolecular complex dynamics.
☆ Accurate structural modelling of protein-glycan recognition with GlycoWeave
Huifeng Zhao, Jintu Zhang, Yifei Xiao, Youqiao Qian, Silong Zhai, Zichang Jin, Li Wang, Yuchao Wang, Hang Dong, Yifan Zhao, Zhenxing Wu, Hongyan Du, Yuansheng Huang, Bowei Zhao, Xiaorui Wang, Shukai Gu, Jike Wang, Zhongjun Li, Zhongtang Li, Tingjun Hou, Yu Kang
Protein-glycan recognition underlies diverse biological processes, yet accurate prediction of protein-glycan complex structures remains challenging owing to the chemical diversity, conformational flexibility and sparse experimental structural coverage of glycans. Here we present GlycoWeave, a glycan-specific diffusion framework that integrates chemically constrained conformational sampling, multiscale molecular representations and multi-source learning. By operating in internal-coordinate space, GlycoWeave preserves glycan connectivity and stereochemistry while efficiently exploring bound conformations. A heterogeneous equivariant network couples atom-level interaction geometry with monosaccharide-level structural context, whereas multi-source, multi-head training integrates experimental complexes with structures refined using classical and quantum mechanics-informed potentials. On a benchmark of 698 protein-glycan complexes, GlycoWeave achieves a 71.9% Top-25 high-quality success rate, compared with 19.2% for AlphaFold 3, while requiring only 0.34 s per sampled pose. GlycoWeave further preserves glycan structural validity, recovers native protein-glycan interactions and reproduces linkage-specific conformational preferences, maintaining high accuracy across diverse glycan architectures and protein environments. These results establish GlycoWeave as an accurate, efficient and chemically faithful framework for modelling protein-glycan recognition. GlycoWeave is freely available at https://cadd.zju.edu.cn/glycoweave.
♻ ☆ mdRNAcat: A Molecular Dynamics RNA Catalog of REST2 Conformational Ensembles
Anton E. Dorn, Emile de Bruyn, Fabrice von der Lehr, Philipp Knechtges, Stefan Kesselheim, Alexander Schug
Learning molecular models of RNA requires simulation data that capture conformational variability and provide consistent physical training signals. Here, we introduce mdRNAcat, a curated dataset of all-atom molecular dynamics (MD) simulations of 48 RNA systems selected to promote sequence diversity. Each system is simulated using replica-exchange with solute scaling (REST2), with 32 Hamiltonian states and production lengths of 200-300 ns per state. The dataset provides atomic coordinates, instantaneous forces, and forces reevaluated under the unscaled reference Hamiltonian, together with structural annotations and replica-exchange metadata. Coordinates and forces are available at 1 ps intervals for the reference state and 100 ps intervals across all states in a standardized HDF5 format. We characterize conformational sampling, exchange behavior, base pairing, and sugar-pucker distributions to document the properties of the simulated ensembles. mdRNAcat provides a reusable resource for developing and evaluating data-driven RNA models, including coarse-grained force fields.

♻ ☆ From static structures to dynamic landscapes: cryo-EM redefines RNA biology
Shekhar Jadhav, Spandan Saha, Qingbin Shang, Marco Marcia
RNA molecules perform diverse biological functions by dynamically exploring multiple conformational states rather than adopting a single static structure. Capturing these ensembles is a challenge in molecular biology. Recent advances in cryoEM are now transforming this landscape by enabling the visualization of RNA molecules across a broad spectrum of functionally relevant conformations at near atomic resolution. Here, we examine how cryoEM is reshaping RNA structural biology changing focus from the analysis of static structures to dynamic conformational landscapes. Through 8 representative case studies we illustrate how cryoEM has revealed previously inaccessible mechanisms of RNA motion, including folding processes, ligand-dependent switching, and cooperative assembly. We specifically discuss emerging experimental and computational approaches that address and overcome the challenges associated with studying dynamic RNAs, particularly in construct design, sample preparation, vitrification, and data analysis. These novel methods resolve conformational variability and enable the reconstruction of discrete and continuous RNA conformational landscapes from cryoEM data, highlighting how structural heterogeneity can be harnessed to extract functional insights. Looking forward, the integration of cryoEM with complementary biophysical techniques and time resolved methodologies promises to bridge structural and temporal resolution, to routinely derive experimental molecular movies of RNA in action. These advances will not only deepen our understanding of RNA biology but also provide new opportunities for RNA targeted therapeutics and the rational design of dynamic RNA-based nanodevices. By connecting structural snapshots into coherent dynamic models, cryoEM is establishing a framework for quantitative descriptions of RNA energy landscapes and their functional roles.
♻ ☆ Commutative Algebra Modeling of Mutation and Disease Association
JunJie Wee, Mushal Zia, Faisal Suwayyid, Hongsong Feng, Yuta Hozumi, Guo-Wei Wei
Genetic mutations can lead to disease by disrupting molecular interactions, altering protein stability, inducing misfolding, and promoting protein aggregation. Although existing predictive models have achieved promising performance, they often lack mechanistic interpretability and fail to fully incorporate the underlying physical and chemical principles governing mutation-induced effects. Moreover, current approaches typically address disease association, protein stability changes, and solubility alterations as separate prediction tasks rather than integrating them within a unified framework. In this study, we introduce a multiscale commutative algebra modeling framework for predicting disease-mutation association. Leveraging persistent Stanley-Reisner theory, we extract algebraic invariants to build two predictive models: Commutative Algebra Neural Network (CANet) and Commutative Algebra Gradient Boosting Tree (CATree). The proposed framework is applied to three biologically connected tasks: disease-associated mutation identification, mutation-induced protein stability change prediction, and mutation-induced protein solubility change classification. Across six benchmark tasks, CANet and CATree consistently achieve leading predictive performance, with up to 7.5% relative improvement over existing methods. These results demonstrate that commutative algebra provides a unified, interpretable, and generalizable modeling strategy for mutation-disease association prediction.
