☆ HGPRGDel: GPR-Aware Learning and Ranking for Growth-Coupled Gene Deletion Design
Ziwei Yang, Takeyuki Tamura
Designing microbial strains for growth-coupled production, in which cellular growth is linked to the synthesis of a target compound through genetic interventions, is a fundamental goal in metabolic engineering. Constraint-based methods can identify growth-coupled deletion strategies, but design for each target typically requires a separate search across a large number of possible gene-deletion combinations. This motivates a reusable approach that learns from previously evaluated strain designs to prioritize candidate interventions before downstream constraint-based evaluation. We present a reproducible protocol for growth-coupled gene deletion design that learns and ranks gene-deletion priorities for each target metabolite. Using computationally curated deletion strategies as learning resources, the protocol generates continuous deletion-priority rankings, constructs candidate strategies at adjustable search-space budgets, and evaluates these candidates through constraint-based metabolic analysis. Its learning component, HGPRGDel, explicitly represents gene-protein-reaction (GPR) logic, capturing how multiple genes can jointly support a reaction or alternative genes can maintain its activity, while integrating molecular and reaction-level information. Across e_coli_core, iMM904, and iML1515, the protocol achieves the highest macro-F1 and AUC among the evaluated learning configurations. HGPRGDel's rankings recovered an average of 90% of the reference deletion genes from MetNetComp by examining fewer genes than the evaluated baseline methods. By separating learned intervention prioritization from downstream constraint-based assessment, the protocol provides a reproducible framework for reusing strain-design knowledge while controlling the size of the candidate space and retaining mechanistic evaluation of growth-coupled production.
☆ pyTransRate: 1 reference-free quality assessment of de novo transcriptome assemblies with current aligners and quantifiers
Matthew MacManes
Summary: The original software TransRate scores a de novo transcriptome assembly by mapping the reads it was built from back to it, with no reference required. Its last release (1.0.3) depends on Ruby 2.2 and on aligner and quantifier versions that can no longer be installed on most systems. pyTransRate is a Python reimplementation of the TransRate method built on snap-aligner 2.0.5 and salmon 2.7.0. In 5,031 paired comparisons (39 assembly-level metrics on each of 129 public assemblies), it reproduces the original sequence metrics and read counts exactly and ranks assemblies consistently with 1.0.3 (Spearman rho = 0.94 for assembly score, 0.98 for good-mapping rate), although its read-based scores are not numerically interchangeable with the original. It also reads gzip- and bgzip-compressed input directly. Availability and Implementation: pyTransRate is implemented in Python 3 and is freely available under the MIT license at https://github.com/macmanes-lab/pytransrate. It runs on Linux and macOS; snap-aligner and salmon are installed from Bioconda. Supplementary information: Supplementary data are available online. Supplementary Table S1 lists the assembly and read accessions for every assembly analyzed; Supplementary Table S2 gives per-assembly values from both implementations.
☆ Stationary Action Principles for Neuronal Growth
Nathan Brodeur, Cristian Staii
Neuronal growth is guided by biochemical, mechanical, and geometrical cues while being influenced by stochastic fluctuations in cytoskeletal dynamics, adhesion, and growth cone sensing. Here, we develop two complementary variational descriptions of mechanically guided axonal growth. First, for neurons growing on periodic parallel micropatterns, we use Langevin and Fokker--Planck models to derive a stochastic path action incorporating deterministic substrate guidance and random fluctuations. The probability of a growth trajectory decreases exponentially with this action, and the most probable trajectories correspond to minimum action paths for fixed boundary conditions. Second, for axons growing on substrates with spatially varying stiffness, we show that stiffness dependent traction generates mechanical guidance and axonal turning at stiffness interfaces. In the quasi-static limit, this response leads to a Fermat-like spatial action with an effective axonal refractive index determined by the traction and substrate stiffness relationship. The resulting framework predicts axonal refraction across stiffness boundaries, including an approximate Snell-like relation and a critical angle condition. Although the stochastic and Fermat-like actions have distinct physical meanings and ranges of validity, both describe how substrate mechanics constrain axonal trajectories. These results connect measurable quantities, including drift, diffusion, stiffness, traction, and growth direction, to global properties of axonal paths and provide a framework for designing biomimetic substrates for controlled neuronal growth and regeneration.

☆ A Differentiable, GPU-Accelerated Finite-Element Formulation in JAX for Large-Deformation Shells with Contact: Application to Patient-Specific Aortic Valve Simulation
Mohsen Nakhaei, Alison M. Pouch, Matthew A. Jolley, Wensi Wu
Patient-specific aortic-valve models can help assess how tissue properties and geometry influence leaflet deformation and coaptation, with potential to inform intervention planning. Large deformations, near-incompressibility, and evolving contact make these simulations computationally demanding and can challenge nonlinear convergence. Differentiable, GPU-accelerated methods offer efficient derivative evaluation and parallel computation, but their matrix-free application to soft-tissue shells with contact remains less established. We developed a forward solver using tatva's energy-based finite-element framework in JAX. The solver combines finite-strain extensible-director shells, hyperelastic constitutive models, and a finite-range repulsive contact energy. Nonlinear equilibrium is solved using a regularized, matrix-free Newton-Krylov method with two-level preconditioning. Automatic differentiation evaluates tangent-vector products without forming or storing the primary tangent matrix, while GPU execution parallelizes element and quadrature-point computations. Constitutive, shell, contact, and self-contact responses were assessed against analytical, semi-analytical, and published benchmarks. At a contact-free plate equilibrium, implicit sensitivities of the converged apex displacement to constitutive parameters, thickness, and pressure agreed with finite differences from independent nonlinear simulations. Simulations of three patient-specific aortic-valve models (two tricuspid and one bicuspid) achieved final relative equilibrium residuals below 10^-4. Median GPU runtimes were approximately 15-25 minutes, with 5.3-6.5-fold speedups relative to corresponding CPU runs on the tested hardware. These results provide component-level verification and demonstrate GPU-accelerated forward valve simulation in the tested cases, supporting future gradient-based inverse studies.
♻ ☆ A general-purpose multi-path stacked coupling for stochastic reaction networks
David F. Anderson, Jingyi Ma
Stochastic reaction networks are continuous-time Markov chain models for interacting populations, with applications in biochemistry, epidemiology, ecology, and related areas. Coupling multiple stochastic reaction networks effectively is a fundamental challenge in stochastic simulation. Existing variance-reducing coupling methods are typically designed for pairs of processes, though specialized multi-path constructions have been developed for particular applications. To fill this gap, we introduce the multi-path stacked coupling (MSC), a space-time Poisson construction that jointly simulates a finite collection of stochastic reaction networks with common reaction channels and reaction vectors, but possibly different intensity functions and initial conditions. Each pairwise marginal of MSC has the same law as the corresponding split-coupled pair, allowing existing variance bounds to transfer directly; in finite-state settings, we also obtain first-order expansions for the mean and second moment of finite-difference numerators. We demonstrate the framework through three sensitivity estimation tasks requiring several nearby parameterized paths: estimating many first derivatives simultaneously, estimating a single first derivative using a wider finite-difference stencil, and estimating higher-order derivatives. Numerical experiments on a processive phosphorylation network demonstrate strong performance in each of the three application areas considered, consistent with the theoretical advantages of MSC: across all three applications, MSC achieves the smallest root mean square error (RMSE) among the methods considered over the tested computational budgets.

♻ ☆ Latent Representation Learning from 3D Brain MRI for Latent Analysis and Prediction in Multiple Sclerosis
Trinh Ngoc Huynh, Nguyen Duc Kien, Nguyen Hai Anh, Dinh Tran Hiep, Manuela Vaneckova, Tomas Uher, Jeroen Van Schependom, Stijn Denissen, Tran Quoc Long, Nguyen Linh Trung, Guy Nagels
Neurological diseases can cause significant cognitive impairment, yet linking brain structure to cognitive function remains challenging due to the limitations of conventional methods and the black-box nature of deep learning. We propose InfoVAE-Med3D, an adaptation of InfoVAE for three-dimensional brain MRI that learns informative latent representations by relaxing the mutual information regularization penalty between inputs and latent representations. We evaluated the method on a healthy control (HC) dataset (\(n=6{,}503\)) with age and sex information and a multiple sclerosis (MS) dataset (\(n=916\)) with age, sex, and Symbol Digit Modalities Test (SDMT) scores. Two-dimensional visualizations revealed sex-related clustering and smooth transitions in grey and white matter volumes. Removing the mutual information regularization penalty generally improved MRI reconstruction and brain age and SDMT prediction using support vector regression, outperforming other variational autoencoder variants and achieving competitive performance with deep learning predictors, particularly on the MS dataset. InfoVAE-Med3D provides informative latent representations for investigating brain-related characteristics and supports latent-space analysis and imaging biomarker discovery for cognitive impairment, particularly in MS.

♻ ☆ Dynamic heart rate variability for classification of arrhythmia burden
Brian Kang, Michael Wacker, Steven S. Kang
Purpose: Heart rate variability (HRV) may provide information beyond heart rate (HR) for identifying arrhythmia burden, but the value of temporal HRV variation remains unclear. This study developed a dynamic HRV measure quantifying the interquartile range of window-level root mean square of successive RR-interval differences (IQRRMSSD) and evaluated its utility in classifying total abnormal-beat and premature ventricular contraction (PVC) burden compared with the conventional RR-based HRV measure, RMSSD. Methods: Manually annotated 30-minute ECG recordings from 43 individuals were analyzed using overlapping windows. A linear support vector classifier was used to classify individuals as having high or low arrhythmia burden, defined using 1% and 10% burden thresholds. Four feature sets were evaluated: HR alone, HR with RMSSD, HR with IQRRMSSD, and all three measures. Performance was assessed using repeated stratified five-fold cross-validation. Results: HRV-containing feature sets generally outperformed HR alone, depending on outcome and threshold. At the 1% threshold, IQRRMSSD showed particularly high sensitivity for total abnormal-beat and PVC burden ($0.970 \pm 0.064$ and $0.882 \pm 0.193$, respectively). At the 10% threshold, conventional RMSSD showed the strongest overall discrimination, achieving a ROC-AUC of 0.947 for PVC burden. Combined features sometimes improved the sensitivity-specificity balance but not overall discrimination. Conclusion: These findings suggest that a conventional RR-based HRV measure is informative for elevated ectopic burden, while a dynamic HRV measure may provide complementary information for lower-burden abnormalities.
♻ ☆ Video-rate quantitative phase and polarimetric imaging (QP2I) for label-free imaging of contracting muscle cells
Lucas Kreiss Roohian, Nachiket Pathak, David Ripp, Mitchell Harling, Nils M. Kronenberg, Marcel Schubert, Malte C. Gather
Quantitative phase imaging (QPI) has become a standard approach for label-free microscopy of transparent specimens, mapping the optical path length. However, the isotropic phase is blind to the molecular order underlying optical anisotropy. Implementations that also add quantitative contrast to birefringence rely on multi-frame illumination or polarization-state scanning, or on dedicated interferometric hardware and thus trade away acquisition speed or the simplicity of a standard microscope. We present Quantitative Phase and Polarimetric Imaging (QPPI), a computational imaging framework that converts a standard differential interference contrast (DIC) microscope with a polarization camera into a single-shot, video-rate, dual-contrast imaging system. We developed a new globally regularized phase reconstruction that eliminates artifacts of prior DIC-to-QPI approaches, providing quantitative phase information, and used per-pixel Stokes-based reconstruction to obtain quantitative anisotropic retardance. We validate the approach across simulations, static reference targets, fixed muscle tissue and living dynamically moving cells. QPPI enables all-optical and label-free studies of contractile dynamics in live cardiomyocytes. It provides both structural and functional readouts at sufficient frame rates to resolve fast cardiomyocyte contractions. Conventional fluorescent labels bleach over time, perturb physiology, and preclude long-term monitoring. Instead, QPPI enables long-term, label-free interrogation of dynamic cellular processes at quantitative contrast and provides structural readouts similar to those provided by established fluorescent labels, such as frame-by-frame sarcomere length. It also offers previously inaccessible measurements, like quantitative analysis of retardance changes during muscle contraction, which provides a direct optical signature of muscle photoelasticity.
♻ ☆ Multiscale MIGHT: Modeling Local and Systemic Particle Transport in the Cardiovascular System
Theofilos Symeonidis, Timo Jakumeit, Fardad Vakilipoor, Robert Schober, Maximilian Schäfer
Analytical models of signaling molecule (SM) transport in the cardiovascular system (CVS) force a choice between whole-body scope and vessel-level resolution: Pharmacokinetic models for systemic transport lump each organ and lack small-scale structure, whereas synthetic molecular communication (MC) channel models resolve branched vessel networks (VNs) only for a single pass or capture recirculation only in topologically simple loops, and only partially include SM degradation. To close this gap, we propose Multiscale MIGHT, an extension of the Mixture of Inverse Gaussians for Hemodynamic Transport (MIGHT) model that jointly captures branched topologies, closed-loop circulation, organ clearance, and chemical degradation of SMs. First, we connect the ends of a branched VN to form a closed loop with local and global SM degradation, model the closed-loop channel impulse response for arbitrary transmitter (Tx) and receiver (Rx) positions as a Laplace-domain feedback system, and approximate it in closed form by an inverse Gaussian mixture over multi-pass trajectories. The model is validated against finite-element simulations in COMSOL. Second, we set up a whole-body model, where an exemplary local VN, resolved at vessel-level detail, is embedded into the remaining systemic circulation, represented by lumped organ blocks. We simulate whole-body SM transport for various Tx sites and SM species. Our results show that, at an Rx in a local VN, only the initial pass produces a distinct peak in the SM concentration, significantly shaped by the local vasculature in case of small Tx-Rx distances; subsequent passes arrive strongly dispersed and are shaped almost entirely by organ clearance and chemical degradation rather than by the local VN topology.

♻ ☆ AI-guided deep visual proteomics reveals recurrence-associated morpho-molecular signatures within triple-negative breast cancers
Yesung Cho, Ji Hwan Park, Chan Il Kim, Hyewon Kim, Honglan Li, Yumin Lee, Geongyu Lee, Sujeong Hong, Seong Min Park, Yoonyoung Lee, Hee Sool Rho, Sumin Lee, Amos Chungwon Lee, Changhwan Lee, Hwanyoung Shim, Hyunwook Kim, Hyeji Shin, Sanha Park, Jihoon Yu, Yoon Hee Shin, Sooheon Kim, Hyunjin Park, Seung Min Park, Sangwan Kim, Yujung Kim, Jinwon Jeong, Sung-Im Do, Eun-Young Kim, Dongmyung Shin, Ingu Do, Jongbae Park
Outcome informed AI can localize prognostically relevant regions in digital pathology, but directly characterizing the molecular basis of these spatial predictions remains challenging. Here, we developed an outcome informed, AI-guided deep visual proteomics framework that uses recurrence-risk heatmaps to guide physical tissue sampling and mass spectrometry-based spatial proteomics in triple-negative breast cancer (TNBC). In 156 patients, high- and low-risk regions coexisted within the tumor compartment and showed distinct nuclear and architectural features, indicating intratumoral variation beyond broad tissue identity. Guided by these maps, we physically isolated and profiled 46 AI-defined high- and low-risk tumor regions from two recurrent cases. High-risk regions were enriched for mitotic programs, whereas low-risk regions showed immune- and antigen-presentation-associated programs. We used proteins that consistently differed between high- and low-risk regions to construct a 13-protein composite. The composite showed a significant effect to discriminate highly recurrent patients from less recurrent patients in an expanded TNBC cohort, while the corresponding transcript-based score showed a similar trend in METABRIC. Integrating the protein composite with the H&E-derived risk score improved out-of-bag discrimination, increasing the C-index from 0.679 to 0.739 and the 5-year time-dependent AUC from 0.675 to 0.770. These findings establish outcome-informed H&E risk maps as spatial guides for molecular discovery, extending prognostic AI from prediction toward direct biological interrogation of recurrence-associated tissue states.

♻ ☆ scTREAT: Latent Diffusion for Single-Cell Drug-Response Prediction
Zhaokang Liang, Shuyang Zhuang, Xiaoran Jiao, Weian Mao, Hao Chen
Generating drug-induced transcriptional responses requires a model to account for cellular background together with molecular identity and dose. We present scTREAT, the first diffusion framework to factorize state and drug guidance for single-cell drug-response prediction. A shared latent expression space connects baseline and response, while Guided Decomposable Attention (GD-Attn) injects a baseline representation and a dose-conditioned molecular embedding. Independent channel dropout exposes four conditioning states during training; inference combines three conditional predictions with separately specified state and drug coefficients, and dose modulates drug-guidance strength. On condition-level pseudobulk profiles derived from the Tahoe-100M single-cell atlas, scTREAT achieves the best value on 10 of 12 metrics for unseen cell line-drug combinations (UC) and 11 of 12 for unseen drugs (UD). Ablations examine condition injection, molecular representation, and guidance design. On UD, the relative gains over the second-best model reach 36.11% and 34.21% for DEG logFC-Spearman and logFC-Pearson. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of combining shared latent expression modeling with explicit state and drug conditioning for response prediction.

♻ ☆ Reading Between the Reads: A Decade On, Revisiting Sequencing Protocol Annotation in the Sequence Read Archive
Jamie J. Alnasir
Background: Experimental metadata are essential for interpreting archived sequencing data. In 2015, Alnasir and Shanahan examined the annotation of fragmentation, adapter ligation and enrichment in the Sequence Read Archive (SRA). This study revisits those measurements using the original SQL keyword definitions on a September 2026 NCBI metadata snapshot reconstructed in the historical SRAdb schema. Results: The contemporary database contained 41,593,302 experiments, compared with 419,620 in 2013. In library_construction_protocol, the proportion matching all three historical keyword groups declined from 4.06% to 1.42%. The protocol field was NULL in 67.65% of contemporary experiments. Searching design_description alongside the protocol field increased combined three-step keyword detection to 1,197,367 experiments (2.88%), with most additional matches occurring where the protocol field was NULL. Coverage varied across sequencing library strategies, ranging from 0.26% for AMPLICON to 7.69% for RNA-Seq. Independent comparison of 150 sampled experiment records against source XML confirmed agreement in all historical keyword classifications. Conclusions: The original criteria identify a smaller proportion of experiments in the designated protocol field than a decade earlier. Additional preparation-related terminology is detectable in general experiment descriptions, exposing the limitations of searches restricted to one metadata field. Differences among sequencing strategies complicate historical comparisons as preparation methods evolve. The findings support more consistent protocol annotation and the need to distinguish keyword occurrence from the adequacy of experimental documentation.

☆ Escaping cells dilute CRISPR screen responses unequally across cell types
Xuanrui Huang
Single-cell CRISPR screens run in many cell types are used to compare how cell types respond to perturbation, to train models that transfer effects between them and to score such models. These uses assume that cells carrying a guide RNA had their target knocked down. Yet cells grow for days before sequencing: when an essential gene is knocked down, knocked-down cells are depleted or stop dividing while cells that escaped knockdown keep growing, and the measured response, an average over all cells carrying the guide, is diluted. We define the escape fraction of a perturbation as the share of its measured cells whose target was not effectively knocked down, and estimate it from each cell's counts of the targeted gene with a two-group mixture that recovers known fractions in simulated mixtures. In most cell types the escape fraction rises with how essential the gene is, it is higher in a later screen of the same cells, and it reaches about half of the measured cells in human embryonic stem cells (hESCs). It predicts how much each response is diluted: the response size falls to (1 - e)^2 of that of the knocked-down cells. Because the escape fraction differs between cell types, it changes all three uses: hESCs respond like K562 cells unless knockdown depletes them, the coefficient a simple transfer model learns is lowered by the dilution of the cell type it predicts, and a benchmark scored on all cells rewards predictions shrunk towards no change, partly because that ground truth is itself diluted. Where cells stop dividing instead, the response also contains expression differences between cell-cycle stages. Response sizes should be compared on knocked-down cells or corrected for the escape fraction.

☆ Multivariate Augmented Prediction-Powered Survival Inference for EHR Association Studies With Limited Chart Review
Xicheng Xie, Katherine Fiona Simpson, Hyojung Jang, Theresa L. Walunas, Lili Zhao
Objective: To reduce bias and improve precision in survival association studies using electronic health record (EHR) data when computational phenotypes (CPs) for outcomes and covariates are error-prone and chart review is limited. Materials and Methods: We developed Multivariate Augmented Prediction-Powered Survival (MAPS) regression, which combines chart-reviewed and CP-derived labels across different validation patterns. MAPS uses a discrete-time survival model with a complementary log-log link to estimate hazard ratios (HRs). It incorporates data-adaptive augmentation weights, inverse-probability sampling weights, and patient-level cluster-robust variance estimation. We compared MAPS with chart-reviewed data (CRD) and CP-only analyses in simulation studies and a masked-validation study of immune-related adverse events (irAEs) among 1,026 patients receiving immune checkpoint inhibitors. Results: In the main simulations, MAPS produced approximately unbiased estimates and generally near-nominal 95% confidence interval (CI) coverage, with narrower intervals than CRD. CP-only estimates became increasingly biased as CP errors increased. In the EHR analysis, MAPS estimates closely agreed with fully validated estimates and improved precision over CRD, particularly at lower validation rates. CP-only analysis suggested increased irAE risk associated with pre-existing autoimmune disease (AID; HR=1.53; 95% CI, 1.12-2.09), whereas the fully validated analysis did not (HR=0.93; 95% CI, 0.67-1.29). Discussion: Precision gains depended on CP quality and validation rates, while small validation samples may affect confidence interval coverage. Conclusion: MAPS provides a flexible and efficient approach to EHR-based survival inference by combining limited chart-reviewed data with error-prone CPs across different validation patterns.

☆ Structure, Not Sequence: Tracking and Validating Folding Mechanisms in ESMFold's Trunk with Sparse Autoencoders
Bohan Liu, Xiaohan Fan, Wenqian Ye, Aidong Zhang
Structure predictors like ESMFold fold proteins using neural network computation and have transformed structural biology. However, the driving mechanism from amino-acid (AA) sequence to 3D structure is insufficiently understood, and biological meaning remains locked in the dense network. Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) have shown promising results in obtaining interpretable concept features over an overcomplete basis of intermediate protein sequence-model representations. Yet folding models, which predict physical shape from AA sequence semantics, have no such account. We hypothesize that the features carrying the folding computation evolve in the 3D structural regression process. In this paper, we interpret the folding trunk in three steps. First, a pilot analysis on the representation distribution across modules and recycles locates distinct spatial computation in the trunk. Second, the dictionaries expose a division of labor: the single representation carries inherited semantics, and the pair representation computes the pairwise geometry. Third, we interpret the dictionary at scale with a reasoning language model, trace how the concepts compose across blocks by gradient-based attribution, and follow the interpreted code to the model's folding output, verifying where the computation aligns with, or departs from, known biochemistry. Together, the sparse dictionaries we train capture the relational geometry the trunk computes beyond the sequence model, and their concept activations forecast the model's own fold quality, turning the black-box folding trunk into a sparse, interpretable, and scientifically verifiable circuit.
♻ ☆ Video-rate quantitative phase and polarimetric imaging (QP2I) for label-free imaging of contracting muscle cells
Lucas Kreiss Roohian, Nachiket Pathak, David Ripp, Mitchell Harling, Nils M. Kronenberg, Marcel Schubert, Malte C. Gather
Quantitative phase imaging (QPI) has become a standard approach for label-free microscopy of transparent specimens, mapping the optical path length. However, the isotropic phase is blind to the molecular order underlying optical anisotropy. Implementations that also add quantitative contrast to birefringence rely on multi-frame illumination or polarization-state scanning, or on dedicated interferometric hardware and thus trade away acquisition speed or the simplicity of a standard microscope. We present Quantitative Phase and Polarimetric Imaging (QPPI), a computational imaging framework that converts a standard differential interference contrast (DIC) microscope with a polarization camera into a single-shot, video-rate, dual-contrast imaging system. We developed a new globally regularized phase reconstruction that eliminates artifacts of prior DIC-to-QPI approaches, providing quantitative phase information, and used per-pixel Stokes-based reconstruction to obtain quantitative anisotropic retardance. We validate the approach across simulations, static reference targets, fixed muscle tissue and living dynamically moving cells. QPPI enables all-optical and label-free studies of contractile dynamics in live cardiomyocytes. It provides both structural and functional readouts at sufficient frame rates to resolve fast cardiomyocyte contractions. Conventional fluorescent labels bleach over time, perturb physiology, and preclude long-term monitoring. Instead, QPPI enables long-term, label-free interrogation of dynamic cellular processes at quantitative contrast and provides structural readouts similar to those provided by established fluorescent labels, such as frame-by-frame sarcomere length. It also offers previously inaccessible measurements, like quantitative analysis of retardance changes during muscle contraction, which provides a direct optical signature of muscle photoelasticity.

♻ ☆ Shared clones make random cell halves look reproducible in pooled screens
Xuanrui Huang
Single-cell perturbation screens are used to train and evaluate models of perturbation effects, and model scores are judged against the reproducibility of the measured effects. Reproducibility is usually judged by splitting each perturbation's cells into two random halves, assumed to be independent replicates. In pooled screens such as Perturb-seq, the cells carrying a guide RNA descend from a limited number of transduced cells, so two random halves contain the same clones: pseudoreplication whose unit is not marked in the data. Using null perturbations (no expected effect) as controls, we compared random halves with independent replicates from separate transductions, and tested directly with halves that differ only in their clones. In a genome-scale screen of primary T cells, null perturbations agreed between random halves almost always and between independent replicates only half the time, the rate expected from noise alone; more cells did not remove it. With halves that share no clone, split by the T-cell receptor constant region or by a clone barcode, the excess agreement largely disappeared. Under a simple model, consistent with a screen that recorded clones, its size equals the share of expression differences set by clones divided by the effective number of clones per perturbation; in cell lines an upper bound on it was small. In the T-cell screen, shared clones inflated benchmark ceilings and reliability labels, the more so in donors with fewer effective clones, and when a model's input and scoring answer come from one transduction, published models that predict effects in a new cell state scored well even on perturbations with no expected effect; for the model tested (STATE), the gain vanished with the same guide from another transduction as answer. Reproducibility is better judged across separate transductions than between random halves.

♻ ☆ MedEvo: Medical Reasoning Evolution through Knowledge Navigation
Xiaoliang Wang, Zhuo Chen, Chuanyang Zheng, Quanbin Wang, Jiangwei Lao, Yingao Liu, Jiajia Liu, Jinming Cao, Honglin Lin, Zhihong Lu, Hao Jin, Jian Wang, Jinjie Gu, Lijun Wu
Medical multimodal reasoning models are typically trained on teacher-generated rationales, discarding the valid reasoning in students' own failed trajectories, while general-domain correction methods offer little guidance for errors in image reading, anatomical context, or differential diagnosis. We propose MedEvo, which uses medical knowledge, namely a tag taxonomy and error-type diagnosis, to navigate both trajectory repair and data selection. With supervised fine-tuning (SFT) alone, MedEvo raises Qwen3.5-9B mean accuracy across seven benchmarks from 65.57% to 69.12% (47.40% to 54.50% on the multimodal track of MedXpertQA (MedXpertQA-MM)), surpassing teacher-distillation SFT (66.93%). Our analyses show that failed trajectories contain reasoning worth preserving, that repair success does not imply learning value, and that independent feedback is key to sustained improvement.

♻ ☆ Coder as Editor: Code-driven Interpretable Molecular Editing

♻ ☆ FRIGID: Scaling Diffusion-Based Molecular Generation from Mass Spectra at Training and Inference Time

☆ MechGate: combining mechanism-derived measures of permeation and efflux to screen small molecule drugs targeting Escherichia coli
Lang Qin, Jagath C Rajapakse
Most antibiotic molecules must cross the cell membrane and escape efflux pumps before hitting their targets. For de novo antibiotic design, very few of the in-silico-generated drug molecules can successfully pass these two steps. Existing evaluations of generated molecules do not separate these steps. Therefore, we introduce MechGate, a mechanism-resolved screening tool evaluating small drug molecules against E. coli, measuring permeation, efflux and antibacterial activity. After SMILES input, five measures are reported side by side: two for permeation via porins, one for permeation via the phospholipid membrane, one for efflux and one for activity. Each measure is read on its own, so a molecule that fails can be traced to the step that stops it. All features are taken from papers that state their mechanism rather than selected by importance ranking. On an external set of marketed antibacterials the measures give a precision of 0.789 to 0.873 against baselines near 0.44, and combining them the way the mechanism predicts raises precision to 0.92. We also find that the four public source libraries are separable from one another using physicochemical features alone; every measure here therefore draws both classes from a single library. All code is open-source and requires no commercial software.

☆ ProtAgent: Post-Training Compact Protein Tool Agents with Interaction-Level Credit Assignment
Yuxiang Wang, Baojian Zhou, Qifang Zhao, Keyue Jiang, Yanghua Xiao, Xiaoxiao Xu
Protein annotation agents must choose suitable bioinformatics tools, interpret their outputs, and use the resulting evidence in the final answer. Most existing systems rely on frontier-scale models. We ask whether a compact model can instead learn this behavior through post-training with real tool interaction. We present ProtAgent, a two-stage pipeline that uses supervised fine-tuning (SFT) to learn the tool interface and standard on-policy REINFORCE to improve the interaction policy. Intermediate tool calls receive action rewards, and the final response receives a terminal answer reward. Step-level returns assign these rewards to the responses that produced them and to preceding actions. The episode-level baseline instead assigns one episode return to every action. On a fixed closed-loop protocol with real bioinformatics tools, ProtAgent-8B achieves 61.00% success under the annotation-coverage metric, compared with 41.60% for DeepSeek-V3.2-Thinking 671B and 60.80% for the strongest evaluated frontier system. In the three-seed comparison, mean success is 42.70% with episode-level returns and 61.40% with step-level returns under identity return normalization; the corresponding group-relative results are 36.50% and 58.20%. The metric measures recovery of reference annotations under this protocol; it does not by itself establish biological correctness or complete evidence grounding.

☆ Multiscale Topology-Regulated Nonlinear Dynamics for Protein B-Factor Prediction and Single-Cell Type Classification
Zihan Wang, Mushal Zia, Xiaoqi Wei, Li Shen, Jian Jiang
Protein B-factor prediction and single-cell type classification are important for characterizing structural variability and cellular heterogeneity, with applications in biological function analysis and drug discovery. However, static geometric descriptors do not directly capture how multiscale connectivity shapes responses to local perturbations. Here, we introduce multiscale graph-coupled Burgers response learning (MGCBRL), a framework integrating algebraic topology and nonlinear dynamics to construct predictive biological representations. Specifically, a filtration converts protein coordinates or cellular principal component scores into weighted graphs at ten neighborhood scales. Independent unit impulses initiate node-specific responses governed by nonlinear Burgers dynamics. Statistical summaries or sampled source-node trajectories then encode the influence of structural neighborhoods on transient signal evolution. The simulated responses serve as computational probes rather than models of biological motion. For cross-protein prediction, multiscale response statistics are combined with spectral perturbation descriptors measuring changes in Laplacian spectra after node removal. For single-cell classification, principal component scores augment these features to retain explicit expression-space information, and the resulting descriptors support downstream supervised learning. Across 104 proteins and 13 single-cell datasets, MGCBRL achieved configuration-selected mean scores of 0.888 for within-protein Pearson correlation and 0.950 for transductive cell classification balanced accuracy, with leave-one-protein-out mean correlations of 0.496-0.558. Our framework provides a shared strategy for transforming multiscale biological connectivity into informative response descriptors, supporting predictive analysis of protein structures and transcriptomic profiles.
♻ ☆ Denoising Diffusion Variational Inference: Diffusion Models as Expressive Variational Posteriors
Yingheng Wang, Wasu Top Piriyakulkij, Volodymyr Kuleshov
We propose denoising diffusion variational inference (DDVI), a black-box variational inference algorithm for latent variable models which relies on diffusion models as flexible approximate posteriors. Specifically, our method introduces an expressive class of diffusion-based variational posteriors that perform iterative refinement in latent space; we train these posteriors with a novel regularized evidence lower bound (ELBO) on the marginal likelihood inspired by the wake-sleep algorithm. Our method is easy to implement (it fits a regularized extension of the ELBO), is compatible with black-box variational inference, and generally outperforms alternative classes of approximate posteriors based on normalizing flows or adversarial networks. We find that DDVI improves inference and learning in deep latent variable models across common benchmarks as well as on a motivating task in biology---inferring latent ancestry from human genomes---where it outperforms strong baselines on the 1000 Genomes dataset.

☆ Computational De Novo Design of Miniprotein Candidate Neutralizers Targeting the IL-23 Cytokine Subunit p19
Raghav Paladugu, Fardina Fathmiul Alam

De novo miniproteins are an attractive alternative, yet published designs target the IL-23 receptor; the IL-23 cytokine itself has not been addressed. ☆ What Transfers Across Protein Pockets? Experience Reuse in Molecular Optimization for Agentic Structure-Based Drug Design
Zaifei Yang, Weiyu Chen, Yaqing Wang, James Kwok
LLM agents optimize a ligand in a protein pocket by editing it step by step and docking each edit. Recent agents first spend extra dockings probing the complex with controlled edits; guidance induced from these probes is reported to help more than the chemical knowledge the model already carries. The probes are then discarded with the instance that produced them, so the obvious next step is to keep them in an experience base, letting a new pocket inherit what earlier ones already paid for. Such a base is read through a \emph{key}: the record properties under which past edits are pooled and a new one is looked up. Using the full log of one such run over the CrossDocked2020 test-set pockets, we ask whether its keys recur on a pocket the base has never seen and still separate improving from worsening edits. \crchange{Using} the contacts at the site being edited, the action, and the round as \crchange{a} key, the base separates them sharply. Within our analysis, this separation is largely explained by how much the edit changed the molecule's size, the action, and the round, none of which describes the target. That is not because the target is irrelevant: naming it outright separates them several times more sharply. For a pocket absent from the base, transfer must rely on a description rather than its identity. Among the exact-match descriptions we evaluate, the more specific ones rarely recur, whereas the coarser ones provide little separation; the signals that do transfer primarily describe the edit and the molecule it produced rather than the target. Under the representations and lookup scheme studied here, an experience base may be more useful for prioritizing an agent's own proposals for docking than for directly prescribing what to build.
☆ Agent-guided epitope excision enables economical de novo binder design
Jun W. Kim, Artiom Myaskovsky, Oscar Donosa, Lawrence Chan, Jamie Rogers, Avinatan Hassidim, Yossi Matias, Annalisa Pawlosky
De novo protein design campaigns remain bottlenecked by the inference compute time required to generate and rank candidates, limiting wider functional exploration. Here, we introduce and evaluate an epitope excision approach that truncates targets to local interface windows to accelerate both backbone diffusion and structure prediction. Across 15 targets and 3,666 designs, excised windows closely reproduced full-target binding interfaces (median CαRMSD of 0.37 Å) and made it practical to design binders against the 719-residue extracellular domain of fibroblast activation protein alpha (FAP) by cutting RFdiffusion and AlphaFold3 GPU time by ~ 88% in one of the epitopes. Pairing this workflow with an agentic optimization loop allowed the system to autonomously identify catalytic-site targeting strategies. Under a fixed compute budget, a human-in-the-loop (HITL) workflow raised the computational hit rate from 5.06% to 9.33% while further cutting GPU hours by ~ 42%. We also found that omitting target multiple sequence alignments during refolding roughly substantially increased the apparent yield. Wet-lab validation with yeast display identified nanomolar binders from the generated designs with the HITL and the fully autonomous agent (human-out-of-the-loop, HOOTL) arms recovering more enriched designs than the PI-alone baseline. Together, target excision and agent-guided search offer a practical way to scale de novo binder design to large, therapeutically relevant proteins.

♻ ☆ Probe Before You Edit: Probing-Guided Molecular Optimization for LLM Agents in Structure-Based Drug Design NeurIPS 2026
Zaifei Yang, Weiyu Chen, Yaqing Wang, James Kwok
Structure-based drug design increasingly employs LLM agents to iteratively refine ligands against a target pocket, yet a viable ligand must satisfy two often-conflicting objectives---binding affinity and druggability---which single optimization steps rarely improve together. To quantify this difficulty, we introduce two diagnostic metrics: the first measures how often a single edit improves both objectives, and the second measures how often a gain on one objective comes with a loss on the other. Applying these diagnostics to current LLM-agent pipelines exposes a consistent failure mode: the agent performs molecular editing without knowing how the pocket-ligand complex responds to local modifications, thus rarely achieving joint improvement. Inspired by medicinal chemists, who probe the pocket-ligand complex with controlled analog edits before choosing an optimization direction, we propose \textbf{PROBE}, an optimization framework built around edit--response probing. PROBE first decomposes the ligand into editable sites and builds a pocket-specific \textbf{site map} that flags where joint gains are plausible, where the two objectives are likely in tension, and where liability substructures should be changed; it then performs controlled probe edits whose responses are distilled into an \textbf{EditManual}. Guided by the site map and EditManual, PROBE runs an iterative multi-agent optimization loop. On the CrossDocked2020 benchmark, PROBE achieves state-of-the-art performance and substantially mitigates the failure modes exposed by our diagnostics metrics. Codes are in https://github.com/yzf-code/PROBE/tree/main
comment: Accepted at NeurIPS 2026. Revised manuscript
QM 4
☆ Shared clones make random cell halves look reproducible in pooled screens
Xuanrui Huang
Single-cell perturbation screens are used to train and evaluate models that predict perturbation effects, and model scores are judged against how reproducible the measured effects are. Reproducibility is usually judged by splitting each perturbation's cells into two random halves. This assumes that the halves are independent replicates. In pooled screens such as Perturb-seq, however, the cells carrying a guide RNA descend from a limited number of transduced cells, so two random halves contain the same clones: pseudoreplication whose unit is not marked in the data. Using perturbations that should have no effect as controls, we compared random halves with independent replicates from separate transductions, and tested directly with halves that differ only in their clones. In a genome-scale screen of primary T cells, null perturbations agreed between random halves almost always and between independent replicates only half the time, the rate expected from noise alone; measuring more cells did not remove this agreement. Splitting the cells into halves that share no clone, by the T-cell receptor constant region or by a clone barcode, largely removed the excess agreement. Its size equals the share of expression differences set by clones divided by the effective number of clones per perturbation; across 16 screens it was largest in T cells transferred into mice and in a genome-scale primary T-cell screen, one to two orders of magnitude smaller in cell lines, and smallest in non-dividing neurons. Shared clones inflate benchmark ceilings and reliability labels, and when a model's input and its scoring answer come from one transduction, published models that predict perturbation effects in a new cell state appear better than a baseline that ignores the perturbation; against cells from another transduction, most fall below it. Reproducibility should be judged across separate transductions.

☆ Video-rate quantitative phase and polarimetric imaging (QP2I) for label-free imaging of contracting muscle cells
Lucas Kreiss Roohian, Nachiket Pathak, David Ripp, Mitchell Harling, Nils Kronenberg, Marcel Schubert, Malte C. Gather
Quantitative phase imaging (QPI) has become a standard approach for label-free microscopy of transparent specimens, mapping the optical path length. However, the isotropic phase is blind to the molecular order underlying optical anisotropy. Implementations that also add quantitative contrast to birefringence, rely on multi-frame illumination or polarization-state scanning, or on dedicated interferometric hardware, that trade away acquisition speed or the simplicity of a standard microscope. We present Quantitative Phase and Polarimetric Imaging (QP2I), a computational imaging framework that converts a standard differential interference contrast (DIC) microscope with a polarization camera into a single-shot, video-rate, dual-contrast imaging system. We developed a new globally regularized phase reconstruction that eliminates artifacts of prior DIC-to-QPI approaches, providing quantitative phase information and used per-pixel Stokes-based reconstruction to obtain quantitative anisotropic retardance. We validate the approach across simulations, static reference targets, fixed muscle tissue and living dynamically moving cells. Thereby, QP2I enables to study contractile dynamics in live cardiomyocytes all-optically and label-free. It provides both structural and functional readouts at frame rates that are sufficient to temporally resolve fast cardiomyocyte contractions. While conventional fluorescent labels bleach out over time, perturb physiology, and preclude long-term monitoring, QP2I provides similar established structural readouts, such as frame-by-frame sarcomere length, as well as previously inaccessible measurements, like quantitative analysis of retardance changes during muscle contraction, as a direct optical signature of muscle photoelasticity. QP2I thus enables long-term, label-free interrogation of dynamic cellular processes at quantitative contrast.
☆ Automated screening of Parkinson's Disease using Smooth Pursuit Eye Movements
J. Camilo Puerta-Acevedo, Maria F. Alcala-Durand, Julián D. Arias-Londoño, Elisa Luque-Buzo, Francisco Grandas-Pérez, Juan I. Godino-Llorente
Smooth pursuit eye movements are frequently altered in Parkinson's disease, but previous studies have relied mainly on conventional parameters that describe the relationship between gaze and target. This study investigates whether complexity and nonlinear dynamics measures of the gaze position sequences can support the automatic screening of Parkinson's disease. Gaze position sequences were recorded with a video eye tracker in 41 participants with early Parkinson's disease and 47 healthy controls during 12 smooth pursuit tasks. Four experimental frameworks and four embedding delay strategies were compared. The selected experimental setting combined baseline wander removal with a fixed embedding delay. After feature selection, a support vector machine was trained for each task and axis and their outputs were combined in a mixture of experts. The pipeline was developed in 70 participants and evaluated on an external test set of 18 participants. On the external test set, the pipeline obtained an area under the receiver operating characteristic curve of 0.86. The most informative features described less persistent and more irregular gaze position sequences in Parkinson's disease, mainly along the horizontal axis. These results support complexity and nonlinear dynamics measures of smooth pursuit as candidate markers for Parkinson's disease screening, pending validation in larger independent cohorts.

♻ ☆ AI-guided deep visual proteomics reveals recurrence-associated morpho-molecular signatures within triple-negative breast cancers
Yesung Cho, Ji Hwan Park, Chan Il Kim, Hyewon Kim, Honglan Li, Yumin Lee, Geongyu Lee, Sujeong Hong, Seong Min Park, Yoonyoung Lee, Hee Sool Rho, Sumin Lee, Amos Chungwon Lee, Changhwan Lee, Hwanyoung Shim, Hyunwook Kim, Hyeji Shin, Sanha Park, Jihoon Yu, Yoon Hee Shin, Sooheon Kim, Hyunjin Park, Seung Min Park, Sangwan Kim, Yujung Kim, Jinwon Jeong, Sung-Im Do, Eun-Young Kim, Dongmyung Shin, Ingu Do, Jongbae Park

☆ RiboBridge: protein-conditioned de novo RNA binder structure design
Zhou Zhang, Hanqun Cao, Minghao Sun, Odin Zhang, Yang Zhang, Pheng Ann Heng, Wujun Li, Tianfan Fu
Designing RNA molecules that bind to target protein structures requires generating both an RNA conformation and its bound complex geometry, without assuming a predefined binding site. Existing approaches generally model these coupled processes within a unified representation, entangling RNA conformation generation with protein-relative placement. In this work, we introduce RiboBridge, a structure-first framework that separates RNA conformational generation from protein--RNA assembly through cross-frame interaction geometry. RiboBridge first proposes multiple candidate RNA-binding interfaces on the protein surface, generates canonical RNA conformations within a pretrained RNA structural latent space (RiboSphere), and then predicts protein--RNA interaction constraints to bridge the independently defined RNA and protein coordinate frames. The resulting interaction geometry is converted into a geometrically consistent protein-relative pose through rigid placement. We evaluate RiboBridge on a temporally split, sequence-filtered, and redundancy-controlled benchmark of 124 protein--RNA complexes. Under post-hoc interface-correspondence evaluation, where native information is used only after generation to select among already generated predicted-pocket candidates, RiboBridge achieves the highest fNAT and DockQ and the lowest interface and ligand RMSDs among compared methods. Component ablations and stage-wise diagnostics further characterize the contributions of RNA conformational generation, interaction prediction, and rigid placement, identifying the main sources of complex-recovery error.
☆ Supercoiling Drives DNA-Protein Co-Condensation
Sihan Liu, Hongbo Zhao, Andrej Košmrlj
The co-condensation of DNA and proteins plays an important role in genome compaction, including heterochromatin formation and chromosome segregation. Meanwhile, transcription and replication continuously generate DNA supercoiling, yet how the resulting torsional stress affects co-condensation remains poorly understood. Here, by coupling DNA elasticity to a protein concentration field, we show that supercoiling acts as a topological catalyst for co-condensation through a mutually reinforcing interplay with protein wetting. Specifically, protein wetting facilitates twist-to-writhe conversion, generating highly writhed DNA conformations that in turn promote capillary collapse into a protein-rich droplet. In the absence of supercoiling, DNA remains kinetically trapped in metastable extended states within our model, whereas sufficiently strong supercoiling removes the nucleation barrier and enables spontaneous co-condensation. These results establish topology as a distinct control parameter for biomolecular condensation and provide a mechanical framework for how active genomic processes can dynamically regulate large-scale genome organization.

☆ Behavioral Mapping of Hierarchical Dynamical Models Accelerates Bayesian Parameter Inference
Jingsong Bai, Ayush Pandey
Bayesian parameter inference for complex nonlinear dynamical models is often limited by the cost of repeated model simulation. We present a behavioral mapping framework that uses the inferred posterior of a higher-level model to accelerate inference of a lower-level model without requiring state or parameter correspondence between them. Building on approximate simulation relations for hierarchically related models, we define the notion of behavioral compatibility and quantify the behavioral support of detailed model parameters using the reduced model posterior. We learn a level set of behavioral support using a Gaussian process and use it to filter detailed model parameter proposals before expensive simulations during Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) inference. We derive conditions under which the behavioral support level set retains all detailed model parameters that fit the experimental data within a prescribed tolerance. We apply this method to a hierarchy of gene-expression models fitted to cell-free experimental data. After initial setup costs, our approach speeds up MCMC by 8.02% for the phenomenological model and 15.21% for the mechanistic model.
☆ Kinetic Proofreading Activation Times Beyond the Weibull Limit
Thomas Mourier, Cameron Hopkinson, Kenny Wong
T cells can detect a few foreign peptide-major histocompatibility complexes (pMHCs) among a far larger number of self pMHCs. Kinetic proofreading explains this discrimination: a receptor that has bound its ligand must complete nKP signaling steps, each at rate kp, before it activates, and dissociation at any step resets its progress. When many receptors are engaged at once, the time to the first activation has been approximated by a Weibull law taken from the short-time limit of this process. The Weibull law is convenient, but it does not contain the dissociation rate koff, even though koff is what proofreading uses to tell ligands apart. Using the exact first- passage law of the underlying continuous-time Markov chain together with stochastic simulations, we show that the relative error of the Weibull law is set by a single dimensionless number Λ, the Weibull-predicted activation time measured in units of nKP + 1 bound-state lifetimes. For Λ < 1 the first receptor to activate does so before its ligand has ever dissociated, and the Weibull law holds. As Λ grows, activation relies increasingly on repeated unbinding and rebinding. The true activation time then exceeds the Weibull prediction by up to 2900-fold on our parameter grid, and the effective Weibull shape falls to one, so activation becomes memoryless instead of timer-like. We derive a closed form that keeps koff by treating the successful pass through the proofreading chain separately from the time lost to failed attempts. It matches exact medians to within 2% in 19 of 25 parameter sets, with a worst case of about 6%, and reproduces the loss of timer-like behavior. Because Λ is computed from the Weibull prediction itself, it shows in advance how far that formula can be trusted; where it cannot, the new expression requires only a one-dimensional root find.

♻ ☆ Hodge-Decomposition of Brain Networks
D Vijay Anand, Moo K. Chung
We analyze brain networks by decomposing them into three orthogonal components: gradient, curl, and harmonic flows, through the Hodge decomposition, a technique advantageous for capturing complex topological features. A Wasserstein distance based topological inference is developed to determine the statistical significance of each component. Our results indicate statistically significant differences in the topological features between male and female brain networks.

☆ Unlocking the Full Potential of Taylor Dispersion Analysis Through Advanced Detection
Rami Laoufi, Agnès Hagège
Today, pharmaceutical sciences rely on a wide range of innovative therapeutics of varying sizes: nanoparticles, proteins, antibodies, and nanocarriers for drug delivery. Measuring their size is one of the crucial parameters in their characterization, to ensure drug safety, stability, and effectiveness. Over the past two decades, Taylor Dispersion Analysis has emerged as an important sizing method. Its scope of application is perfectly suited to the sizes of these therapeutics. By design, it can be coupled with a wide range of detectors, and extensive research has been conducted in this field. In this review, we will present an overview of the studies conducted, and discuss how the application drives the choice of one detector or another (size measurement, aggregation studies, interaction studies, and other analyses, performed in both simple biological buffers and in more complex media, particularly serum) and makes Taylor Dispersion Analysis a highly versatile tool.
☆ QUBO-Based Conformer Selection with Quantum-Classical Hybrid Optimization for Multiconformer Ligand Modeling
Jiyun Lee, Haeseong Kim, Junho Lee, Joonsuk Huh
A ligand can adopt multiple conformations within the same binding site, and these conformations can interact differently with the surrounding protein. Multiconformer modeling must therefore determine which candidate conformations to retain and what occupancies to assign to them. An established automated approach uses mixed-integer quadratic programming (MIQP) with hard occupancy and conformer-count limits for this selection. Here, we reformulated conformer selection as a quadratic unconstrained binary optimization (QUBO) problem with finite penalties and discrete occupancies. We solved the QUBO using a quantum-classical hybrid solver. We compared this workflow with the MIQP-based qFit-ligand workflow across 83 paired X-ray ligand entries using the same experimental inputs and the same qFit-ligand processing protocol. For each entry, both final models were evaluated against the same composite-omit map generated from the common input structure before the two workflows diverged. Quantum-qFit produced higher ligand-region real-space correlation coefficients (RSCC) in 62 entries 74.7% and lower occupancy-weighted torsional strain in 66 entries 79.5%. Similar directional trends remained in the 28 entries with matched final conformer counts. The molecular Electron Density for Individual Atoms score (EDIAm) decreased in most entries. This result shows that higher regional map agreement did not necessarily correspond to stronger atom-level density support. Overall, the results show that the conformer-selection formulation can change which ligand states are represented from the same experimental data and provide a QUBO framework for studying multiconformer ligand selection.

☆ Escaping the Phylogenetic Trap: Persistent Homology Enables Cross-Species Ion Channel Classification via Multimodal Graph Neural Networks
Arezoo Bybordi, Favour Achimba, Mariam Gelashvili, Jessy Ramirez, Weigang Qiu, Mandë Holford, Anita Raja
Protein classifiers are highly sensitive to evaluation protocol: random train/test splits induce phylogenetic leakage, allowing models to exploit taxon-specific correlations and substantially overestimate out-of-distribution performance. This issue is particularly acute for venom compound classification by ion-channel target (Ca2+, K+, Na+), where reported accuracies of 80-90% do not reflect generalization to unseen taxa. We formulate cross-species prediction as a structured distribution shift in which taxonomic identity acts as a confounder, requiring representations that capture structural features invariant across phylogeny. Using a shared multimodal backbone that fuses ESM-2 sequence embeddings with a disulfide-aware graph attention network, we systematically compare five structural inductive biases (spectral, Riemannian, SE(3)-invariant, statistical-manifold, and persistent-homology representations) under a leave-one-taxon-out evaluation protocol on a benchmark of 1,373 ColabFold-predicted venom compounds spanning five taxa. The persistent-homology representation summarizes backbone geometry through topological descriptors that are robust to local structural perturbations, providing an inductive bias toward phylogenetically invariant learning. Under conventional i.i.d. splits, all models achieve similar accuracy (81-86%), masking differences in inductive bias. Under taxonomic shift, this parity collapses: a sequence-only baseline drops to approximately 30%, the remaining geometric priors cluster at 54-55%, while our persistent-homology model (PH-GNN) achieves 63.4%, outperforming the next-best method by over 8 percentage points. These results demonstrate that random-split evaluation obscures meaningful differences in representation quality and identify topological representations as an effective inductive bias for learning under structured biological distribution shift.

☆ AI Mindmeld: A Latent Interconnect Protocol
Siavash Golkar, Jake Kovalic, Shirley Ho
LLMs can act as scientific agents using tools to consult specialists and run experiments. Yet specialist information must enter their context, generally as text, images, audio or video: a bottleneck between models with rich, high-dimensional representations. We advocate direct representation exchange: a mindmeld conveying many properties, including information an LLM would not think to request. It should be extensible to new specialists, flexible across questions and tasks, and compositional across independently connected specialists without joint training. The Latent Interconnect Protocol (LIP) learns connectors mapping specialist representations into LLM internal states. Training only connectors keeps this practical as LLMs grow and evolve. We connect three protein and three small molecule specialists to Gemma 3 (4B and 12B parameters) and Kimi K2.6 (1T total, 32B active), freezing all pretrained models. Independently trained connectors provide useful information without changing the LLM or existing connectors, supporting familiar and new question types about unseen objects. We demonstrate zero-shot transfer to cross-domain tasks without joint training. Task performance and compositionality generally improve with LLM capability, with especially strong gains in zero-shot task transfer. Limitations remain: current LLMs were not trained for specialist representations as native inputs; access does not ensure reliable interpretation, integration or use in reasoning. We call on specialist developers to provide consistent representation interfaces and, especially, on LLM developers to train models to receive, combine and reason with these inputs. These efforts could expand scientific agents' information while preserving conventional tools' flexibility. Data, pretrained connectors, and code for training, evaluation and interactive inference: https://github.com/PolymathicAI/AI-Mindmeld
♻ ☆ Pushing Biomolecular Utility-Diversity Frontiers with Supergroup Relative Policy Optimization
Xinwu Ye, He Cao, Hao Li, Bin Feng, Zijing Liu, Xiangru Tang, Yu Li, Shenghua Gao
Biomolecular generators are often adapted with reward feedback to improve task-specific utility, but pushing utility alone can concentrate generation on a narrow family of candidates. Maintaining diversity is difficult because sample diversity is a set-level property. We introduce Supergroup Relative Policy Optimization (SGRPO), a flexible GRPO-style framework that directly constructs rewards from set-level diversity. For each condition, SGRPO samples a supergroup of candidate sets, compares their diversity under the same condition, and redistributes the group diversity reward to individual rollouts through leave-one-out diversity contributions before combining it with rollout-level utility. This design decouples SGRPO from a particular generator, utility reward, or diversity metric, and allows instantiation with different GRPO-style approaches. We evaluate SGRPO on three main settings: de novo small-molecule design, pocket-based small-molecule design, and de novo protein design, instantiating it with both GRPO and Coupled-GRPO across autoregressive and discrete diffusion generators. Across decoding sweeps, SGRPO expands the utility-diversity Pareto frontier and achieves the best frontier-level metrics relative to baselines. Our analyses further show that direct set-level diversity rewards remain effective with small groups and help preserve broader generation-distribution coverage during post-training. The code is available at https://github.com/IDEA-XL/SGRPO.
♻ ☆ Advances in Machine Learning for Directed Evolution: A Five-Year Retrospective
Bruce J. Wittmann
The last five-plus years have seen many protein engineering disciplines transformed by advances in machine learning (ML), but the same cannot be said for directed evolution. Reflecting on a previously co-authored perspective, I discuss why I believe this to be the case, arguing that a disconnect between the goals of machine-learning-assisted directed evolution (MLDE) researchers--"identify an optimal protein"--and the goals of directed evolution more broadly--"identify a sufficient protein given time and resource constraints"--is a principal culprit. As an example, I highlight how nearly all current MLDE methods neglect to account for the cost of DNA synthesis, resulting in strategies that have limited practical applicability regardless of the underlying models' capabilities. I close by discussing recent works that are exceptions to this overarching trend, and emphasize that the last five years of efforts in ML-assisted protein engineering and the prescribed reframe of MLDE objectives need not be mutually exclusive.

☆ Learning Epidemic Threshold Equations from Simulation: REEBLRA, an Interpretable Bayesian Classification Framework for Reproduction Numbers
Jomar F. Rabajante, Norvin P. Bansilan, Maria Czarina T. Lagura-Arreza, Destiny SM. Lutero, Emerson R. Rico
The basic reproduction number, $R_0$, is central to infectious-disease modelling, yet analytic expressions can be difficult to derive for high-dimensional, nonlinear, or structurally complex models. We introduce REEBLRA ($R_0$ Equation Estimation using Bayesian Logistic Regression Algorithm), an interpretable framework that learns explicit surrogate equations for epidemic thresholds from parameter--response data. Rather than directly regressing $R_0$, REEBLRA estimates $\Pr\{R_0(\boldsymbolθ)>c\}$ for parameter vector $\boldsymbolθ$ and threshold $c$. It provides a binary equation for classifying epidemic invasion ($R_0>1$) and successive threshold equations defining ordered brackets that approximate $R_0$, the expected number of secondary infections caused by a typical case in a fully susceptible population. The resulting equations support parameter screening, sensitivity interpretation, threshold classification, and exploratory analysis when analytic derivation is cumbersome. Bayesian logistic regression using standardized polynomial-like features and proper weakly informative priors yields regularized estimates, posterior uncertainty, and finite coefficients under complete or quasi-complete separation. We establish a mechanistic--surrogate concordance principle linking the full fitted-score gradient to the local gradient of $\log R_0$ under explicit regularity and tangency assumptions, allowing conditional interpretation of threshold directions. Across seven analytic-label benchmarks associated with ODE models, including a 15-compartment five-group SIR system, five-fold binary AUC was 0.999--1.000 and ordered-bracket accuracy was 0.951--0.998. REEBLRA complements rather than replaces mechanistic derivations, bridging simulation, machine learning, and reproduction-number analysis. An accompanying Shiny app supports implementation.

☆ Non-Destructive Detection of Early Drought Stress via Spectral Submanifold Analysis
Sunghwan Jung, Isabella Thiel, Jisoo Yuk, George Haller
Non-destructive early detection of water stress is critical for precision agriculture, yet current detection methods are often labor-intensive or destructive. We propose a rapid, non-invasive method based on Spectral Submanifold (SSM) reduction to analyze the nonlinear vibration dynamics of soybean plants. Unlike traditional linear modal analysis, our data-driven approach reveals a distinct transition in the dominant dynamics of the plant stem as drought progresses. Specifically, we uncover a sign reversal in the nonlinear damping coefficient within SSM-reduced models of full plants. Our finding reveals a fundamental transition in the plant's dissipative mechanics from a pressurized, fluid-rich vessel exhibiting superlinear (viscoelastic) damping to a dry, cellular solid dominated by sublinear (frictional) damping. This transition is closely correlated with the onset of physiological stress indicated by a significant decrease in the values of mature-leaf soil plant analysis development (SPAD) and colorimetric metrics. In contrast, traditional metrics, such as linear frequency and leaf wilting, do not provide consistent statistical signals of water stress. This study provides the first experimental evidence that SSM-based nonlinear model reduction can detect the changing internal state of a living organism, offering a physics-based metric for high-throughput plant phenotyping.

♻ ☆ Can synthetic data reproduce real-world findings in epidemiology? A replication study using adversarial random forests
Jan Kapar, Kathrin Günther, Lori Ann Vallis, Klaus Berger, Nadine Binder, Hermann Brenner, Stefanie Castell, Beate Fischer, Volker Harth, Bernd Holleczek, Timm Intemann, Till Ittermann, André Karch, Thomas Keil, Lilian Krist, Berit Lange, Michael F. Leitzmann, Katharina Nimptsch, Nadia Obi, Iris Pigeot, Tobias Pischon, Tamara Schikowski, Börge Schmidt, Carsten Oliver Schmidt, Anja M. Sedlmair, Justine Tanoey, Harm Wienbergen, Andreas Wienke, Claudia Wigmann, Marvin N. Wright
Synthetic data hold substantial potential to address practical challenges in epidemiology due to restricted data access and privacy concerns. However, many current methods suffer from limited quality, high computational demands, and complexity for non-experts. Furthermore, common evaluation strategies for synthetic data often fail to directly reflect statistical utility and measure privacy risks sufficiently. Against this background, a critical underexplored question is whether synthetic data can reliably reproduce key findings from epidemiological research while preserving privacy. We propose adversarial random forests (ARF) as an efficient and convenient method for synthesizing tabular epidemiological data. To evaluate its performance, we replicated statistical analyses from six epidemiological publications covering blood pressure, anthropometry, myocardial infarction, accelerometry, loneliness, and diabetes, from the German National Cohort (NAKO Gesundheitsstudie), the Bremen STEMI Registry U45 Study, and the Guelph Family Health Study. We further assessed how dataset dimensionality and variable complexity affect the quality of synthetic data, and contextualized ARF's performance by comparison with commonly used tabular data synthesizers in terms of utility, privacy, generalization, and runtime. Across all replicated studies, results on ARF-generated synthetic data consistently aligned with original findings. Even for datasets with relatively low sample size-to-dimensionality ratios, replication outcomes closely matched the original results across descriptive and inferential analyses. Reduced dimensionality and variable complexity further enhanced synthesis quality. ARF demonstrated favourable performance regarding utility, privacy preservation, and generalization relative to other synthesizers and superior computational efficiency.
♻ ☆ Population Neuroscience 3.0: Bridging large-N and small-N paradigms in brain-wide association studies
Zi-Xuan Zhou, Xi-Nian Zuo
Brain-wide association studies (BWAS) leveraging large datasets, have led to divergent and sometimes opposing views on research practices and priorities. The debates span multiple dimensions, including large-N vs.\ small-N sampling, cross-sectional vs.\ longitudinal, observational vs.\ interventional/causal, population generalizability vs.\ individual specificity, data-driven vs.\ hypothesis-driven, prediction-oriented vs.\ explanation-oriented, and molecular/cellular vs.\ systems-level. Taken together, these tensions are not only about methodology, but also about how large-N population studies and small-N, mechanism-focused studies should relate to one another. Shifts along these axes have sharpened disagreements and added to fragmentation in an already heterogeneous field of cognitive neuroscience. Here, we sketch an integrative response grounded in population neuroscience, organized around a closed-loop ``design--analysis--interpretation'' research cycle that aims to build consensus while bridging these divides. Our central claim is that population neuroscience offers a distinctive population-level vantage point for identifying general patterns, characterizing inter-individual variabilities, and benchmarking intra-individual changes, thereby providing reference frameworks through which small-N, mechanism-focused studies can co-evolve with large-N population studies. Population neuroscience is not simply about providing larger N for BWAS; more fundamentally, it is about accumulating cross-scale priors and shared infrastructures for design, analysis, and interpretation across human neuroscience studies. In this sense, we outline a ``third-generation'' agenda for population neuroscience that shifts emphasis from amassing isolated associations to building reference frameworks for cumulative mechanistic and translational research.

♻ ☆ Fixed-Dimensional Latent Flow for Generating Variable-Size 3D Molecules
Weichi Yao, Cameron Gruich, Bryan R. Goldsmith, Yixin Wang
Molecular size is intrinsically linked to composition, structure, and properties, but most 3D molecular generators treat size as a predefined input rather than a quantity to be generated. We introduce Equivariant-Free Transformer-Autoencoded Latent Flow Matching, a two-stage framework that samples a fixed-dimensional latent vector using flow matching and employs an autoregressive Transformer to determine molecular size, atom types, coordinates, and chemical attributes.
